Conversational Search: From Information

Résumé

La recherche conversationnelle s’intéresse à faire une recherche d’information suivant une
séquence d’interactions entre les utilisateurs et un système d’information. Les systèmes de recherche conversationnelle existants reposent principalement sur le dialogue en langage naturel,
qui offre une interface interactive flexible permettant de satisfaire les besoins d’information
complexes des utilisateurs. Contrairement aux systèmes de recherche ad-hoc traditionnels —
où l’entrée est une requête par mots-clés et le résultat retourné consiste en “dix liens bleus”,
un système de recherche conversationnelle exige de comprendre l’évolution de l’intention de
recherche de l’utilisateur au fil des conversations en cours, ainsi que d’effectuer une recherche
d’information et une génération augmentée de recherche (Retrieval-Augmented Generation)
pour fournir une réponse finale à l’utilisateur.
Dans cette thèse, nous nous efforçons d’améliorer les performances du système de recherche
conversationnelle en développant diverses approches traitant différents problèmes sous-jacents.
Nous étudions d’abord la compréhension de l’intention de recherche de l’utilisateur lors
d’interactions multi-tours entre l’humain et le système par la reformulation de requêtes
conversationnelles. Ensuite, nous modélisons le contexte historique pour faciliter la recherche
dense conversationnelle sensible à l’historique (history-aware), dans le but d’améliorer les
résultats de recherche. Une fois l’information récupérée, nous explorons la manière de l’utiliser
pour augmenter la génération de réponses. Nos premières explorations tentent d’exploiter les
informations historiques pour stimuler la qualité de la génération augmentée de recherche en
contexte conversationnel. Ensuite, nous optimisons la procédure de décodage des modèles de
langage génératifs en développant un mécanisme qui intègre la qualité des documents pour
la génération augmentée de recherche. De plus, nous proposons un cadre unifié permettant
d’optimiser à la fois la recherche d’information conversationnelle et la génération augmentée
de recherche conversationnelle de manière de bout en bout (end-to-end).
Toutes les approches proposées sont évaluées sur des benchmarks de recherche conversationnelle et de questions-réponses largement utilisés. Les résultats expérimentaux démontrent
que nos différentes méthodes obtiennent toutes des améliorations substantielles par rapport
aux baselines solides précédentes.
5

Cette série de méthodes décrites dans la thèse tracent un chemin d’évolution bien claire
de la recherche conversationnelle : de la pure recherche d’information via une conversation à
son intégration dans une application plus générale pour répondre à une question. C’est ainsi
que nous voyons la direction pour le développement futur.
Mots clés: Recherche conversationnelle, Recherche d’information, Génération augmentée
par recherche, Modélisation des interactions, Grands modèles de langage.

6

Abstract

Conversational search is concerned with information retrieval through a sequence of interactions between users and an information system. Existing conversational search systems are
primarily based on natural language dialogue, which provides a flexible interactive interface to
satisfy the complex users’ information needs. Different from the traditional ad-hoc search information system with keyword-based query as input and “ten blue links” as return results, the
conversational search system requires comprehending the evolving user search intent among
the ongoing conversations, and conducting information retrieval and retrieval-augmented
generation to provide a final response to the user.
In this thesis, we try to improve the performance of the conversational search system by
developing various approaches addressing different underlying problems. We first investigate
the approaches to understand user search intent in multi-turn human-system interactions via
conversational query reformulation. Then, we further conduct historical context modeling to
facilitate history-aware conversational dense retrieval to improve the retrieval results. With the
retrieved information, we continue to explore how to utilize it to augment response generation.
Our initial explorations try to leverage historical information to boost conversational retrievalaugmented generation quality. Then, we optimize the decoding procedure of generative
language models by developing a mechanism to incorporate document quality for retrievalaugmented generation. Furthermore, we propose a unified framework to optimize both
conversational information retrieval and conversational retrieval-augmented generation via
an end-to-end manner.
All proposed approaches are evaluated on widely used conversational search and question
answering benchmarks. The experimental results show our different methods all achieve
substantial improvements over previous strong baselines.
The series of methods described in this thesis portrait a clear path of evolution of
conversational search: from pure conversational search to its integration in a more general
application to answer questions. This is the direction we identify for future development of
conversational search.

7

Keywords:

Conversational Search,

Information Retrieval,

Retrieval-

Augmented Generation, Interaction Modeling, Large Language Models.

Chapter 6.

Open Large Language Model Decoding to Incorporate Document
Quality in Retrieval-Augmented Generation . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 127

4.5

Overview of HAConvDR. The first stage (left) is to conduct pseudo relevance
labeling (PRL) between the current query and each historical turn. Based on
the PRL results, the second stage (middle) is to perform context-denoised query
reformulation and positive and negative supervision signals mining. The third stage
(right) is to conduct conversational dense retrieval training with history-aware
contrastive learning. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 92

Chapter 1

Introduction

Search engines have become integral to modern society, serving as essential tools to satisfy
users’ information needs. The development of search tools is accelerated by the advances in
artificial intelligence (AI) techniques. A notable advancement is conversational search, an
emerging paradigm that provides multi-turn natural language interactions between human and
AI systems for complex and accurate information access (46; 221), which is the foundational
technique of different commercial conversational search systems in the era of generative AI,
such as Perplexity.ai1 and SearchGPT2.
Conceptually, conversational search represents a paradigm shift in interactive information
access, moving beyond traditional single-shot queries toward a dynamic framework (236; 130).
Compared to traditional search engines that rely on keywords or short phrases, conversational
search engines (25; 221) greatly enhance the efficiency of information exchange and optimize
the user experience. This approach supports more complex user queries, manages longer
and more intricate historical contexts, and offers comprehensive capabilities for information
integration and processing. Fig. 1.1 illustrates the differences between traditional ad-hoc
search and conversational search. In this real example, a traditional search engine (singleshot search) fails to maintain the context across queries. After the user’s query, “What
is information retrieval”, and then follows up with “Tell me some of its famous scholars”,
the traditional search engine provides irrelevant results because it does not recognize that
the user is still referring to “information retrieval scholars”. In contrast, the conversational
search engine (multi-turn search) successfully manages multiple query turns by retaining
context, correctly interpreting the follow-up question, and providing accurate information
about notable scholars in the field of information retrieval.

1Perplexity.ai: https://www.perplexity.ai/
2

SearchGPT: https://searchgpt.com/

Query 1

what is information retrieval
Information retrieval is the science of
searching for information in a document,
searching for documents themselves, and
also searching for the metadata that ...

Answer

Information retrieval (IR) is a crucial field within computer science and
information science that focuses on the process of identifying and
retrieving information from a collection of resources in response to a user's
information need …

today, these are some of the best
professionals in the scholar field.

of information retrieval include:

computational linguistics...

Fig. 1.1. Comparison between a traditional search engine and a new conversational search
engine on an original and its follow-up query. The term “its” in the second query refers to
the term “information retrieval” in the first query. However, it is challenging for traditional
search engines to understand such omissions in follow-up user queries.

The transition from single-turn to interactive search enables users to satisfy complex,
evolving information needs through sustained, multi-turn natural language dialogue with an
intelligent system. The core functionality of conversational search systems is to support the
evolving information goal, where a user’s initial, possibly vague, information need is refined or
shifted through interaction. Thus, the conversational search systems require understanding the
users’ search intent within their context-dependent queries by comprehending the associated
historical context. Besides, the development of generative language models facilitates the
goal of search tasks beyond mere relevance to encompass a richer user experience with less
cognitive load and higher efficiency, which not only returns the relevant web page links but
also synthesizes a final response as an output. Ultimately, conversational search systems
should first achieve the information retrieval task and then be capable of integrating the
search results to facilitate the retrieval-augmented response generation task.
To implement a conversational search system, multiple components should work collaboratively to manage the multi-turn interactions, understand evolving intent, access information,
and present it effectively, ultimately enhancing the overall search experience. In this thesis,
34

we focus on two important tasks in conversational search: i) conversational information
retrieval and ii) conversational retrieval-augmented generation, targeting addressing their
corresponding research questions and challenges to facilitate Human-AI interactions.

1.1. Challenge of Conversational Search
The task of conversational search can be defined as identifying relevant information from a
large knowledge collection for the users’ queries in conversations, where their input is contextdependent. A conversational search process faces three key issues: i) Understanding users’
search intent conditional on the historical context, ii) Improving conversational information
retrieval performance, and iii) Optimizing the utility of the retrieved information for final
response generation. We will review them in the following sections.

1.1.1. Difficulties in Historical Context Modeling and Conversational Information Retrieval
The conversational information retrieval task requires the conversational search systems to
retrieve relevant information from external knowledge bases to satisfy the user’s information
needs within the context of an ongoing dialogue. As conversations progress, the conversational
historical context can become increasingly complex and lengthy, posing challenges of handling
such multi-turn inputs to understand users’ real search intent.
To capture the real information needs in each query turn, an intuitive method is conversational query reformulation (40), which leverages a reformulation model to transform
the context-dependent queries (i.e., the last query, together with the previous conversation
context) into stand-alone queries, which can then be used by any ad-hoc retriever models.
Given that existing ad-hoc search models can be reused directly, conversational query reformulation demonstrates substantial practical value for industries in quickly initializing
their conversational search engines. However, optimizing the reformulation model for the
downstream search task in a two-stage, reformulation-retrieval, approach is challenging. This
difficulty arises due to the separate generation process in reformulation, which disrupts the
back-propagation of gradients, resulting in sub-optimal performance (199; 115). Besides, the
supervision signals or the standard for evaluating the reformulated queries are usually lacking
in existing datasets and practical situations (185; 81), which makes it difficult to identify
whether the reformulated queries are beneficial for information retrieval tasks.
Another alternative solution is to learn a conversational dense retriever to encode the user’s
real search intent and candidate documents into a learned embedding space in an end-to-end
manner. The implicit query understanding ability is expected to be improved via fine-tuning
with relevance signals, so that the trained conversational retrievers can directly handle the
35

whole conversation session as input queries. However, most of the existing conversational
dense retrievers (216; 96; 78) are simply learned by fine-tuning the pre-trained ad-hoc encoders
via contrastive learning on conversational search data. As conversational search contexts
are much more diverse and long-tailed, these methods show unsatisfactory performance via
being trained on limited data (6; 2). This is because 1) the initial ad-hoc encoders undergo
pre-training exclusively using concise ad-hoc queries; and 2) the current conversational search
data, primarily human-generated, is not as abundant as ad-hoc search data due to the limited
deployment of real conversational search systems. Besides, simply leveraging all historical
context might inject additional noise to degrade the performance, as not all historical context
needs to be useful for current turn retrieval. Thus, the main challenge is the complexity
of encoding the entire context, where the tailored historical context modeling is helpful to
identify useful information or signals from the context and eliminate the harmful parts.

1.1.2. Difficulties

in

Optimizing

Conversational

Retrieval-

Augmented Generation
A key distinction between conversational search systems and traditional search engines is
their ability to deliver more than just a list of links. Conversational search systems can tailor
their responses to better suit users’ needs, providing various output formats such as direct
and concise answers (149), summarized contents (41), or even structured data like tables (94).
This is conducted after the conversational information retrieval stage, and then the system
tries to synthesize the retrieved information along with the contextual historical context to
generate precise and relevant responses based on a generative language model.
In the era of generative AI, the development of large language models (LLMs) can directly
return a response to the user based on the parametric knowledge under a conversational/chat
mode without search tools. However, the LLMs would generate plausible but incorrect
responses for some given queries when the internal knowledge is out-of-date. Thus, a
desirable way to incorporate powerful LLMs with the search component is to supply the
up-to-date knowledge retrieved from external resources for developing an intelligent and
complete conversational search system. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) (49) is a
widely used technique for integrating search and response generation. However, the existing
methods (33; 42) simply adapt the single-turn RAG pipeline even under the conversational
scenario, which only leverages the reformulated query of the current turn and its associated
retrieved results as the input for the generator model. Such a paradigm overlooks the
information from historical turns and might result in sub-optimal performance (160). Thus,
the challenge lies in how to leverage historical information, in addition to the query-response
pairs, to facilitate the conversational RAG (213).
36

Besides, another challenge remains in managing the effective integration of internal and
external knowledge sources (56). This is related to making RAG systems more robust to the
noise introduced during the retrieval phase (i.e., retrieved non-relevant information), and
enabling the systems to understand to what extent to rely on internal or external knowledge
to respond to the users. With the improvements in either conversational information retrieval
or retrieval-augmented response generation, a more challenging question is how to jointly
optimize both tasks in an end-to-end manner (93), which can facilitate the system’s fidelity
to utilize retrieved knowledge and generate more precise and informative responses.

1.2. Our Approaches and Contributions
In this thesis, we focus on both the conversational information retrieval task and the
conversational retrieval-augmented generation task, as well as their joint optimization. Our
approaches and contributions are briefly summarized as follows:
• Understanding Search Intent with Generative Query Reformulation. There
are two methods to overcome the semantic missing in context-dependent users’ queries:
query rewriting and query expansion. They can produce different effects by restoring
the missing tokens in the context or introducing relevant supplementary information
to the query. We propose a generative query reformulation approach to combine
the advantages of both approaches. Then, we further leverage the advancement of
LLMs with their parametric knowledge to enhance the generative query reformulation
procedure from multiple aspects.
• Capturing Concise Query Representation via End-to-End History-Aware
Conversational Dense Retrieval. The two-stage “reformulation-retrieval” pipeline
cannot directly optimize the downstream retrieval tasks. To capture the more concise
query representation in conversational scenarios, a more desirable way is to leverage
historical context to conduct end-to-end conversational dense retrieval, where the
retriever model can implicitly model the user search intent with the whole context
as input. To improve the performance of conversational information retrieval, we
focus on conducting tailored historical context modeling to identify useful historical
interactions and eliminate the harmful information from the complex and long-tailed
dialogue. Then, the selected historical turns would facilitate the model’s history-aware
capacity in conversational dense retrieval.
• Utilizing Various Historical Information to Boost Conversational RetrievalAugmented Generation. Different from the single-turn retrieval-augmented generation, more abundant historical information is available in the conversational scenarios.
We investigate how to leverage different types of historical information, including
37

historical search results, historical turn dependency, and historical grounded evidence, and verify their feasibility to boost the response generation performance in a
training-free manner.
• Optimizing Decoding Procedure of Language Models for Robust and Effective Generation. Although it is generally assumed that the retrieved information is
relevant and useful for content generation, or the LLMs can judge its relevance, this
is not always true in practice. This is because the noise is usually contained in the
retrieved information due to the retrieval defects, and the language models might not
understand the limits of their parametric knowledge and capacity. To improve the
noise tolerance capacity of the generator models, we develop a training-based approach
to guide the LLMs to incorporate document quality in the retrieval-augmented generation task. This framework serves as a new paradigm to modulate the LLMs’ internal
information processing mechanisms with explicit indicators to improve robustness in
answer decoding when the input context contains various noise. Different types of
explicit quality indicators are constructed by extracting features from the retrieved
documents, and then they are applied to modify the attention score computation
among the networks of LLMs. In addition, a robustness enhancement mechanism is
integrated into the training procedure to enable the models to handle various noisy
environments.
• Optimizing Retrieval and Generation via Unified Framework. Though we
achieve significant improvements in both the conversational information retrieval
task and the response generation task, jointly optimizing both is crucial to building
an intelligent conversational search system. We first present a unified generative
language model that can handle both retrieval and generation tasks in conversational
scenarios. Two mechanisms are designed to seamlessly integrate retrieved information
into response generation and address data discrepancy issues during joint fine-tuning.
Then, we further explore the agentic paradigm by proposing a unified framework
via end-to-end training to optimize conversational search agent models. The agentic
systems interleave search and reasoning across turns in conversations and are equipped
with contextualized reasoning capability, including history-conditional search query
generation, and the utilization of search results for accurate answer generation via
reinforcement learning.

1.3. Organization of the Thesis
The remaining of the thesis is organized as follows.
38

In Chapter 2, we will introduce some background knowledge of conversational search
systems, as well as the paradigms and components to achieve conversational search, such
as conversational query reformulation, conversational information retrieval, conversational
retrieval-augmented generation, and their evaluation.
In Chapter 3, we will address the problem of understanding user search intent in multi-turn
interactions by query reformulation via converting context-dependent queries into stand-alone
ones for conversational information retrieval.
In Chapter 4, we further improve the understanding of user search intent via historical
context modeling to achieve history-aware conversational dense retrieval. We will describe
how to identify relevant historical turns via the impact of usefulness in downstream tasks to
facilitate current turn retrieval.
In Chapter 5, our focus shifts from the information retrieval task to the retrieval-augmented
response generation task. The historical information is used to boost conversational retrievalaugmented generation via different designed training-free strategies.
In Chapter 6, we aim to improve the effectiveness and robustness of the system in the
retrieval-augmented generation task by incorporating document quality in language model
decoding. We will describe the corresponding training framework rather than solely relying
on prompting approaches.
In Chapter 7, we focus on how to optimize conversational information retrieval and
response generation via unified frameworks in an end-to-end manner.
Finally, general discussions about this thesis and some conclusions, as well as future
studies, will be given in Chapter 8.

39

Chapter 2

Background of Conversational Search and
Related Work

This chapter provides necessary background knowledge of conversational search and the
literature review related to this thesis. We first present a brief definition of conversational
search systems (Section 2.1), with the introduction of components in the systems and their
technical terms. Then, the associated approaches on conversational query reformulation
(Section 2.2), conversational information retrieval (Section 2.3), conversational retrievalaugmented generation (Section 2.4), and the evaluation of the conversational search systems
(Section 2.5) are introduced in each following section, which will provide the necessary basis
to contextualize the Chapters about our research.

2.1. Definition of Conversational Search
Conversational search aims to provide the flexibility based on multi-turn interactions
between human and AI systems to satisfy users’ complex information needs. A conversational
session could contain a current user query qn and n − 1 associated historical turns Hn =
n−1
{qi , Pi , ri }n−1
i=0 , where qi , {P i }i=0 and ri denote the user queries, search results and generated

response of each preceding interaction, respectively. If we also include the utilization of
retrieved information in answer generation, then the conversational information accessing
system requires first retrieving top-k relevant passages1 Pn = {ptn }kt=1 from a large collection
C via a retriever model Ret as Pn = Ret(qn , C), then using the retrieved information to
augment the generator model G to produce the current turn response rn to the user query
qn as rn = G(qn , Pn , Hn ). Finally, the results produced by the retriever model and generator
model will be evaluated as an information retrieval task and an answer generation task. This
1In this thesis, the terms “passage” and “document” are used interchangeably when describing a piece of

retrieval unit without explicit distinction.

is the general definition of conversational search related to the content of this thesis, while
the broader definition related to mix-initiative actions (3) is not involved.

2.2. Conversational Query Reformulation
In conversational search, query reformulation is a common technique to deal with the
complex user intent, which is often obscured within follow-up questions and results in contextdependent queries (27; 28; 29). Unlike traditional information retrieval, conversational search
usually needs to address two key semantic phenomena: anaphora and ellipsis (180; 145).
Anaphora occurs when the semantics of an expression depend on the preceding context,
whereas ellipsis involves the omission of semantic elements that are understood from prior
discourse. For instance, following the example given in Fig. 1.1, where the first query is “what
is information retrieval”. The second query “tell me some of its famous scholars exhibits
the anaphora phenomenon, as the word “its” has no independent meaning on its own and
should refer to the phrase “information retrieval”. If the third query is “And the one on
computer vision”, then it conceals the ellipsis phenomenon. Because this follow-up query is
completely missing the verb and the core question structure, where the full, unabbreviated
meaning is: “who are the famous scholars in computer vision”. Thus, to reveal the true
user intent, systems should employ adaptive context tracking to resolve these references
and reconstruct omitted information. Consequently, query reformulation approaches need
to be a dynamic, context-aware process that continuously evolves alongside the dialogue
trajectory. Existing literature generally categorizes conversational reformulation methods
into two primary approaches: query expansion and query rewriting. These are discussed in
detail below, followed by the evaluation of conversational query reformulation performance.

2.2.1. Query Expansion
Query expansion is an effective technique for improving retrieval performance in traditional
IR (39; 204), where the expansion terms can come from different resources such as user logs,
pseudo-relevant feedback, and document collection to provide the relevant supplementary
information to the query. Due to the lack of query reformulation annotation, earlier works
in conversational IR borrow the idea of query expansion from traditional IR to expand the
current query turn from different resources (81; 185; 129). Various alternatives can be used
to achieve query expansion, including relevance term classification, knowledge expansion, etc.
Relevant term classification transforms query reformulation as a binary classification task,
where terms in the query context are labeled as relevant or irrelevant. The relevant terms are
then appended to the original query as expansion. This approach systematically incorporates
important terms into the original queries, thereby improving the retrieval performance.
42

Typically, Kumar et al. (81) develop a binary term classifier with weak-supervision signals
to select relevant terms from the dialog history and pseudo relevance feedback (PRF) to decontextualize the conversational query. Voskarides et al. (185) propose QuReTeC with distant
supervision, whose pseudo labels are readily available and can be inferred from user-system
interactions. Lin et al. (96; 100) estimate term importance during query reformulation, which
evaluates the significance of different terms within the context, ensuring that the most critical
terms are retained or emphasized in the reformulated query. As the capacity of pre-trained
language models continues to scale, the parametric knowledge from the language models
becomes one of the expansion resources (120; 190). The synthesized response generated by the
language models demonstrates their effectiveness for searching relevant information (114; 211).
Although the query expansion approaches achieve significant improvement in conversational search without relying on human labeling for model training, a remaining challenge
is to distinguish the usefulness of the expansion terms (12; 55), i.e., the pseudo supervision
signals generated by the probability of language modeling guarantee the effectiveness for
search. This is an open question in the context of query expansion for IR, where we will
investigate this problem in this thesis.

2.2.2. Query Rewriting
Query rewriting is a fundamental approach to transform context-dependent queries into
stand-alone ones conditioned on the previous conversational context, which has been applied
in early studies (40) and continues to evolve with advancements in LLMs (114; 127; 214).
In early studies, Yu et al. (215) propose a rule-based query simplification method that
generates distantly supervised data to train a query generation model. Lin et al. (97) deploy
sequence-to-sequence architecture models as a de facto principle to mimic the human-rewritten
query. Then, several following studies (30; 181) further improve the query rewriting results
by generating better rewritten queries. However, these approaches highly rely on the quality
of human annotations to train the rewriter models, which might limit the upper bound
of the conversational search performance. Wu et al. (199) first prove that the humanannotated rewritten query might not necessarily be the best search query. To this end, some
works (199; 18) adapt reinforcement learning by using retrieval improvements as reward
signals to optimize the rewriting model training toward downstream search tasks directly.
Besides, the other studies (145; 115) leverage edit-based methods to perform explicit query
rewriting by injecting the ranking signals. Inspired by the strong capabilities for long-context
understanding and text generation of LLMs, Mao et al. (114) leverage LLMs as search
intent interpreters for the first time to contribute to conversational search and enhance the
performance by fusing multiple prompting results. Ye et al. (211) achieve informative query
43

rewriting by multi-stage generation. To avoid the high cost of directly calling LLMs to
conduct query rewriting and human-annotated labor, Jang et al. (61) initialize the training
objective for each query turn by LLMs and design an iterative mechanism to improve the
generated query according to the ranking results.

2.2.3. Evaluation
The evaluation of query reformulation quality is critical to ensure the effectiveness
of conversational search systems. Two primary methods are employed to evaluate the
performance of reformulated queries: 1) lexical overlapping metrics for reformulated queries
and 2) end-to-end retrieval performance.
Lexical Overlapping Metrics.

The lexical overlapping metrics measure the accuracy

of the reformulated query by comparing it to a reference query using token-level precision,
recall, and F1-score. Precision evaluates the proportion of correctly generated tokens among
all generated tokens, recall measures the proportion of correctly generated tokens among
all relevant tokens, and the F1-score provides a harmonic mean of precision and recall.
These metrics provide a quantitative assessment of how closely the reformulated query
matches the reference query, offering insights into the exactness and completeness of the
query reformulation process (40; 6).
End-to-End Assessment.

Another method for evaluating query reformulation is end-to-

end assessment, which measures the effectiveness of the reformulated query in final retrieval
performance. This principle can be applied to both sparse and dense retrieval systems. Similar
to the ad-hoc search, after retrieving relevant passages by using the reformulated queries,
their effectiveness is measured based on the retrieval metrics such as Mean Reciprocal Rank
(MRR), Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG), and Recall at various cut-off
levels (e.g., Recall@10, Recall@100). This end-to-end evaluation provides a comprehensive
assessment of how well the reformulated queries perform in real retrieval scenarios, reflecting
the practical impact of the query reformulation on their search results (12; 115; 131).

2.3. Conversational Information Retrieval
In multi-turn information-seeking dialogues (150), conversational search systems should
retrieve relevant passages from an external large collection. This task is significantly more
complex than traditional ad-hoc retrieval, as it requires a deep semantic understanding of both
the immediate user query and the broader conversational context (46; 221; 128). Furthermore,
user-system interactions are often characterized by diverse linguistic patterns and frequent
topic shifts, which complicate context modeling. The primary challenges hindering current
44

conversational information retrieval models are multifaceted and can be categorized into the
following key aspects.
First, although different architectures of neural models have shown the advanced ability
of sequence modeling, e.g., encoder-based BERT (32), encoder-decoder-based T5 (153), and
recently emerged decoder-only deeper LLMs (206; 140; 64), the comprehensive interpretation
of multi-turn natural language dialogues remains a persistent challenge in the field. Second,
shifts in search intent often introduce extraneous noise into the historical context, potentially
obscuring the user’s current objectives (116; 131). Consequently, developing effective methods
to denoise conversational history is critical for maintaining retrieval precision. Third, training
data for conversational information retrieval remains scarce, as conversational search engines
lack the widespread deployment of their traditional ad-hoc counterparts (117; 15). The process
of labeling multi-turn interactions is exceptionally labor-intensive for human annotators,
and capturing precise relevance signals across extended dialogues is inherently difficult. To
address these challenges, the existing studies of conversational information retrieval can be
categorized into three groups: (1) conversation modeling, (2) context denoising, and (3) data
augmentation.

2.3.1. Conversation Modeling
Unlike traditional one-shot ad-hoc retrieval, modeling multi-turn conversations requires
sophisticated modeling for conversational context. HAM (151) is an early study that utilizes
BERT (32) and the attention mechanism to perform soft selection for useful conversation
histories by assigning different weights to historical interactions based on their relevance
and usefulness in terms of the current turn query’s information needs. Qu et al. (149)
propose a foundational approach for modeling conversational context by concatenating all
historical queries with the current turn and leveraging a simple PLM-based encoder to
transform it into a dense representation. Then, the relevance judgment annotations are used
as supervision signals to guide the model with the attention mechanism to re-weight the
different turns in the context. Le et al. (85) utilize the sparse retriever SPLADE (43) to
model the conversations into the sparse representation of queries and passages, and Jeong
et al. (62) form the conversational dependency to achieve phrase-level retrieval for search
results.
In the era of generative AI, the powerful context understanding ability of LLMs provides
more alternatives to facilitate conversational modeling. Pan et al. (142) introduce a chain-ofactions framework that utilizes systematic prompting and a suite of predefined operations to
manage conversational dynamics. Besides, Mao et al. (113) propose ChatRetriever, which
employs a streamlined yet effective conversational instruction-tuning technique. By combining
45

Fig. 2.1. An example of the end-to-end learning of the model for conversational dense
retrieval with implicit de-noising. Each historical turn would have a certain degree of
correlation with the current turn, e.g., irrelevant, partially relevant, or strongly relevant.
contrastive learning with an LLM-based dense retriever, their approach achieves superior
modeling of complex dialogue trajectories. Collectively, these works underscore the potential
of aligning LLMs for passage retrieval within multi-turn scenarios.

2.3.2. Context Denoising
Within a conversational session, not all historical query turns are relevant to address
the current user’s information needs (96; 199). Simply concatenating all historical context
during the conversation modeling would inject unnecessary and noisy turns, and thus degrade
the performance of the system (116; 126). To this end, existing studies aim to mitigate the
negative impact of irrelevant or noisy historical context. A common practice is shown in
Fig. 2.1, aiming to teach the model to implicitly identify to what extent (e.g., attention
scores) a historical turn is relevant to the current query. In this research line, Yu et al. (216)
leverages dense retrieval to encode conversational queries and passages into a shared vector
space where semantic similarity can be directly measured. It employs a teacher-student
framework, which involves using an existing, well-trained dense retriever as a teacher to
guide the student model, by inheriting its passages’ representations and learning to mimic
the teacher’s embeddings for queries. This strategy enhances model ability by capturing
the necessary context while ignoring unrelated information to improve information retrieval
accuracy across conversational turns. Mao et al. (116) develop a framework by combining
curriculum learning and contrastive learning to progressively guide a conversational query
encoder on how to filter out irrelevant information from the conversation history. It involves
46

generating noisy and clean versions of conversational data and using these to train the model
by gradually increasing the complexity and difficulty of the training data. Krasakis et al. (80)
aim to address context denoising by leveraging existing ad-hoc search data to understand
and filter out noisy context, improving the relevance judgment of retrieved passages without
additional fine-tuning on conversational datasets. Specifically, it contextualizes the query
embeddings within the conversation while ensuring that only the current query terms are
matched with potential answers. More recently, Mo et al. (132) propose QRACDR to first
define a better aligned embedding space for the query representation learning and enable
the model to learn to align the query representation with this area via rewritten queries
and relevance judgments, which obtains a less noisy context representation implicitly. Then,
Lupart et al. (107) follow this principle to leverage LLM to obtain further improvement.
Instead of implicitly conducting context denoising, identifying and selecting useful historical
conversational context explicitly is another alternative and could be used together with the
existing principle, which is investigated in this thesis.

2.3.3. Data Augmentation for Conversational Dense Retrieval
Effective query understanding within conversational information retrieval systems relies
heavily on large-scale datasets, due to the long-tail and complex linguistic phenomenon in
multi-turn scenarios. However, abundant annotations are usually unavailable to make the
training procedure feasible. To enrich the conversational retrieval data, various levels of data
augmentation are explored in the literature, including (1) relevance judgment generation, (2)
contrastive sample generation, and (3) conversation session generation.
Relevance Judgment Generation

In the context of information retrieval, manual

relevance judgments are expensive to obtain, while such an issue becomes much more obvious
in conversational search. To mitigate the scarcity of annotations, a common practice in the
literature is to generate them automatically. For example, Lin et al. (96) create pseudorelevance labels based on the pseudo relevance feedback to help guide the model to learn how
to integrate conversational query reformulation directly into a dense retrieval framework via
an end-to-end manner, which achieves a more coherent optimization across stages compared
with separately. Jin et al. (68) leverage LLMs to provide a form of supervision signal that can
guide the training of a conversational retriever without the need for extensive manual labeling.
The principle is to deploy LLMs as an “instructor” to generate soft labels, which indicate
the relevance of session-passage pairs through an unsupervised training framework with the
relevance score estimated by the LLMs. This approach sidesteps the data scarcity issue
by harnessing the generative capabilities of LLMs for data augmentation in conversational
information retrieval tasks.
47

Contrastive Sample Generation

Sufficient supervision signals, either positive or negative,

are important for dense retrieval (76) with contrastive learning (159). For conversation
scenarios, additional contrastive samples can be generated from the conversational history.
The basic assumption is that among the top-ranked historical retrieved results, the ones
highly relevant to the current query can be used as pseudo positive samples, while others are
less relevant and can serve as hard negatives for training. A typical approach is proposed by
Kim et al. (78) to identify a “retrieval shortcut” phenomenon, which occurs when models
overly rely on partial conversation history, but ignore the current query and results in topic
drift. In this thesis, we try to mine additional hard negatives from the historical turns to
mitigate the dependency on retrieval shortcuts by forcing the model to consider both the
current question and the entire conversation history.
Conversation Session Generation

As conversational search engines are not widely

deployed yet, it is challenging to obtain enough high-quality session-level search data to
facilitate the fine-tuning of conversational dense retrievers. To address this, Mao et al. (117)
propose a data augmentation solution by transforming readily available web search sessions
into conversational search sessions, which is based on the structural and behavioral similarities
between web search sessions and conversational search sessions. It reorganizes raw web search
sessions into a heterogeneous graph that represents the multi-turn nature of user interactions,
which is then enriched with additional queries to improve diversity. Then, a random walk
sampling algorithm is employed to generate pseudo conversational search sessions that can
be used to train conversational dense retrieval models. With the development of LLMs,
some studies (134; 70; 135) leverage LLMs to create additional conversational session data
to enhance the diversity and volume of training data through two approaches: dialoguelevel session generation, which creates entire sessions based on a given topic, and querylevel augmentation, which rephrases existing queries to express the same intent in various
ways. Similarly, Huang et al. (59) propose CONVERSER, a framework to provide guided
examples to achieve few-shot generation. Chen et al. (15) introduce ConvAug, addressing
the limited linguistic diversity in existing conversational search datasets that often hinders
model adaptation to real-world complexities. Drawing inspiration from human cognitive
processes to enhance data quality and minimize synthetic errors, ConvAug leverages LLMs to
produce multi-level augmented dialogues that encompass a broader spectrum of conversational
contexts. Furthermore, the framework incorporates a difficulty-adaptive sample filter to
isolate challenging instances, thereby effectively expanding the model’s learning horizon for
complex interactions.

48

2.4. Conversational Retrieval-Augmented Generation
The development of generative models facilitates the evolution of conversational search
systems that might enrich the user experience by producing contextually aware and personalized responses (229). As multi-turn dialogue becomes the standard paradigm for interacting
with LLMs in information retrieval tasks, the development of novel conversational search
systems, specifically those integrating generative modules, has attracted significant research
interest. Based on powerful LLMs as generators, response generation is coupled with retrieval
through Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) paradigms. This integration means the
generation component is not merely presenting information but actively synthesizing retrieved
knowledge within the ongoing conversational context to meet the user’s information needs,
further blurring the lines between traditional retrieval and generation stages.
Conversational RAG (213; 19) is not well-studied in the literature. The existing studies
mainly focus on single-turn RAG, which only treats a single stand-alone query or converts the
multi-turn context-dependent query by reformulation technique without leveraging historical
information for RAG (160). In terms of the single-turn RAG paradigm, the generation
component is pivotal for crafting responses that are both informed by external knowledge
and coherent with the parametric ones. The retrieved passages usually served as external
knowledge, and the generator model, usually the LLMs in existing studies, processes them
with two key methodologies (49): context curation (237; 207; 110) and generator finetuning (36; 98), which try to adapt the consistency between IR and RAG (24; 198; 125) and
satisfy the specific requirements of the domain or task, respectively. Besides, fine-tuning
knowledge bases or enhancing LLMs’ internal knowledge could be other alternatives to tune
systems to meet specific conversational search expectations in generative paradigms (217).
However, within the scope of this thesis, a standalone LLM, regardless of fine-tuning, does not
constitute a complete conversational search system without a dedicated retrieval component.
This is because solely relying on a language model with parametric knowledge is not enough
for information-seeking goals, and it is also the fundamental weakness of LLMs. Thus, the
existing LLMs’ application requires integration with search engines (e.g., RAG), and there is
no evidence showing that the functionality of the search component could be replaced (222).
Regarding the generation for conversational search, the single-turn RAG systems overlook
the historical information (2; 213; 126), e.g., historical search results, the context history, turn
dependency, etc. Because conversational search systems must account for evolving dialogue
dynamics and shifting contexts to ensure responses are both relevant and coherent, the
inclusion of historical information is essential for generating accurate and reliable results (221;
128). However, it is unclear whether the additional conversational context process can further
improve the generation results, which will be investigated in this thesis.
49

2.4.1. Utilization of Historical Search Results
One of the important but overlooked historical pieces of information is the search results
from previous turns, which might be helpful to the current turn RAG (213; 142). The
assumption is that the retrieved results from the earlier turns could be a supplementary
enhancement to the generation of the current turn’s responses. The potential improvement
might be attributed to increasing the recall of the current turn via improving search diversification or pushing the low-ranked documents to the top position when they frequently
occur in the previous turn via enhancement. From a much broader perspective, the key
idea is to integrate the historical search results with the current ones by selecting the useful
information and avoiding injecting additional noise. Then, the systems are expected to
construct a narrative that is not only contextually relevant but also dynamically adaptive to
the evolving conversational threads, allowing the system to exhibit a semblance of episodic
memory, recalling and referencing previous interactions to inform the current discourse.
Nevertheless, it is still unclear what principle should be followed to use historical search
results. This question is less explored in the literature. Two potential challenges should be
taken into account when designing the approaches. One challenge is the long context input,
while the long conversational session and large top-k retrieved list will significantly increase
the length of input and result in high latency costs. Another challenge lies in identifying
the utilization of historical search results. Since the relevance/usefulness could have some
inconsistency between IR and RAG, the passages identified by the retriever might not align
with the requirements of the generator. These challenges will be investigated in this thesis.

2.4.2. Integration of Retriever and Generator
One crucial factor for the quality of conversational RAG is how to better integrate the
information retrieval process into the content generation procedure, i.e., the collaborative
integration between the retrieval model and the generation model. There are two related
research lines in the literature. One is to optimize both tasks, so the intrinsic knowledge of the
retriever and generator could be shared and jointly improved (88). Another one focuses on the
capacity of the generator by effectively utilizing retrieval results from different enhancement
aspects (233; 99).
The principle of joint optimization of information retrieval and response generation tasks is
to leverage the supervision signals of both tasks for end-to-end training. Early studies attempt
to directly use the ground-truth answer of the generation task to impact the retriever, since
the relevance judgments for retrieval tasks are not differentiable (152; 157). The following
studies in the era of generative AI attempt to develop a unified model to connect both tasks
within a single framework (136; 113). The key challenge lies in maintaining the capacity of
50

both tasks within a single model without catastrophic forgetting (44), which obtains some
significant improvement via a jointly fine-tuning framework (136; 93). With the advancement
shown in reinforcement learning (RL), a series of studies tries to design the reward function
based on the ground-truth of the RAG task to directly guide the generator to interact with
the retriever, which enables the dynamic invocation for the retrieval models (67; 106).
On the other hand, some studies focus on optimizing the capacity of the generator to deal
with different response generation issues, such as long-context processing (147), efficiency
decoding (202), robustness enhancement for noisy environments (178), and faithfulness
identification (226). In the era of LLM, prompting (102), the advanced models are a simple
and effective way to instruct them to generate answers, where the answer decoding highly
relies on the designed prompt and internal attention mechanism. To improve the content
generation performance, these studies (201; 187; 31) try to select relevant and reliable retrieved
information for the generator input. However, we cannot expect the identification of LLMs
to be always correct, and judging each piece of retrieved information would result in a high
computation cost. In this thesis, we investigate more sophisticated approaches to utilize the
information retrieval process to augment the quality of the generated response.

2.5. Evaluation of Conversational Search Systems
A complete and intelligent conversational search system should be integrated with multiple
components, including query reformulation, historical modeling, conversational retrieval, and
response generation, etc. The evaluation of existing conversational search systems can be
divided into two categories (22): 1) based on ranking metrics (e.g., MRR, NDCG, Recall),
or 2) based on the semantic similarity metrics (e.g., F1, BLUE, and ROUGE), where the
former is usually applied to retrieval tasks while the latter is commonly used with response
generation tasks.

2.5.1. Retrieval-based Evaluation
Building upon the paradigm of ad-hoc retrieval, prevalent conversational information
retrieval frameworks often model individual turns as an independent query, utilizing sessionspecific relevance judgments as ground-truth. This is because the available conversational
search datasets are constructed by inheriting from the single-turn datasets. In addition to
the traditional ad-hoc search evaluation and focus on conversational features, Fu et al. (45)
examine whether the same pattern of the assessment in ad-hoc search exists in conversational
search based on both the user satisfaction and the observed performance of the search. Li et
al. (89) and Siblini et al. (166) argue that the existing benchmarks using ground-truth answers
provided in historical context are unreliable to evaluate the developed models regarding real
51

human-machine conversations. To simulate an open-ended environment with unpredictable
conversation trajectories for the practical situation, a series of studies leverages user simulators
to provide feedback to the system’s responses. Early works (163; 101) study how to estimate
the effectiveness of conversational search based on user simulation. Then, Wang et al. (193)
simulate the risk of clarifying user questions and develop a risk-aware control model in terms of
the search experience. To further facilitate the simulation of user feedback for conversational
search, some recent studies (141; 194; 10) develop user simulator-based frameworks to build
up more user-system interaction and improve the conversational search systems via specific
user feedback. Since developing new evaluation approaches is not our focus, we follow the
traditional evaluation standard in this thesis.

2.5.2. Generation-based Evaluation
The flexible user-system interaction provides the chance to address complex information
needs by multi-turn querying, which means the requirement of the user might not only be a
rank list but also a precise answer from the retrieved results. Such a paradigm has become
significantly more popular in the era of generative AI with the development of LLMs, making
generation-based evaluation essential. However, conventional metrics such as F1, BLEU
score, and exact match (EM) might not fully capture the quality of the generation. On
one hand, some reasonable and correctly generated answers might not be annotated as the
evaluation gold standard and fail in the traditional token-level metrics. On the other hand,
the LLMs can yield accurate responses despite imperfect retrievers or hallucinations with
semantically relevant retrieved text (198), raising the issue of evaluating the system by taking
into account both retrieval and generation. Aim to combine the top retrieved documents into
a comprehensive, pertinent, and concise response. Lajewska et al. (84) collect snippet-level
answer annotations for existing conversational search benchmarks to fill the evaluation gap
between retrieval and generation. To improve the correlation of retrieval effectiveness with
the performance of the generation procedure, Salemi et al. (162) propose the eRAG metric,
which evaluates the utilization of each retrieved document based on the ground truth labels
of downstream tasks. Nevertheless, the evaluation gap remains between the aspects of IR
and RAG, i.e., an IR-relevant document might not be effective for the downstream RAG
task (24). Thus, how to assess the performance of retrievers within LLM-based generators
and better align the retriever-generator with the overall conversational search system is still
an open challenge. In this thesis, we aim to improve the performance of response generation
via the joint optimization for both the retriever and generator in an end-to-end manner.

52

Chapter 3

Query Reformulation for User Search Intent
Understanding in Multi-Turn Interactions

3.1. Introduction
In Chapter 2, we define the general scenario of conversational search and introduce
its foundational applications and frontier challenges. The first core task, conversational
information retrieval, aims to satisfy users’ complex information needs by retrieving relevant
information from external knowledge through multi-turn conversational interactions. One of
the main challenges of conversational information retrieval is to determine users’ real search
intents in their context-dependent queries based on the interaction context and formulate
good search queries accordingly. To achieve this, the query reformulation technique (40)
is often employed to create de-contextualized queries that are compatible with standard
retrieval systems. The research in the literature predominantly categorizes these techniques
into two streams: query rewriting, which generates human-rewritten (215; 180) stand-alone
queries, and query expansion, which supplements queries with contextual keywords (81; 185).
Although both approaches achieve promising results, they are all studied separately.
Existing methodologies exhibit two primary limitations. First, query rewriting and query
expansion can produce different effects. Query rewriting resolves ambiguity by restoring
missing tokens, whereas query expansion introduces relevant supplementary information
to the query. Both effects are important for query reformulation, and it is thus beneficial
to use both of them. Second, previous query reformulation models have been optimized
to produce human-rewritten queries, independently from the passage ranking task. Even
though human-rewritten queries usually perform better than the original queries, existing
studies have shown that they may not be the best search queries alone (96; 199). Therefore,
it is useful to incorporate additional criteria directly related to ranking performance when
reformulating a query.

Fig. 3.1. An example of a conversational search session and the high-level comparison
between the original method and our proposed ConvGQR. The dashed box illustrates the
potential connection (underline) between the relevant passage and expansion terms.
As shown in the left part of Fig. 3.1, the current query is asking about the goat’s breed.
Although the human-rewritten query recovers the crucial missing information (i.e., “goat”)
from the context, it is still possible to further improve the search query. To tackle these
problems, we first propose ConvGQR, a new Generative Query Reformulation framework
for Conversational search. It combines query rewriting with query expansion. The right side
of Fig. 3.1 illustrates the differences between ConvGQR and the previous query rewriting
method. In addition to query rewriting based on human-rewritten queries, our ConvGQR
also learns to generate the potential answer of the query (e.g., the answer in the downstream
QA task) and uses it for query expansion. This strategy is motivated by the fact that a
passage containing the generated potential answer is more likely a relevant passage, because
either the generated answer is the right answer, or it may co-occur with the right answer in
the same passage. The final query reformulation model is trained by combining both query
rewriting and query expansion criteria in the loss function. Moreover, a knowledge infusion
mechanism leverages relevant passage information to supervise both query rewriting and
expansion, thereby aligning the generation process with the ultimate goal of maximizing
retrieval effectiveness.
Following the advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) since the introduction of
ConvGQR, there has been a significant shift toward integrating these models into conversational search systems for query reformulation. Initial studies (114; 211) employ LLMs to
generate search queries directly from conversational context. While seemingly straightforward, this approach has demonstrated superior effectiveness in query rewriting compared
to fine-tuning smaller language models, such as T5 (155; 23). However, these performance
gains are predominantly attributed to the use of proprietary, closed-source LLMs (140; 7).
54

This disparity exists largely because the closed-source models consistently exhibited superior
capabilities in complex reasoning tasks compared to their open-source counterparts (51; 73)
during the initial stages of the LLM era.

Fig. 3.2. An illustrative example of a conversational history (left box) and the gold positive
passage relevant to the last user turn. The enhanced history obtained using our method,
CHIQ, is in the middle box. The right box shows the three search queries generated by
an LLM conditioned on the original history, and our CHIQ-FT and CHIQ-AD variants.
Underlined terms in the gold passages are those that appear in the query generated by our
approaches, which is conditioned on the enhanced history and did not appear in the query
generated by the method that uses the original history.
One of the main challenges in conversational search resides in the ambiguous nature of
the conversation history. As an example shown in Fig. 3.2, solving the co-reference relation
in q4 and synthesizing the detailed information in r3 are crucial for generating an effective
search query. While even small-scale open-source LLMs (e.g., 7B parameters) possess the
fundamental NLP capabilities required for these tasks (177; 64), the primary difficulty lies
in fully leveraging these capabilities for query reformulation. Thus, the key challenge is to
unlock the full capabilities of open-source LLMs for conversational query reformulation. This
requires a strategic pre-processing of the conversation history to enhance its quality, rather
than relying on the raw conversation for direct query generation.
To this end, we propose CHIQ, an LLM-enhanced framework designed to enhance the
quality of contextual history for improving query reformulation. As illustrated in Fig. 3.2,
we leverage the inherent NLP capabilities of LLMs (e.g., solving co-reference relations or
expanding the context) alongside their vast parametric knowledge to disambiguate the
conversation history. This refinement, in turn, enhances the relevance of the generated
55

search queries. We investigate various methods for integrating refined conversational history
into existing frameworks, including ad-hoc query reformulation, the generation of pseudosupervision signals for query reformulation model fine-tuning, and a hybrid fusion of both
approaches.
We conduct extensive experiments across five well-established conversational search
benchmarks under both dense and sparse retrieval settings. The experimental results indicate
that our ConvGQR outperforms most existing query reformulation methods based on small
language models, and our CHIQ achieves state-of-the-art performance across most settings,
often surpassing systems powered by closed-source LLMs with the enhanced conversational
history.

3.2. Previous Study
3.2.1. Task Formulation
The query reformulation task in conversational search is formulated as designing a query
reformulation model to transform the current query qn together with the conversational
′
historical context Hn = {qi ,ri }n−1
i=1 into a reformulated query qn , where the qi and ri denote

the query and the system answer of the ith previous turn, respectively. Then, this query qn′
is used to retrieve the relevant passages p+
n from a large passage collection C via a retriever
model. The system performance highly depends on the quality of the reformulated query qn′ .

3.2.2. Query Reformulation in Conversations
The intuitive idea is that a well-formulated search query from the conversation context
can be submitted to an off-the-shelf retriever for search without modifying it. Query rewriting
and query expansion are two typical query reformulation methods. Query rewriting aims
to train a rewriting model to mimic human-rewritten queries. This approach is shown to
be able to solve the ambiguous problem and recover some missing elements (e.g., anaphora)
from the context (215; 97; 180; 114). However, Wu et al. (199) and Lin et al. (96) argue that
the human-rewritten queries might not necessarily be the optimal queries. Wu et al. (199)
enhance the rewriting model by leveraging reinforcement learning. However, it turns out
that reinforcement learning requires a long time for training. To be more efficient, Lin et
al. (96) propose a query expansion method by selecting the terms via the normalization
score of their embeddings, but it still needs to re-train a retriever. Some earlier query
expansion methods (81; 185) also focus on selecting useful terms from conversational context
via classifying whether a term should be expanded. The previous studies show that query
rewriting and query expansion can both enhance the search query and produce better retrieval
56

results. However, these approaches have been used separately. Our ConvGQR model thus
integrates both query rewriting and query expansion to reformulate a better conversational
query.

3.2.3. Large Language Models Enhanced Query Reformulation
In the era of LLMs, there have been multiple endeavors to integrate LLMs to solve
traditional ad-hoc search sub-tasks (236), such as achieving query expansion via parametric
knowledge (190; 47), conducting dense retrieval (109; 189) using generative language model as
backbone, and performing re-ranking by prompting LLMs to output the document order (172).
In terms of query reformulation in conversational search, Mao et al. (114), and Ye et al. (211)
explore how LLMs can understand users’ contextualized search intents via tailored prompting
methods. Unlike direct rewriting the query using LLMs, we investigate various approaches
for integrating refined conversational history into query formulation frameworks.

3.3. ConvGQR: Generative Query Reformulation for
Conversational Search
The primary objective of query reformulation is to generate a rewrite that aligns with the
interpretation of a human expert. Such a process effectively resolves linguistic ambiguities
inherent in conversational queries, such as ellipsis and co-reference. Consequently, query
rewriting serves as an integral part of framework. Query rewriting can be cast as a sequenceto-sequence text generation problem: given the query in the current turn and its historical
context, we aim to generate a rewritten query. Inspired by the large capability of pre-trained
language model (PLM), we employ a generative PLM for query rewriting to mimic the human
query rewriting process.
However, relying solely on query rewriting may not yield the optimal search query (215; 6)
and the standard query rewriting models are agnostic to the retriever (96; 199), a query
rewriting model alone cannot produce the best search query. For example, the query rewriting
model is trained alone without the retrieval signals and mostly focus on anaphora and ellipsis
problems. The other information, e.g., the co-occurrence terms in the relevant passages, could
be also helpful for improving the retrieval performance. To bridge this gap, we incorporate a
query expansion component designed to supplement the query with terms likely to appear
in relevant passages. While various expansion strategies exist, we adopt a method proven
effective in the domain of question answering (120; 16): generating a potential answer based
on the current query and its historical context. These generated answers serve as expansion
terms, effectively leveraging the vast repository of world knowledge implicitly captured within
57

Generative PLM – Expansion

Fig. 3.3. Overview of our ConvGQR. Two generative PLMs are used to generate a rewritten
query and expansion terms for both training and inference. The knowledge infusion mechanism
(dashed boxes) is only applied during training.
a large PLM. The generated potential answer can be useful for passage retrieval in two
situations: i) the generated answer is correct, so a passage containing the same answer could
be favored; ii) the generated answer is not a correct answer, but it co-occurs with a correct
answer in a passage. This can also help determine the correct passage, and this is indeed the
very assumption behind many query expansion approaches used in IR. Motivated by this, we
use another PLM to generate the potential answer to expand the current query.
The overview of our proposed ConvGQR is depicted in Fig. 3.3. It contains three main
components: query rewriting, query expansion, and knowledge infusion mechanism. The last
component connects query reformulation and retrieval.

3.3.1. Query Reformulation by Combining Rewriting and Expansion
Both the query rewriting and query expansion modules utilize the historical context
Hn = {qi ,ri }n−1
i=1 concatenated with the current query qn as their input. Following the
convention established in a previous study (199), we employ a separation token “[SEP]”
between adjacent turns and arrange the conversation history in reverse chronological order,
as formally defined in Eq. 3.3.1.
S = [CLS] qi [SEP] qi−1 · · · q1 [SEP].
Query Rewriting.

(3.3.1)

The objective of the query rewriting model is to learn a mapping

function F(Hn , qn ) = q ∗ parameterized by a generative PLM, where q ∗ is a target sequence
used as the supervision signal (which is a human-rewritten query in the training data).
Through this process, the model learns to extract salient information from the context
58

Hn that is absent in the current query qn , thereby generating a stand-alone rewrite that
approximates q ∗ . Ultimately, the optimization of the model parameters θF is conducted via
maximum likelihood estimation:
θF = arg max
θF
