∏︂

Pr (q ∗ |F{Hn , qn }, θF ) .

(3.3.2)

i=1

Query Expansion. Previous studies indicate that PLMs can function as closed-book QA
systems, leveraging their internal parametric knowledge to respond directly to queries (152; 2).
While the factual accuracy of these generated responses is not always guaranteed, such
potential answers serve as effective expansion terms (120), steering the retrieval process
toward passages containing the predicted answer or semantically similar information. During
training, we employ the ground-truth answer r∗ as the target objective. r∗ could be a short
entity, a consecutive segment of text, or even non-consecutive text segments, depending on
the dataset. In inference for a new query, the potential answers are generated by the query
expansion model and used to expand the previously rewritten query.
A concrete example could be found in Fig. 3.1, where the query rewriting aims to recover
the terms “Boer goat” and the query expansion targets on adding the addition terms “1900s for
meat product” that co-occur in the relevant passage. Thus, the final form of the reformulated
query is the concatenation of the rewritten query and the generated potential answer as
expansion. The two generative PLMs for rewriting and expansion are fine-tuned with the
negative log-likelihood loss to predict the corresponding target with an input sequence {wt }Tt=1
as Eq. 3.3.3, however, with different training data.
Lgen = −

T
∑︂

log (Pr(wt |w1:t−1 , Hn , qn )) .

(3.3.3)

t=1

3.3.2. Knowledge Infusion Mechanism
An important limitation of the existing generative conversational query reformulation
methods is that they ignore the dependency between generation and retrieval. They are
trained independently. To bridge this gap, we propose a knowledge infusion mechanism to
optimize both query reformulation and search tasks during model training. The core intuition
is to align the hidden representations of the generative model with those of relevant passages.
If the hidden states of the generative model contain the information of the relevant passage,
the resulting queries are enriched with salient semantic features, thereby enhancing retrieval
effectiveness through improved semantic alignment.
To achieve this goal, an effective way is to inject the knowledge included in the relevant
passage representation into the query representation when fine-tuning the generative PLMs.
Concretely, we first deploy an off-the-shelf retriever acting as an encoder to produce a
59

representation hp+ for the relevant passage. To maintain consistency, the retriever is the
same as the one we use for search. Thus, the representation space for passages is kept the
same for both query reformulation and retrieval stages. Once the session query representation
hS is encoded by the generative model, we distill the knowledge of hp+ and infuse it into
the hS by minimizing the Mean Squared Error (MSE) as Eq. 3.3.4. Both hS and hp+ are
sequence-level representations based on the first special token “[CLS]”. Finally, the overall
training objective LConvGQR consists of query generation loss Lgen and retrieval loss Lret . A
weight factor α is used to balance the influence of query generation and retrieval.
Lret = MSE(hS , hp+ ),

(3.3.4)

LConvGQR = Lgen + α · Lret .

(3.3.5)

3.3.3. Training and Inference
Two generative models with different targets for query rewriting and expansion are trained
separately. The final output of the ConvGQR is the concatenation of the rewritten query and
the generated potential answer. The knowledge infusion mechanism is applied only for the
training stage, which guides optimization toward both generation and retrieval. The dense
retriever is frozen to encode passages for generative PLMs training.

3.4. CHIQ: LLM-Enhanced Contextual History for Improving Query Reformulation
The development of LLMs enables them to become the default option for conversational
query rewriting. Concretely, given the historical context Hn and the current turn query qn ,
the rewritten query qn′ could be produced by instructing an LLMs as follows:
qn′ ← LLM(I CQR ◦ Hn ◦ qn )

(3.4.1)

where ◦ denotes concatenation and I CQR is a manually-engineered instruction prompt
describing the CQR task. The choice of LLM(·) has predominantly favored closed-source
commercial models, such as ChatGPT, some of which require multiple iterations to get the
optimal query (114; 211). In this study, we leverage the basic NLP capabilities of open-source
LLMs to generate enhanced historical context H′ , a clearer and less noisy version of H. This
refined version can then serve as a substitute for H in Eq. 3.4.1, potentially improving the
quality of qn′ using an open source LLM.
60

3.4.1. History Enhancement with Parametric Knowledge
We introduce five distinct strategies to mitigate the linguistic ambiguity inherent in
conversational history Hn , mapping each approach to a foundational NLP task. Building
upon this, we describe the design of specialized prompts that enable an LLM to clarify specific
components or the entire historical context. The comprehensive prompts, accompanied by
illustrative examples for each scenario, are provided in Appendix B.
Question Disambiguation (QD).

Users often expect a human-to-human level of interac-

tion with modern conversational systems. They usually use acronyms, ambiguous words, or
co-reference substitutes when asking questions, assuming the system possesses native linguistic
intuition and robust reasoning skills. For a search system, the original user’s search intent
is often unclear and ambiguous. To address this, we introduce an LLM-based prompting
strategy, denoted as I QD , which processes the conversational history Hn and the current user
query qn . The goal is to generate qn′ , a self-contained, disambiguated version of the original
query, to serve as a refined input for the reformulation process defined in Eq. 3.4.1.
Response Expansion (RE).

In conversational interactions, system responses are fre-

quently concise, particularly for factoid-based queries. While such brevity facilitates quick
information acquisition for the user, it often lacks the descriptive richness required by retrieval
systems for effective query rewriting or expansion. To address this discrepancy, we design a
prompt I RE that instructs an LLM to enrich the content of the most recent system response.
By leveraging the preceding conversational history, the model synthesizes a more exhaustive
′
and self-contained response rn−1
. The enhanced historical context, Hn′ , is subsequently

constructed by substituting the original response with its enriched counterpart. For the
example shown in Fig. 3.2, the response r1 originally as a pure name “Pattie Boyd” r1′ could
be further expanded with the background of this person.
Pseudo Response (PR).

Given the demonstrated capacity of LLMs to encapsulate vast

amounts of human knowledge, they can be effectively employed to anticipate potential system
responses. The underlying intuition is that even a noisy predicted response may contain
noisy terms for retrieval, particularly when the LLM is instructed to generate a self-contained
answer. Consequently, we introduce a prompt I P R that utilizes the historical context Hn
and the current query qn to synthesize a pseudo response rn′ . This generated content is then
integrated into the input for Eq. 3.4.1, enriching the query generation process with additional
semantic context. For the example shown in Fig. 3.2, the response r4′′ is a pseudo response in
terms of current query q4 that is generated according to the parametric knowledge of LLMs.
Topic Switch (TS).

In conversational sessions, it is natural for different turns to address

distinct aspects of a topic, some of which may become irrelevant as the conversation evolves.
This issue is particularly pronounced in long-form conversations. In such scenarios, utilizing
61

the entire history is likely to introduce noise that distracts the CQR module, resulting in
sub-optimal query reformulation. To mitigate this, we introduce a prompt I T S that instructs
the LLM to detect whether a topic switch has occurred between the current query qn and the
preceding history Hn . If a shift is identified, the history is pruned to retain only the most
′
′
recent turn, Hn′ = {qn−1
, rn−1
}, while the earlier, irrelevant turns are discarded to ensure a

more focused generation of qn′ .
History Summary (HS).

As a conversation goes on, the accumulating historical context

often introduces more noisy and irrelevant information. A summary of historical context is
expected to contain only the most useful information of the original long context and better
serve for query expansion, e.g., a summary that concentrate the crucial information among
the multi-turn conversations. Consequently, we introduce a prompt I HS that processes
the conversational history, whether in its raw or previously enhanced form, to generate a
concise summary H′ . This summarized context serves as a refined foundation for subsequent
reformulation tasks.

3.4.2. Ad-hoc Query Reformulation
While the five methods described above can be employed independently to refine the
rewritten query qn′ , a more robust approach involves leveraging their complementary strengths
to construct a superior conversational history. To this end, we define several combinatorial
configurations for updating the inputs in Eq. 3.4.1. Specifically, +QD and +RE denote
′
the replacement of qn and rn−1 with qn ◦ qn′ and rn−1
, respectively. The +PR configuration

appends the pseudo response rn′ to the input sequence, while +TS prunes the history to the
most recent turn upon detecting a topic shift. Finally, +HS replaces the dialogue history with
a condensed summary. In our default configuration, we implement a hierarchical pipeline: we
first execute the TS detection. If a topic switch is confirmed, we apply the QD+RE+PR
configuration to the truncated history. In the absence of a topic switch, we apply QD+RE+PR
to the full history, followed by +HS to summarize the enriched context. Experimental results
are reported based on this integrated configuration.

3.4.3. Search-Oriented Fine-tuning
Prior research has demonstrated that fine-tuning small-scale language models, such as T5base, for conversational query generation is both effective and computationally efficient (97).
These models typically take the dialogue history Hn and the current query qn as input, using
either human-rewritten rewrites (199) or LLM-generated queries (61) as supervision signals.
However, a significant limitation of these approaches is their neglect of ranking signals during
62

training. Furthermore, the static supervision signals themselves may be sub-optimal for the
ultimate retrieval task (96; 129).
Considering that oracle search queries are typically unavailable and costly to annotate,
we propose extending the existing approach to generate pseudo-supervision signals for query
reformulation by leveraging the outputs produced in Section 3.4.1. More precisely, we propose
three modifications to Eq. 3.4.1 to obtain a search-oriented qn′ as follow:
Q′n ← LLM(Î CQR ◦ Hn′ ◦ qn ◦ p+
n)

(3.4.2)

where we replace Hn with the enhanced history Hn′ and update the instruction I CQR to
Î CQR to condition the query reformulation on the gold passage p+
n and prompt the LLM to
generate multiple pseudo queries in the same forward pass.
qn′ ← arg max
S(Q′n , p+
n ),
′
q

q ′ ∈ Q′n

(3.4.3)

Then, we select qn′ , the one with the highest retrieval score S determined by an off-the-shelf
retriever and relevance judgment from the set of pseudo queries Q′n as Eq 3.4.3, which is
used as the supervision signal to fine-tune a reformulation model with a mapping function
F(Hn′ ◦ qn ) = qn′ by maximum likelihood estimation. It is important to note that the process
described in this section is conducted strictly offline as a one-time procedure to generate
pseudo-labeled queries for fine-tuning our search-oriented reformulation model. During
inference, Hn and qn serve as inputs for the fine-tuned model to generate the query qn′ . Since
no LLM calls are required during the online stage, the system maintains low latency, ensuring
that retrieval efficiency remains largely unaffected.

3.5. Experiments
3.5.1. Experimental Setup
Datasets. Following previous studies (199; 78), five conversational search datasets are used
for our experiments. The TopiOCQA (2) and QReCC (6) datasets are used for normal query
reformulation training. Three other widely used TREC CAsT datasets (27; 28; 29) are only
used for zero-shot evaluation as no training data is provided. These datasets have different
concentrations as shown below. The statistics and more details are provided in Appendix A.
• TopiOCQA is a relatively new published dataset featured with topic switching. As
the conversation goes on, the topics may switch to related topics, a phenomenon
commonly observed in information-seeking search sessions.
• QReCC is conducted specifically for conversational open-domain question answering,
where each query turn would have the human-annotated rewritten query as reference.
63

• TREC CAsT are curated by the human experts of TREC Conversational Assistance
Track (CAsT). Each CAsT dataset has dozens of information-seeking conversations
comprising hundreds of turns.
Evaluation Metrics. To evaluate the retrieval results, we use four standard evaluation
metrics: MRR, NDCG@3, Recall@10, and Recall@100, as previous studies (6; 2; 116). We
adopt the pytrec_eval tool (183) for metric computation. We evaluate our approaches and
the compared methods for both dense and sparse retrieval.
Baselines. We compare our ConvGQR and CHIQ with a variety of baseline methods. The
ConvGQR is mainly compared with systems without using LLMs, including:
(1) Raw: The query of the current turn without reformulation.
(2) GPT2QR (6): A strong GPT-2 (153) based QR model.
(3) CQE-sparse (96): A weakly-supervised method to select important tokens only from
the context via contextualized query embeddings.
(4) QuReTeC (185): A weakly-supervised method to train a sequence tagger to decide
whether each term contained in historical context should be added to the current query.
(5) T5QR (97): A query rewriting approach fine-tuned with manual annotations provided
in QReCC as the supervised signals via the T5-base model.
(6) CONQRR (199): A reinforcement-learning and T5-based QR model which adopts
both BM25 and conversational fine-tuned T5-encoder as retrievers.
Note that CQE-sparse and ConvDR need to train a new conversational query encoder to
determine the relevance between the long context and passages, while the other baseline
methods and our ConvGQR are based on the off-the-shelf retriever only. Besides, the results
of Human-Rewritten queries in the original datasets are also provided.
The CHIQ is defined with three variants to produce different enhanced generated queries:
• CHIQ-AD directly uses the queries generated by the ad-hoc method described in
Section 3.4.2 as input for an off-the-shelf retriever.
• CHIQ-FT search queries generated by a small LM (e.g., T5) fine-tuned for the CQR
task following the approach described in Section 3.4.3.
• CHIQ-Fusion We fuse the rank list retrieved by CHIQ-AD and CHIQ-FT using the
result-level fusion technique (96), which consists of aggregating multiple ranked lists
retrieved by each query into a single list to produce Pn .
Then, it is compared with the LLM-enhanced conversational search systems, including:
(1) InstructorR (68): A LLM-based general conversational dense retriever tailored to
various tasks and domains by fine-tuning with various task-specific instructions and relevance
judgments based on the FlanT5-XL model.

64

(2) IterCQR (61): An iterative query rewriting method using the initial query generated
by ChatGPT-3.5 and refining a query turn multiple times according to the ranking signals
feedback during the training stage.
(3) RETPO (214): A retriever preference adapted query rewriting method that fine-tunes
LLaMA-2-7B as a QR model with an external QR dataset generated by GPT-4.
(4) LLM-Aided (211): An informative conversational query rewriting by directly prompting
ChatGPT-3.5 as both query rewriters and rewrite editors twice to incorporate all the desirable
properties for producing the final rewritten queries.
(5) LLM4CS (114): A state-of-the-art LLM-based prompting method for conversational
query rewriting. We implement it with full aggregation by calling LLMs five times for the
query and response generation, but without the chain-of-thought (CoT) content because of
the efficient annotation consideration in practical scenarios.
For the zero-shot scenario, ConvGQR continues to compare with CQE-Sparse, QuReTeC,
and T5QR systems. CHIQ additionally compares with LLM-based retriever, e.g., create the
query and document representation by the ending token from a decoder-only model, including
RepLLaMA (109), E5-Mistral (189), and LLM-Embedder (225), and two LLM-based query
expansion approaches HyDE (47) and Query2doc (190).
Implementation Details. We implement the generative PLMs for ConvGQR based on
T5-base (155) models. When fine-tuning the generative PLMs, the dense retriever is frozen
and acts as a passage encoder using ANCE fine-tuned on MS MARCO. For the zero-shot
scenario, we use the generative models trained on QReCC to produce the reformulated queries
and retrieve relevant passages. For CHIQ, we conduct experiments with the instruction-tuning
variants of LLaMA-2-7B (177) as LLM(·) in Eq. 3.4.1 and Eq. 3.4.2. In addition, we use
FlanT5-base (23) as the backbone when fine-tuning a CQR model on TopiOCQA and QReCC
to ensure fair comparison. The fine-tuning process consists of 10 epochs with a learning
rate of 1e-5 and a batch size of 8 for both datasets. The dense retrieval and sparse retrieval
(BM25) are performed using Faiss (69) and Pyserini (95), respectively.

3.5.2. Main Results
Main evaluation results on QReCC and TopiOCQA for dense retrieval and sparse retrieval
across different systems are reported in Table 3.1 and Table 3.2, respectively.
Our ConvGQR.

We find that ConvGQR achieves significantly better performance on

both datasets in terms of MRR and NDCG@3 and outperforms other methods on most
metrics, either with dense retrieval or sparse retrieval. For example, on QReCC with sparse
retrieval, it improves 15.1% MRR and 33.9% NDCG@3 over the second best results. This
indicates the strong capability of ConvGQR on retrieving relevant passages at top positions.

Table 3.1. Performance of dense retrieval methods on QReCC and TopiOCQA datasets.
Bold indicates the best results in either non-LLM-based and LLM-based group. † denotes
significant improvements with t-test at p < 0.05 for ConvGQR over T5QR, CHIQ-FT over

These results demonstrate the strong effectiveness of our method. Additionally, we observe
that CONQRR, which also utilizes downstream retrieval information but with reinforcement
learning, achieves better performance on certain recall metrics, suggesting that downstream
retrieval information is beneficial for conversational search and should be carefully leveraged.
Moreover, we find ConvGQR can even perform better than human-rewritten queries on
QReCC. It confirms our earlier assumption that the human-rewritten query (oracle query) is
not the silver bullet for conversational search. This finding is consistent with some recent
studies (96; 199; 119). The improvements of ConvGQR over human-rewritten queries are
mainly attributed to our query expansion and knowledge infusion, which introduce retrieval
signals to the learning of query reformulation.

that is complementary and not captured by CHIQ-AD.

3.5.3. Ablation Study
Compared to a standard query reformulation

Ablation on ConvGQR’s Components.

method, our proposed ConvGQR has two additional components, i.e., a query expansion
component based on generated potential answers and a knowledge infusion mechanism. We
investigate the impact of different components by conducting an ablation study on both
QReCC and TopiOCQA. The results are shown in Table 3.3. We observe that removing
any component leads to performance degradation and removing all of them drops the most.
In fact, when both components are removed, ConvGQR degenerates to the T5QR model.
The improvement of ConvGQR over T5QR directly reflects the gains brought by query
expansion and knowledge infusion. The above analysis confirms the effectiveness of the added
components.
Ablation on History Enhancement in CHIQ.

We study the contribution of each of

our proposed prompts in Section 3.4.1 for query enhancement by conducting ablation studies
on Hn′ when one of the prompts is not used. Table 3.4 presents the ablation performances of
dense and sparse retrieval on TopiOCQA and QReCC. The History Summary (HS) is ablated
alongside (TS), as HS is not activated if a new topic is detected.
We observe that all our proposed enhancements to the history context contribute positively
to the performance of the CHIQ-AD method, although some enhancements are more effective
than others. On one hand, detecting topic switching is particularly crucial on TopiOCQA,

3.5.4. Zero-Shot Performance
The zero-shot evaluation is conducted on three TREC CAsT datasets to test the transferability of ConvGQR and CHIQ. By comparing with the other strongest methods in Table 3.6,
we have the following main findings.
About ConvGQR.

The ConvGQR outperforms all the other methods on the more

difficult dataset CAsT-20 and CAsT-21, and matches the best results on CAsT-19, which
demonstrates its strong transferability to new datasets. The human-rewritten queries in CAsT
datasets achieve the highest retrieval scores, because they have been formulated carefully by
experts for search. This observation is different from the results of QReCC in Table 3.1, for
which query rewriting has been done by crowd-sourcing. However, this observation should not
lead to the conclusion that human-rewritten queries should be used as the gold standard for
the training of query rewriting. Because it is difficult to obtain a large number of high-quality
human-rewritten queries as in the CAsT datasets, which only contain a very limited number
(hundreds) of queries. Consequently, leveraging the parametric knowledge synthesized by the
PLM to generate expansion terms remains a highly effective strategy for optimizing retrieval
performance on unseen queries.
About CHIQ.

While CHIQ-FT exhibits a slight performance deficit relative to CHIQ-AD,

the systematic fusion of their outputs consistently yields superior results across all three
datasets. This trend aligns with the patterns observed in our previous evaluations in Table 3.1.

of improving the generalization capabilities of the models to handle complex and diverse

3.5.5. Impact of Generated Answer for Retrieval in ConvGQR
The aforementioned hypothesis of the ConvGQR for query expansion is that the PLMgenerated potential answers might contain useful expansion terms that co-occur with the
right answer in the relevant passages. To investigate how expansion terms are related to
retrieval performance, we use three metrics to analyze their correlation with the retrieval
score.
Correlation Analysis. Specifically, for each rewritten query with expansion terms, we
first calculate the token overlaps between the generated answers and the relevant passages,
which can measure their co-occurrence. However, the potential problem is that the generated
answers or relevant passages are of variable lengths. Therefore, we further normalize it by the
length of its corresponding relevant passage. Besides, we compute the F1 scores between the
generated answers and the gold answers to explore if the generation quality has an impact
on retrieval effectiveness. Finally, we calculate the Pearson Correlation Coefficient (PCC)

The results are shown in Fig. 3.4. The relative PCC value can reflect the helpfulness of
generated answers for different datasets to some extent. For example, the PCC of QReCC and
CAsT-20 are higher than TopiOCQA and CAsT-19, suggesting that the potential answers
are more useful in the first datasets. This is consistent with our previous experimental
observations that QReCC and CAsT-20 have larger improvements by ConvGQR compared
to TopiOCQA and CAsT-19. Thus, the co-occurrence between generated answer and the
relevant passage is crucial for the retrieval effectiveness for ConvGQR.
The PCC of generative score F1 is the highest among the three metrics, which indicates
its strong correlation with retrieval effectiveness. However, utilizing generated answers alone
as search queries could produce false positive results as we will demonstrate in the subsequent
analysis. As a result, it may not reflect the genuine correlation strength in comparison to the
co-occurrence metric.
Effects of Different Generated Forms. Table 3.7 presents a performance comparison of
three distinct reformulation strategies: the rewritten query, the generated pseudo-answer, and
the concatenation of both. This analysis allows us to evaluate the individual and collective
contributions of the rewriting and expansion components to retrieval effectiveness. We
find that using the concatenation of both significantly outperforms the two other forms
alone, indicating that these two forms can complement each other to achieve better retrieval
performance, which confirms again our initial hypothesis. Additionally, we observe that using
the rewritten query alone yields better results than using the generated answer, particularly
on TopiOCQA. The potential reason is the different forms of answers in the datasets: QReCC
is more related to factoid questions than TopiOCQA. Generating accurate responses for nonfactoid questions poses a significant challenge for PLMs in a closed-book setting. Consequently,

3.5.6. Impact of Knowledge Infusion Loss in ConvGQR
We conduct an analysis of the impact of two knowledge infusion loss functions in our
ConvGQR trying to approach the query representation to that of the relevant passage:
contrastive learning (CL) loss and mean square error (MSE) loss. They correspond to
Eq. 3.5.1 and Eq. 3.3.4. The difference between them is that the MSE loss only considers
positive passages hp+ while the CL loss also considers negative passages hp− for model
training as:
e(hS ·hp+ )
LCL = − log (h ·h ) ∑︁
.
e S p+ + P− e(hS ·hp− )

(3.5.1)

Table 3.8 presents the conversational search performance on the QReCC dataset when
the reformulation model is optimized using these two distinct loss functions. Interestingly,
queries trained with CL loss performed slightly worse than those optimized via MSE loss. In
most previous literature (203; 76), the CL loss usually performs better for dense retrieval
training, thus we expected similar results. The reason for the opposite result might be as
follows: since ConvGQR is mainly a generation task rather than a retrieval task, a positive
passage can provide a clear signal to instruct the right direction for the target generation,
while the additional negative passages used in CL loss only suggest the wrong directions to
avoid. Intuitively, the generation objective has only one correct optimization direction but
many wrong directions in the high dimensional latent space. This may make it difficult for
the knowledge infusion mechanism to determine the correct direction to follow, resulting in
sub-optimal queries. Note that despite the above observation, our method ConvGQR trained
with CL loss still outperforms most of the existing baselines.

3.5.7. Comparison of Using Open and Close Source LLMs in CHIQ
In addition to evaluating open-source models, we employ the proprietary LLM, ChatGPT3.5, to isolate the specific impact of history enhancement. Table 3.9 presents the dense retrieval
performance across three TREC CAsT test sets when query reformulation is conducted via
the CHIQ-AD approach, comparing the original historical context against the enhanced
version. Our observations indicate that even a powerful model like ChatGPT-3.5 benefits
significantly from history enhancement, yielding NDCG@3 improvements of 5.4%, 4.3%, and
0.9% on CAsT-19, CAsT-20, and CAsT-21, respectively. These results suggest that despite
the superior reasoning capabilities of state-of-the-art closed-source LLMs, explicit enrichment
of the conversational context remains vital for resolving complex dependencies in multi-turn
interactions.

3.5.8. Case Study
ConvGQR. To provide a more intuitive understanding of how expansion terms influence
ConvGQR, we present two illustrative examples, a successful case and a failure case, in
Tables 3.10 and 3.11, respectively. In these scenarios, the model is evaluated on its ability to
effectively rewrite the query and synthesize a potential answer that aligns with the groundtruth human rewrite and the ground-truth response. In Table 3.10, although the model
produces the same rewritten query as the human, which solves the anaphora problem of
75

Context: (QReCC Session 2)
q1 : What are the main breeds of goat?
r1 : Abaza...Zhongwei
q2 : Tell me about boer goats.
r2 : The Boer goat is a breed of goat that was developed in South Africa in the early 1900s for
meat production. Their name is derived from the Afrikaans (Dutch) word Boer, meaning farmer.
Current Query: q3 : What breed is good for meat?
Human-Rewritten: q3∗ : What breed of goat is good for meat?
ConvGQR Reformulated Query:
qˆ3 : What breed of goat is good for meat? The Boer goat is a breed of goat that was developed in
South Africa in the early 1900s for meat production.
Relevant Passage:
p∗ : Here are some notable breeds ... Boer goats were bred in South Africa for meat ... Before
Boer goats became available in the United States in the late 1980s, Spanish goats were the standard
meat goat breed ...
Dense Score: 0.06 (Human-Rewritten)

1.00 (Ours)

Sparse Score: 0.03 (Human-Rewritten)

0.13 (Ours)

Table 3.10. A successful example illustrating the reformulated query by ConvGQR. Rewritten and expanded query are in blue and orange, respectively. The expansion terms and gold
answer are underlined and italicized in the relevant passage.
“goat” with the context, the query expansion generated by ConvGQR with the knowledge of
“Boer goat” can still improve the performance for both dense and sparse retrieval. In this
case, even though the generated answer is not a correct answer to the question, there is a
strongly similar description (underlined) that co-occurs with the right answer in the relevant
passage. This example shows a typical case where the generated answer can be highly useful
expansion terms. In Table 3.11 for failure case analysis, the generated answer cannot act as
useful expansion terms and even hurts the retrieval results. The possible reason is that the
PLM generated a redundant answer, and there are no co-occurring and semantically related
terms contained in the relevant passage. Thus, the expansion terms are harmful. This is a
case that we should improve in the future.
CHIQ. To gain a deeper insight into the underlying mechanisms and inherent limitations of
our CHIQ framework, we conduct a qualitative analysis of the enhanced dialogue history, as
illustrated in Table 3.12. This investigation highlights the complementary functions of each
enhancement prompt in refining the original context. Specifically, QD and RE primarily assist

q1 : What are the best ways to cook a turkey?

in resolving coreferences and clarifying acronyms to full names, TS helps remove irrelevant
content, PR speculates on relevant terms that may occur in the response, and HS not only
converts the conversation into plain text but also ensures that key terms from the conversation
are preserved. While prompts such as PR and RE generation generally aid in retrieval, they
may also introduce noisy terms due to the wrong fact generated by LLMs that hurt the
ranking results. Finally, we also notice that the queries generated by CHIQ-AD and CHIQ-FT
are of different styles. The first focuses on expanding more relevant terms to increase the
matching scores, while the latter queries are more concise with higher efficiency for retrieval.
Nevertheless, aggregating the output rank lists from both approaches helps refine the final
results by ranking the relevant passages higher.

3.6. Conclusion and Future Work
In this Chapter, we aim to comprehend user search intent in multi-turn conversational
interactions via developing conversational query reformulation approaches.
We first present a generative query reformulation framework, ConvGQR, which integrates
query rewriting and query expansion toward generating more effective search queries through
a new knowledge infusion mechanism. The ConvGQR framework is implemented with two
T5-base PLM and trained with relevance judgments and ground-truth answer signals in both
information retrieval task and response generation task. Then, with the emergence of LLMs,
we further propose CHIQ, an approach that leverages the basic NLP capabilities of LLMs to
enhance the quality of contextual history for improving the query reformulation performance
in terms of conversational information retrieval.
Extensive experimental results on five public datasets demonstrate the superior effectiveness of our ConvGQR and CHIQ models by comparing with SLM-based and LLM-enhanced
systems in conversational information retrieval. We also carried out detailed analyses to
78

understand the effects of each component of both approaches on the performance improvements. Besides, the investigation of LLM-enhanced query reformulation shows that instead
of simply asking an LLM to generate a search query, it is critical to design strategies to
generate different facets of enhancement in view of finding the target information. This aspect
could be further optimized in future studies, especially the pattern of input queries from the
users would also shift in Human-AI interaction scenarios, compared to the traditional ad-hoc
search.
In this Chapter, we follow the “rewrite-retrieval” paradigm, i.e., conversational query
reformulation in the literature, to tackle the conversational information retrieval task. Although it is straightforward, it is trained separately from the retriever and thus does not
directly optimize toward the downstream search task. A more tailored way is to develop a
conversational retriever that can directly handle the whole conversational session as an input
query. However, the long-tailed input requires specific context modeling for the historical
interactions, which is the target of our next Chapter.

79

Chapter 4

Historical Context Modeling for
History-Aware Conversational Dense Retrieval

4.1. Introduction
As in any conversation in natural language, queries in conversational search may involve
omissions, references to previous turns, and ambiguities (154). Consequently, a fundamental
challenge in conversational search lies in accurately deciphering the underlying information
need by modeling context-dependent queries within their broader dialogue history. As the
discussion in last Chapter, a two-stage “reformulation-retrieval” pipeline (185; 180; 215; 81;
199) is a common practice, which first trains a query reformulation model with external data
or human reformulated queries, then feeds the rewritten queries into a retriever. However,
it is required that sufficient manual annotations are available for model training, which is
difficult to obtain in practice (6; 2). Furthermore, the manually reformulated queries may not
be the best search queries because the reformulation is based on human understanding of the
query rather than on the search results. We also observe that the rewriting model is usually
trained separately from the retriever, making it difficult to optimize the whole system for the
final retrieval performance (96; 199). In particular, the learned reformulation model may not
best fit the retriever used in the subsequent step.
Another research direction (149; 216; 78; 116) tries to train an end-to-end conversational
query encoder by leveraging all the previous context information in a more tailored way. A
common approach uses all the historical queries in the session to expand the current query.
Although this expansion leads to improved results, it makes a strong assumption that all the
previous queries are related to the current one. In reality, we often observe topic switches
within a conversation session, which means the current query may not be related to the
previous ones and thus require specific historical context modeling.

Q1: What Disney movie is the Evil Queen from?

The Evil Queen ...

Fig. 4.1. Q2, Q3 and Q4 are about “Snow White”, while Q1 is about “Evil Queen”. Q2
and Q3 are judged relevant to Q4 in human judgments. Instead, our model judges Q1 and
Q3 are useful for Q4 based on their impact to improve the retrieval effectiveness of Q4.
Fig. 4.1 illustrates such an example, where the human is talking about movies and their
characters with the interactive system. This conversation involves different topics, and the
discussion shifts among them. We see that only part of the historical queries is relevant to
the last query, either by human annotators or by our model. Thus, simply leveraging all the
historical queries will inevitably inject irrelevant information into the expanded query and
result in sub-optimal queries. This fact will be experimentally confirmed in Section 4.3.2.
Intuitively, a better solution that we will implement is to use only the relevant previous turns
to expand the current query via historical context modeling. So the crucial question is: how
can we select useful information from history context for query expansion/reformulation?
A naive idea would be training a classification model based on human annotations to
identify the relevant previous queries. Unfortunately, this approach cannot be implemented
due to the fact that human annotations of query relevance are usually unavailable in practice. Such relevance labels are also unavailable in most existing datasets for conversational
search (27; 28; 6; 2). Thus, identifying relevant previous queries is a non-trivial task in the
absence of relevance labels.
Some existing studies (149; 78; 216; 96) exploit the powerful PLMs (32; 103) and use
the attention mechanism (184) to determine how strongly historical queries are related to
the current one. A common practice is to fine-tune a pre-trained dense retriever (203; 76)
82

with conversational search data. This approach would require a considerable amount of
annotated conversational search sessions, with relevance labels for previous queries. To this
end, some pioneering studies (2; 116) proposed annotated conversational search sessions.
However, this gives rise to two critical issues: (1) It is difficult to produce a large number
of manual annotations in general conversation search sessions; and (2) it is questionable if
human annotations are the best supervision signal to use to train a model. In fact, when a
human annotates the relevance of a previous query, she/he would basically consider if the
two queries are semantically related, without being able to judge if expanding the query with
it would improve the search result. This latter would require deep knowledge about how
the retrieval model will use the query, which often remains opaque to a human annotator.
Therefore, a human annotator could select seemingly relevant but useless queries, while
missing some semantically less related but actually useful queries.
We first investigate how to select useful historical turns to expand the current query in
conversational search. The selection is made according to the usefulness of the previous
queries, i.e., if the latter can help improve the retrieval effectiveness of the current query.
Intuitively, this could help select more useful previous queries for the search task. As we
can see in Fig. 4.1, our system can make different judgments than a human annotator.
Concretely, Q1 is judged useful because it is about the same movie, although about a different
character. This is a piece of useful information to expand Q4. On the other hand, Q2, which
is manually judged relevant, does not provide any useful information, although being about
the same character (“Snow White”). To select useful previous turns, we are inspired by earlier
work that selects useful expansion terms or pseudo-feedback documents for query expansion
according to their impact (12; 55). Our situation is similar: we consider the previous turns
as candidates for expanding the current query, and we aim at selecting the ones that can
improve the search effectiveness. Therefore, a similar principle can be used, i.e., a selection
model is trained with a set of automatic labels generated according to whether the previous
turn can bring improvement in retrieval. Then, this trained query selector model can help
to identify whether a historical turn is necessary to be added to the current query turn as
selective expansion in test-time conversational information retrieval.
After the initial exploration, we further investigate how to desire a more desirable
approach by performing end-to-end history-aware conversational dense retrieval via training
a query encoder that incorporates only the useful conversation history (149; 216) rather
than utilizing all historical turns to reformulate the current query as the input to the model.
This is motivated by the problem of topic-switch again, which can give rise to the issue of
shortcut history dependency (78; 42), i.e., the reformulated query depends excessively on the
historical turns while neglecting the current query. The issue becomes much more severe

Fig. 4.2. Illustration of shortcut history dependency – passages addressing historical information needs p∗3 can be ranked higher than those addressing the current information need p∗4 ,
due to the noise in the reformulated query. The highly relevant passage p∗1 could serve as

when conversations are long. We illustrate this issue by an example in Fig. 4.2. Given the
current query q4 , instead of retrieving the passage p∗4 (addressing the current information
need) in top-ranked positions, the retriever ranks p∗3 (addressing historical information needs)
higher than p∗4 . To tackle the aforementioned challenge, we put forward HAConvDR, a
new History-Aware Conversational Dense Retrieval method with specific historical context
modeling, consisting of two prongs of enhancements.
The first prong is to incorporate an explicit historical context denoising mechanism into
the model training process so that the model is less affected by the noisy history while being
history-aware. Our initial exploration of evaluating the relevance of historical turns based
solely on their queries served a similar purpose but proved inherently limited. Historical
queries are frequently underspecified and fail to capture the full breadth of the conversational
context. To address this, we incorporate the passages retrieved for each historical query,
providing a more comprehensive grounding to accurately assess the intent of previous turns.
We then retain the relevant historical turns for context-denoised history modeling. The second
prong is to mine additional supervision signals to further alleviate the pitfall of shortcut
history dependency. Despite having context-denoised history for query expansion, a single
84

ground-truth passage (given by the dataset) is often indirect and insufficient to guide the
training of conversational information retrieval due to the remaining noise in the reformulated
query. Thus, mining additional supervisions, either positive (117) or negative (78), can
enhance the original supervision signal and reduce the negative impact of the distractors in
the conversation history.
Different from the aforementioned work that acquires additional supervision by human
annotation or retrieval, we mine pseudo positive and hard negative supervisions from the
conversation history based on the same relevance judgment of historical turns used for query
reformulation. Intuitively, among the top-ranked historical ground-truth passages in Fig. 4.2,
some of them can be highly relevant to the current query, which resembles the pseudo relevant
documents in Pseudo Relevance Feedback (PRF) (204), while others are less relevant and
can serve as hard negatives for training. These additional supervisions enable the model to
be aware of the usefulness or harmfulness of historical ground-truth passages and leverage
them in a history-aware contrastive learning process.
We carry out extensive experiments on two conversational search datasets, TopiOCQA
and QReCC, to test the effectiveness of the relevant query selector model for test-time
conversational search and HAConvDR in end-to-end conversational dense retrieval. The
results show that our method outperforms most existing strong baselines, demonstrating how
relevance judgments of historical turns can benefit conversational information retrieval.

4.2. Related Work
4.2.1. History Modeling in Conversational Search
Conversational search is defined as iteratively retrieving documents for users’ queries in a
multi-round dialog (27; 28). A later query may depend on the previous (historical) ones in
the session. Thus, the major challenge is to conduct history modeling to de-contextualize the
user query for search intent understanding. One research direction is to explicitly reformulate
queries. Typical methods include selecting important terms from the previous queries to
expand the current query via a term classifier (81; 185), or reformulating the query based on
a generative PLM (215; 199; 114; 129). Both methods need external data or human-rewritten
oracle queries for training. However, it is found that the manual queries may be sub-optimal
for search (199; 96). Therefore, training a query reformulation model according to manually
reformulated queries may lead to a sub-optimal solution.
85

4.2.2. Query Expansion in Conversation
For conversation search, expanding the query in the current turn based on historical
context can be viewed as a type of query expansion (39), which is an effective technique for
improving retrieval performance. The expansion terms can come from different resources,
such as user logs, pseudo-relevant feedback, and document collection. It is critical to use
appropriate terms to expand the query. However, it has been shown that the candidate
expansion terms selected by a method (e.g., pseudo-relevance feedback) are very noisy: they
could be useful, useless, or even harmful to improve the retrieval effectiveness. Therefore,
it is important to select only good terms for query expansion (12; 169). In the case of
pseudo-relevance feedback, there is no further indication or annotation about the usefulness of
an expansion term. Therefore, Cao et al. (12) proposed a method to automatically annotate
the usefulness of a candidate term by testing if incorporating it into an expanded query. If it
does, then the term is labeled useful; if it degrades the performance, then it is labeled harmful.
A similar approach has been adapted for selecting pseudo feedback documents for query
expansion (55). Besides, using entity overlap to select the history queries is another interesting
naive solution. However, previous works (179; 71) found that entities are often omitted in
later turns rather than repeated, which results in poor coverage in finding useful turns in
conversational search. In our work, we borrow the idea of pseudo-labeling to annotate the
usefulness (or relevance) of a historical query. Based on the pseudo labels, a query selection
model (selector) will be trained.

4.2.3. Conversational Dense Retriever
Conversational dense retrieval is another paradigm to achieve conversational search in
addition to conversation query reformulation. The principle is to leverage conversational search
data to fine-tune a well-trained ad-hoc retriever. Since there are limited supervision signals in
the current conversational search datasets, the dense retriever will have difficulty to identify the
relevant historical turns to enhance the current query. Previous studies (216; 96; 117; 134; 15)
usually focus on few-shot scenarios or rely on external resources, but without context denoising.
Regarding context denoising, several studies (116; 119; 131; 113) have introduced sophisticated
mechanisms to facilitate a model’s denoising capabilities through both explicit and implicit
strategies. However, these approaches generally overlook the potential of integrating historical
feedback to further refine the denoising process.
86

4.2.4. Supervision Signals in Dense Retrieval
Robinson et al. (159) demonstrate that sufficient supervision signals, either positive
or negative (especially hard negatives), are important for contrastive learning. For dense
retrieval, hard negatives are usually mined by BM25 (76) or a vanilla backbone model (203).
In the conversational scenario, Kim et al. (78) use the query reformulation model to construct
hard negatives, and Mao et al. (116) rely on human annotators to generate augmented
positives, but the amount of generated data is limited. Differently, our method leverages
additional supervision signals from the historical ground-truth passages to enhance the
model’s history-awareness (e.g., enjoying the efficiency and avoiding the harmfulness).

4.3. Relevant Query Selection and Expansion for Conversational Information Retrieval
The general assumption behind the existing conversational dense retrieval methods (180;
80; 149; 216; 96) is that all historical queries in previous turns are useful for understanding
current query. Therefore, expanding the query by all of them should yield better retrieval
results. Typically, they expand the current query by concatenating all historical queries.
Although expanding the query in this manner improves performance over the baseline, we
contend that a uniform expansion strategy is sub-optimal. On one hand, the historical queries
may be on a different topic, thus not useful to enhance the current query. This happens
especially in long sessions where the user may be shifted. On the other hand, even within
the same topic, historical queries vary in their utility for expansion. Certain turns naturally
possess a stronger semantic relationship to the current intent than others.
The above phenomena have been experimentally shown in (12) when expanding a query
in pseudo-relevance feedback. To verify if the same phenomena exist in conversational search,
we run a similar experiment to compare the following two scenarios: (1) expand the current
query with all historical queries, (2) expand it with only relevant historical queries/terms.
We aim to demonstrate that the latter strategy significantly outperforms the former in terms
of both retrieval accuracy and contextual relevance. Notice that the first method is widely
used in existing works. The second one is an idealized case because the relevance labels of
previous queries are usually unavailable. Meanwhile, within the second strategy, we want to
compare the effectiveness of token-level and turn-level expansion based on different types of
retrievers. Following (12), we identify the useful expansion queries from the history according
to whether the selected expansions can bring improvement in retrieval. We describe the
process below.

4.3.1. Generation of Pseudo Relevance Labels
Our goal is to generate gold Pseudo Relevance Labels (PRL) for each historical query/term
with respect to a given query. We assume that a relevant historical query/term should have
a positive impact when the current query is expanded to include it. The pseudo labeling is
based on the impact on retrieval results of a candidate turn/term when the latter is used to
expand the query.
The turn-level generation procedure is detailed in Algorithm 1. We begin by utilizing the
current raw query to retrieve a ranked list of documents, subsequently calculating the baseline
retrieval performance, measured by the MRR score in this instance. Then we iteratively
concatenate the query with each of the historical queries as a new reformulated query and
get the corresponding retrieval score. If the second score is higher than the initial one,
then the relevance of the historical query is labeled “positive”, otherwise, “negative”. Query
expansion is produced by concatenating the expansion query qi with the current one qn ,
i.e., q exp = qn ◦ qi (i ∈ [1, n − 1]), where ◦ means concatenation. Similarly, we can also create
term-level pseudo labels for all the terms in the historical queries.

4.3.2. Effectiveness of Selecting Useful Expansions
We assume the gold PRL as the results from an oracle classifier that can correctly separate
the useful and harmful historical expansion turns/terms. Before proposing an approach to
select useful expansion turns/terms, we first examine if the gold PRL can indeed improve
88

Fig. 4.3. Potential effectiveness of expansion methods for retrieval with different experiment
settings on TopiOCQA.
retrieval performance by using the relevant expansion turns/terms. We formulate these query
expansion forms as:
q raw = qn ,
q all = q1 ◦ · · · qi · · · ◦ qn−1 ◦ qn ,
q PRL = q1L ◦ · · · qjL · · · ◦ qn ,

i ∈ [1, n − 1],

j ∈ [1, n − 1] and Lgold
n [j] = 1.

Fig. 4.3 shows the retrieval effectiveness scores with these types of queries on the TopiOCQA dataset. We observe a significant improvement in retrieval effectiveness when queries
are expanded using the generated PRL. This improvement is consistent across both sparse
and dense retrieval frameworks: BM25 (158) and ANCE (203). Generally, incorporating
the full history for query expansion proves beneficial, yielding substantial performance gains
over the baseline raw queries. This result is consistent with the observations in previous
studies. However, when we use only relevant queries annotated by PRL, the effectiveness
is much higher. This demonstrates clearly the potential benefit of selecting the relevant
previous historical context and confirms our earlier assumption. Our results also indicate
that expansion based on individual relevant tokens is better suited for the sparse retriever,
whereas holistic turn-level expansion yields superior performance when paired with the dense
retriever The comparison between the two retrievers clearly shows the advantage of the dense
retriever ANCE. Therefore, in our experiments, we will focus on dense retrieval with relevant
historical queries as expansion units.

Given the high impact of relevant queries for expansion, our problem is to develop an
effective selection model to identify them. The principle we use is the selected expansion
query can bring improvement in retrieval and be judged as relevant for the current query
turn by a neural selection model.

4.3.3. Binary Classification of Historical Queries
The goal of the selector model is to identify if an i-th historical query qi is relevant (useful)
to the current n-th query qn . We use the pseudo-labeled queries as identified in Section 4.3.2
as training data to train a selector model. The method overview is shown in Fig. 4.4.
Selector Model.

The purpose of the selector is to determine which historical queries are

relevant (useful) for the current one. This can be done in two possible ways: as a classification
problem or as a search problem. With a classification approach, we train a classifier to classify
candidate queries into relevant/irrelevant classes. A straightforward approach is to directly
train a powerful PLM such as BERT (32) with PRL data as a selector. A potential drawback
of this approach is that a vanilla BERT model, lacking specialized fine-tuning for information
retrieval, may produce sub-optimal query embeddings (148). As an alternative, we can utilize
a search tool to calculate a matching score between the current query and potential candidates,
leveraging established retrieval signals to assess relevance. We leverage the ANCE (203)
90

dense retriever, which has already been fine-tuned by ad-hoc search data (137) to train a
new selector model. We will test both approaches in our experiments.
Weight Assignment to Training Samples.

force the selector to pay more attention to selecting the positive ones. Therefore, we assign

selector training, we can apply it to select useful expansion historical queries. According to
the relevant judgment produced by the selector, the current query turn can be expanded with
the predicted positive queries and fed into the (off-the-shelf) retriever (ANCE) to produce a
rank list. By treating each expanded query turn as a standalone input, this pipeline allows
any off-the-shelf retriever to be deployed directly. This approach eliminates the need for
specialized retraining on conversational datasets, ensuring greater architectural flexibility.

4.4. History-Aware Conversational Dense Retrieval
In the preceding section, we explored the development of an effective selection model
designed to evaluate the utility of historical queries. This model facilitates a “select-thenretrieve” paradigm, identifying the most beneficial expansion turns to enhance the current
turn’s query during test-time conversational information retrieval. In this section, we aim
to develop a history-aware conversational dense retriever (HAConvDR) in an end-to-end
training and inference manner by using the ground-truth passage from historical turns as
additional supervision signals.
The method overview is shown in Fig. 4.5. Our HAConvDR consists of three stages. The
first stage is to generate the pseudo relevance labels (PRLs) for historical turns by evaluating
whether a given turn with a query-passage pair is relevant to the current query. In the second
stage, we leverage the generated PRLs of historical turns for two purposes. The first purpose
is to use the relevant historical turns to perform context-denoised history modeling, while
the second purpose is to create additional positive and negative training pairs by leveraging
historical passages according to their PRLs. In the third stage, we utilize the identified

historical context and augmented training pairs to train a dense retriever. This dual-encoder
architecture is optimized via history-aware contrastive learning. We emphasize that, in our
framework, the conversation history serves a dual purpose: it provides essential contextual
information for the current turn and acts as a source of rich supervision signals for model
training. We describe each stage as follows.

4.4.1. From Query-Based Judgments to Query-Passages Pairs for
Pseudo Relevance Labels Generation
We first apply the pseudo-relevance labeling (PRL) framework to historical context
modeling by identifying relevant turns for context denoising. These labels serve as the
foundation for mining additional supervision signals. While the query-centric approach in
the preceding section is directly tied to the retrieval task, it is limited by its exclusive focus
on historical queries, effectively ignoring their associated retrieved passages. To fully leverage
the conversational information retrieval context, we incorporate these corresponding passages
into our selection process. Specifically, we determine if a ground-truth passage p+
i from
a previous turn i remains relevant to the current turn by evaluating the retrieval impact
when that passage and its associated query are concatenated to the current query turn. To
implement it, we replace the RankList-concat Rqi = f (qn ◦ qi ) by Rqi = f (qn ◦ qi ◦ p+
i ) for PRL
generation in Algorithm 1. Subsequently, by incorporating the historical retrieved passages
into the labeling process, the resulting PRLs partition the set of historical ground-truth

ground-truth passages are unavailable, we demonstrate that top-ranked retrieved passages
can serve as a viable substitute for supervision.

4.4.2. Context-Denoised History Modeling
Based on the derived PRLs information of historical turns, we conduct history modeling
by expanding the current query qn into a context-denoised one qnc as:
qnc = qn ◦ · · · p+
i ◦ qi · · ·

(4.4.2)

where qi and p+
i are from relevant historical turns, and ◦ denotes concatenation.
Since the expanded query contains historical passages Ph+ , a potential concern arises
regarding the length of the expanded query – it might exceed the input length limitations
of some PLMs. However, the analysis of the generated PRL statistics reveals that only
a small portion of historical turns are deemed relevant and used for query reformulation.
This indicates the practical feasibility of our approach. Nonetheless, in future work, we
will consider developing a more sophisticated mechanism to make a more strict selection of
relevant passages in Ph+ .

4.4.3. History-Aware Contrastive Learning
Contrastive learning is a prevalent approach to train dense retrievers (76). This approach
first projects queries and passages into an embedding space with dual encoders EQ and EP .
It then evaluates the relevance of any given pair of query and passage (q, p) by taking the dot
product similarity S(q, p) = EQ (q)T · EP (p). Finally, supervision signals are derived from the
positive and negative passages so that the distance between a query and a relevant passage
(positive pair) should be closer than that between the same query and an irrelevant passage
(negative pair). These supervision signals are back-propagated to train the encoders.
In a research setting, for the current query qn , the relevant passage (positive passage)
is the ground-truth passage p+
n given by the dataset. For the irrelevant passages (negative
passages), one option is to simply take the passages other than p+
n found in the same training
batch. These negative passages are referred to as in-batch negatives, here denoted as Pb− . In
addition to in-batch negatives, another commonly adopted approach is to leverage retrieved
93

hard negatives Pr− (96; 78; 76). One way to obtain such negatives is to use the top-ranked
passages retrieved with qn by an off-the-shelf retriever (e.g., BM25) after removing p+
n (if
present). Supervision signals generated from these retrieved negatives are believed to be more
meaningful than those from in-batch negatives. The power of retrieved negatives suggests
that the effectiveness of supervision signals could be heavily impacted by the quality and
quantity of the positive and negative pairs.
Given the insight that augmenting positive and negative pairs can boost retrieval performance, we propose to mine additional pairs to further enhance the contrastive learning process.
For this very purpose, the derived PRLs information of historical turns in Section 4.4.1 comes
in handy.
Intuitively, Ph+ contains historical passages from the historical turns that are deemed
relevant to qn . Although Ph+ may not directly address the information need of qn , Ph+ helps
enhance or complement qn . We believe this relationship can serve as a proxy to claim a
certain level of relevance between Ph+ and qn . Therefore, we use Ph+ as pseudo positives.
Similarly, passages in Ph− are less relevant to qn as demonstrated by the irrelevant PRLs. So
we use Ph− as additional hard negatives. More importantly, Ph− resembles retrieved negatives
Pr− in the sense that both are hard negatives that can generate more meaningful supervisions.
We refer to Ph− as historical hard negatives.
By leveraging these pseudo positives and historical hard negatives mined from the
conversation history, we upgrade traditional contrastive learning to a history-aware contrastive
learning framework. This approach allows the model to better distinguish between subtle
contextual nuances. Formally, we define the set of positive and negative passages utilized
for training as Eq. 4.4.3. The final training objective of our HAConvDR is illustrated in

4.5. Experiments
4.5.1. Experimental Setup
Datasets.

We evaluate our methods on two widely-used conversation information retrieval

datasets, the same as in the previous Chapter. The first is the TopiOCQA (2) dataset
that contains complex topic-switch phenomena within each conversational session. These
94

sessions have the potential to conceal a wealth of supervision signals in historical turns.
Another dataset is QReCC (6), where most queries in a conversational session are on the
same topic. The selection of these specific datasets ensures that we evaluate the model’s
performance across conversations with diverse intrinsic characteristics, thereby facilitating a
more comprehensive and informative analysis.
Evaluation metrics.

For an adequate comparison with previous studies, we use four

standard evaluation metrics: MRR, NDCG@3, Recall@10, and Recall@100 to evaluate the
retrieval result by adopting the pytrec_eval tool (183) for metric computation.
Baselines.

For our relevant query selector trained with PRLs, since the experimental

settings of existing works are not exactly the same as ours, it is unfair to directly compare them.
Thus, we implement several strong baselines following the existing methods (149; 216; 116)
with the conditions of only considering the historical queries in the conversational context as
Hn = {qi }n−1
i=1 . The compared baseline methods include:
(1) No expand: The query of the current turn without expansions.
(2) QuReTeC (185): A token-level expansion method to train a sequence tagger to decide
whether each term contained in the historical context should be added into the current query.
(3) All expand (216): It expands the current query with all historical turns and input to
the ANCE (203) dense retriever. This is a widely used approach and the strongest baseline
in the literature (149; 116).
(4) Rel. expand (Human): It uses only the manually determined relevant historical queries
to expand the current query. This method requires human annotations about topic (2), where
we assume two turns are related if their ground-truth topics are the same.
(5) GPT2QR (215): This model is based on a GPT-2 (153) query reformulation model
fine-tuned on QReCC dataset.
For our history-aware conversational dense retriever (HAConvDR), we extend to consider
not only the historical queries, but also their associated ground-truth passages and answers as
n−1
Hn = {qi , p+
i , ri }i=1 . We compare with two lines of conversational search approaches. The first

line (CQR) performs conversational query reformulation based on generative rewriter models
and off-the-shelf retrievers, including PLM-based GPT2QR (215), QuReTeC (185), CQESparse (96), T5QR (97), CONQRR (199), and ConvGQR (129), and LLM-based approaches
IterCQR (61), and LLM-Aided IQR (211). The second line (CDR) conducts conversational
dense retrieval based on ad-hoc search dense retrievers to learn the latent representation of
the reformulated query, including Conv-ANCE (119) using the original contrastive ranking
loss, InstructorR (68) utilizing LLMs to predict the relevance score between the session and

95

passages then conduct the training of the retriever, ConvDR (216) relying also on humanrewritten queries as supervision signals and SDRConv (78) that includes mining additional
hard negatives. The LLM-based methods employ ChatGPT or LLaMa as backbone models.
Implementation details.

Regarding the relevant query selector, we implement our

selection and retrieval modules based on BERT (32) and ANCE (203), adopting classification
and retrieval paradigms, respectively. For the training of our History-Aware Conversational
Dense Retriever (HAConvDR), we utilize ANCE as the backbone architecture. Finally,
efficient similarity search and dense retrieval are conducted using the Faiss library (69).
During HAConvDR training, we only update the parameters of the query encoder while
keeping the passage encoder frozen. The number of mined positives and negatives from
historical turns can vary across different query turns. Instead of trying to utilize all of them,
we randomly select one historical pseudo positive and one historical hard negative (along
with the top retrieved hard negative) for each training instance to strike a balance between
effectiveness and efficiency.

4.5.2. Results of Relevant Query Selector on Test-Time Conversational Information Retrieval
We first evaluate the effectiveness of test-time conversational information retrieval with
selective expansions. Table 4.1 shows the overall results using queries expanded in different
ways and submitted to dense retriever ANCE. We show three different versions of our relevant
query selector models: “BERT PRL” selects useful historical queries using BERT, “ANCE
PRL” selects the queries using ANCE, and “+ Weight Assignment” uses soft selection (i.e.,
by assigning a weight to each historical query according to their category during training).
We have the following observations:
(1) Our proposed methods with useful expansions outperform all baseline methods on both
datasets. Specifically, our best relevant expansions (Rel. expand) improve 19.1% and 20.6%
on MRR and NDCG@3 over the main competitor that expands with all historical context
(All expand) on TopiOCQA. The above result confirms that only part of the historical queries
are useful for expanding the current query, while the remaining ones are noise that would hurt
retrieval performance. Such observation is consistent with previous studies. It also outperforms
the Query Rewriter method, validating our assumption that we can reformulate the query
for conversational search, relying on useful historical queries automatically determined rather
than manually rewritten queries. In addition, our method is much more efficient: the inference
speed of our selector is hundreds of times higher than that of Query Rewriter. Besides,
the method of relevant expansions by human annotations (Rel. expand Human) is also
better than expanding all queries (All expand), though it is clearly lower than the oracle

is uncommon in QReCC: the conversations contain fewer turns (12.3 in TopiOCQA v.s. 3.6
in QReCC), and the queries in a conversation mostly revolve around the same topic (199).
However, in a more realistic setting with a longer conversation, we expect that topic-switching
in conversation would appear more often, and the utility of our approach would be more
obvious.
(3) The oracle relevant expansions by gold PRL are still far ahead of those with our
selector. To approach the upper bound, the PRL produced by the ANCE selector with
specific strategies can further improve the retrieval scores. Thus, training a better selector is
important in future work.

modeling to formulate input query and history-aware contrastive learning with mined supervision signals enhance the ranking ability of our HAConvDR. ii) Conversational dense retrieval

Moreover, we find that performance improvements are more pronounced on TopiOCQA.

greater potential for longer sessions with topic shifts. In contrast, the turns in QReCC are
usually on the same topic, and the ground-truth passages of historical turns can also properly
address the information needs of the current query. In such a situation, most previous turns
can be relevant, making it less critical to select the relevant turns. Our results demonstrate
that our approach is better at addressing this practical situation.

4.5.4. Ablation Study
Training Strategies of Relevant Query Selector.

To evaluate the performance of the

selector model, we report the precision, recall, macro F1, and accuracy of different selector
training strategies on our generated PRL data in Table 4.3. We can observe that there is no
big difference in both the retrieval and classification performance between using BERT PRL
and ANCE PRL. By exploiting the weight assignment (W.A.) strategy, all the metric scores
except the recall of the selector drop a lot. Since it obtains better search results as shown in
Table 4.1, we conjecture that the recall score of the selector is more related to the retrieval
results than precision.
Effectiveness of History-Aware Contrastive Learning.

Compared to the contrastive

learning technique in conversational dense retrieval, our proposed HAConvDR introduces
two extra components, i.e., context-denoised history modeling and history-aware contrastive
signals comprising historical pseudo positives and historical hard negatives. To assess the
effectiveness of these individual components, we conduct an ablation study and present the
99

analysis in Table 4.4. We observe that, on both datasets, removing pseudo positives can
cause a more pronounced performance degradation compared with removing hard negatives.
This suggests that, although both hard negatives and pseudo positives are useful, the latter
serves as a more effective supervision. This insight complements the currently prevalent
studies on negative mining. On the other hand, we observe the decrease is more prominent on
TopiOCQA, which is true for both removing hard negatives and pseudo positives. This can
be attributed to the prevalence of topic-switch phenomena within the sessions in TopiOCQA,
where historical supervision can and should be leveraged to boost performance, as illustrated
in our approach.

4.5.5. Analysis of Query Selector on Topic Judgment
Among a conversation, the turns with the same topic often have similar gold passages,
and thus we could assume these query turns are relevant. Here, we analyze the topic changes
brought by different judgment methods and their impacts on retrieval performance. We
follow the previous work (2) to classify the topic-switch into three types: topic shift, topic
return, and no-switch, based on topic annotation by humans or our PRL. The topic shift and
topic return mean that the last historical query is irrelevant to the current one, while the
topic return means that there are some relevant turns in history.
Judgment Analysis.

Table 4.5 shows the number of topic-switch types determined in

different ways: Human, Gold PRL, and predicted PRL by the selector. The difference is
that the humans annotate topics with concrete topic names (e.g., “Disney Movie”) for each
turn, while our PRL only indicates if two queries are relevant. We can see that the machine
judgments present more topic switches. The Gold PRL and Predicted PRL have considerable
amounts of consistent judgments as human annotators on topic shift and topic return types.
However, they do not have much overlap in the no-switch judgment. As the machine-selected
expansions perform better on search than those of humans, this suggests that some queries
on the same topic may still be useless for expanding the current query. This also indicates

that the human topic annotation might not be the best relevant indication to use to train a
