judgment methods. The number of topics in a conversation is determined by topic shift. We
can see that our PRL tends to judge more topics in TopiOCQA than in QReCC. Previous
experimental results show that the performance of our methods is much better on TopiOCQA
than QReCC. Thus, we conjecture that our approach can make more impact on conversations
with more turns, higher topic diversity, and finer-grained topics.
Impacts on Retrieval.

Fig. 4.6 shows the impact of different judgment methods on

retrieval performance by counting the successful (without shadow) and failed (with shadow)
examples. Here, a successful/failed example means its MRR score will increase/drop if we
expand only relevant history queries to the current query turn based on the PRL, compared
with taking all history queries for expansion. We find that our PRL can make more impact on
the topic-switch cases than those of no-switch. This phenomenon is not clearly observed on
the QReCC, in which the topic-switch phenomenon is less frequent. This result is consistent
with our initial intuition and previous analysis, as we might only need to expand relevant
history queries if there are several topics contained in a conversation. Another interesting
finding is that the query expansion selected via Gold PRL still leads to some failure cases,
101

Fig. 4.6. The statistics of the successful (without shadow) and failed (with shadow) retrieval
cases under different topic-switch types and relevance judgment methods.
which indicates the dependency between each expansion query might have an impact on
retrieval effectiveness, which could be further explored.

4.5.6. Investigations of Historical Supervision Signals on HistoryAware Contrastive Learning
In this section, we analyze how HAConvDR alleviates the issue of models favoring
retrieving historical turn passages over current ones by examining the effect of historical
supervision signals with quantitative and qualitative analysis.
Quantitative Analysis.

The quantitative analysis is presented in Fig. 4.7, which shows

the percentage of the queries that rank the historical ground-truth passages higher than those
of the current turn. We observe that our model can decrease the percentage of irrelevant
historical gold passages for TopiOCQA, but not much for QReCC. It indicates that the
supervision signals for history-aware contrastive learning are stronger in TopiOCQA than in
QReCC, and it is consistent with the observation in Section 4.5.3 that the improvements in
TopiOCQA are more obvious.
Qualitative Analysis.

To gain more insights into our approach, we did a qualitative

study to visualize an example in the embedding space as Fig. 4.8, which shows T-SNE
visualization (182) to compare the ANCE dense retriever with and without HAConvDR
training in the embedding space. In contrast to the vanilla ANCE, which is unsuccessful
102

Fig. 4.7. The percentage of the queries whose retrieved list has the ground-truth passage of
the historical turns ranked higher than its own.

in distinguishing the gold passage from the ground-truth of the historical turns, the ANCE
backbone trained with our HAConvDR exhibits a stronger ability to differentiate it from
the distractors. Besides, our model can also discriminate the gold passages of relevant and
irrelevant turns, showing the effectiveness of these supervision signals toward better search
results. The corresponding example is provided in Appendix C.

4.5.7. Impact of Substituting Historical Ground-Truth Passages
The computation of PRLs for historical turns relies on having access to historical groundn−1
truth passages {p+
i }i=1 . In many real-world applications, identifying ground-truth passages

can be accomplished by analyzing user clicks, engagement, and feedback. However, we
acknowledge that there are applications where historical ground-truth passages are difficult
to obtain. In such cases, we can use top-retrieved passages as a substitute. This simple
substitution allows us to perform the proposed approach described in Section 4.4.1 with
only minor modifications. Specifically, in Alg. 1 and Eq. 4.4.2, the relevant passage p+
i is
approximated by the concatenation of the top-k retrieved passages for qi , where k is a hyperparameter. This retrieval is completed with the same backbone model of the conversational
dense retriever. Meanwhile, Pn− in Eq. 4.4.3 degrades to the combination of Pb− ∪ Pr− . The
rest of the approach is kept as is.
103

Fig. 4.8. T-SNE visualization of query, ground-truth passage, pseudo positives, and history
hard negatives embeddings via two ANCE models with and without HAConvDR training.
We conduct an ablation study to verify the effectiveness of our approach under this
adaptation, with results presented in Table 4.7. We see that the PRL information still
contributes to the retrieval performance of the reformulated query, further indicating its
effectiveness. Besides, we find model performance degrades as k increases, suggesting that
longer contexts are more likely to contain noise, which cannot be entirely compensated by
our approach. This suggests the potential for more advanced context-denoising approaches.
Finally, we find that using the full model with history-aware contrastive learning under the
adapted setting continues to yield better results on top-ranking positions and outperforms
most existing systems in Table 4.2.

4.6. Conclusion and Future Work
In this Chapter, we focus on historical context modeling for developing history-aware
conversational dense retrieval. The common assumption is that not all historical turns are
related to the current one, so we should incorporate the useful portion instead of all to
avoid injecting noise and resulting in a performance degradation. We first investigated the
problem of query expansion in conversational information retrieval. We proposed a selection
model to identify useful historical queries for query expansion. The model was trained
using pseudo-relevance labels obtained automatically according to the impact of a candidate
expansion query on retrieval effectiveness. We showed in our experiments that such a selection
model can determine useful queries that perform even better than human-annotated queries.

The study demonstrates that we can learn to select useful historical queries automatically
without human annotation.
Following the same pseudo-relevance labeling principle, we extend the query-based judgments into considering query-passages pairs and facilitate the scenarios with conversational
dense retrieval, rather than only applying the query selector model at test-time conversational
information retrieval. We present a new history-aware contrastive learning strategy for
conversational dense retriever training, HAConvDR, which is based on context-denoised query
reformulation and additional supervision signals mining from historical turns. Extensive
experimental results on public datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our model. Furthermore, we conduct comprehensive analyses to gain insights into the impact of each component
of HAConvDR on enhancing search performance and provide valuable insights on how they
can work well for conversations with topic shifts.
While the preceding chapters addressed conversational information retrieval, specifically
the challenge of identifying relevant information for context-dependent queries. We now shift
our focus to the synthesis of final responses for the users. Building upon the performance
gains of our proposed retrieval strategies, the next objective is to investigate how to leverage
retrieved knowledge and historical context to generate coherent answers. In the LLM era,
this conversational response generation task represents the second fundamental pillar of the
research of conversational search.

105

Chapter 5

Boosting Conversational Retrieval-Augmented
Generation by Leveraging Historical
Information

5.1. Introduction
Multi-turn interactions between users and information-seeking systems provide a framework for satisfying complex information needs, a capability driven by recent advancements
in conversational search (77; 46; 221; 128). In a typical conversational search pipeline, a
retriever first identifies relevant evidence from external knowledge sources, after which a
generator synthesizes a precise response grounded in the retrieved results. While previous
research, including our explorations in the preceding chapters, has significantly enhanced
conversational information retrieval by modeling user intent through context-dependent
queries (185; 96; 216; 199; 119), then the next question is how to optimize conversational
response generation by effectively leveraging rich historical context, which remains underexplored in the literature. In this Chapter, we aim to address this challenge.
The rapid evolution of Large Language Models (LLMs) has fundamentally transformed user
information-seeking behavior, shifting from one-shot ad-hoc searches to multi-turn interactive
dialogues that culminate in response generation (4). To mitigate the risk of LLMs generating
plausible but incorrect responses, particularly when their parametric knowledge is insufficient
or outdated, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has emerged as an intuitive and robust
solution (88). However, current methodologies (33; 42; 213; 142) primarily adapt single-turn
RAG pipelines to conversational settings by first reformulating the context-dependent query of
the current turn and then utilizing its specific retrieved results for generation. This paradigm
often fails to effectively integrate longitudinal historical data, such as previous query-response
pairs, their associated evidence, and implicit turn dependencies. As illustrated in Fig. 5.1,

Convert to single-turn RAG pipeline

Information in multi-turn scenario for RAG

!! : Where is 2022 World
Cup take place?

!# : Who plays the
first game?

"!∗ : The 2022 FIFA World

Fig. 5.1. Illustration of the available historical information, e.g., turn dependency and
historical retrieved results, within multi-turn scenarios (right), which are overlooked in the
existing single-turn RAG pipeline (left). The qn , Pn , and rn∗ denote the query, top-k retrieved
passages, and the ground-truth response of turn n.

this abundance of historical context is crucial for synthesizing an optimal response in the
current turn. The assumptions behind are i) some top-ranked historical retrieved evidence
might be highly relevant to the current query (133), serving similarly as the pseudo-relevant
documents in pseudo relevance feedback (204; 133), but are not retrieved or top-ranked for the
current turn (78); and ii) the context-dependent query in conversation is usually ambiguous
and complex even after reformulation, which requires de-noising the retrieved context for
generating high-quality responses (13; 19).
However, leveraging historical information for response generation is non-trivial, as it
requires isolating salient context from noisy, long-tailed conversations while simultaneously
meeting the stringent efficiency requirements of real-time content generation. For example,
incorporating all historical information for the current turn is infeasible since it might surpass
the constraints of model context input, require high-cost computing resources, or raise the risk
of injecting additional noise. Even with the advance of long-context LLMs, exploiting accurate
historical information for LLM-based generation is still necessary for better performance,
which is proven in previous studies (218; 205; 147; 86). Our investigation further supports
this conclusion in conversational settings as presented in Section 5.3.2. Therefore, it is crucial
to explore how to effectively and efficiently leverage useful historical information to enhance
response generation in conversational settings.
To address the aforementioned issues, we design various strategies to improve the performance of retrieval-augmented response generation by leveraging historical information in
conversations, addressing the following research questions:
• RQ1: How feasible is it to leverage historical information to boost the performance
of the generated response for the current turn?
108

• RQ2: What is the best practice to identify and leverage various useful historical
information for the current turn’s response generation?
• RQ3: Why can the information from historical turns contribute to the response
generation of the current turn?
To address these inquiries, we first conduct a preliminary test to verify the feasibility of
leveraging different retrieved evidence from historical turns, aiming to confirm our conjecture
by analyzing their effectiveness. Then, motivated by our observations, we propose different
strategies to boost the response generation performance from different aspects, including
integrating the search results, capturing and leveraging the turn dependency, and identifying useful historical information. The various strategies could be used individually or
in combination. The principle is to decide how to leverage the historical information for
the current turn’s RAG. Experimental results on three public benchmarks demonstrate the
effectiveness of our approach, showing that integrating abundant historical information into
conversational RAG leads to superior performance compared to existing single-turn RAG
systems. Specifically, our method consistently outperforms previous strong baselines across
different settings, achieving approximately a 10% absolute improvement over the second-best
approach. In addition, we further analyze the potential principle of using various historical
information, helping to understand the behind-the-scenes behavior of the models.

5.2. Background: From Conversational IR to Conversational Response Generation
Conversational search systems with a flexible interface enable multi-turn interactions with
users to satisfy their complex information needs, where information retrieval and retrievalaugmented response generation are two key components. Besides, the transition between
them would highly impact the system’s performance.

5.2.1. Conversational Information Retrieval
A primary challenge in conversational information retrieval is the accurate interpretation
of a user’s true search intent, which is often obscured within context-dependent queries. The
quality of these initial retrieval results is critical, as it directly dictates the effectiveness and
accuracy of the subsequent conversational response generation. Two main research lines in
the literature have been developed to achieve conversational information retrieval, including
conversational query reformulation (CQR) and conversational dense retrieval (CDR). With
the emergence of LLMs, recent methods adapt LLMs to directly generate the reformulated
queries for retrieval (211; 114) or to generate high-quality pseudo queries as supervision
109

signals for reformulation model training (61; 214; 227; 127; 83). Parallel to CQR, the CDR
approach (149; 96; 78; 119; 68; 113; 132; 107) learns to map search intent and documents
into a shared latent space by utilizing conversation-document relevance as a supervision
signal. However, the CDR paradigm is heavily dependent on the availability of high-quality
relevance judgments (26; 117; 134; 15). Given that conversational search systems are not yet
ubiquitous, the resulting data scarcity remains a primary bottleneck for training robust CDR
models.

5.2.2. Conversational Response Generation
From another perspective, in the domain of conversational systems, the most prominent
framework for response generation is Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) (88), which
traditionally focuses on crafting responses for single-turn queries using retrieved external
knowledge. Existing literature has extensively explored various facets of RAG, such as
improving response accuracy (65; 220), mitigating hallucinations (165; 170), enhancing the
reasoning capabilities of LLMs (8; 73), and fine-tuning models with external corpora (98; 118).
However, these efforts primarily address isolated queries and largely overlook the rich historical
context inherent in multi-turn conversations. Some earlier studies (230; 123; 156) attempted
to select the knowledge provided in previous dialogue content to improve the generation for the
current turn, but the candidate knowledge is provided in a limited pool rather than an openretrieval setting (149), which is impractical. Although some recent studies (142; 213; 160; 125)
attempt to investigate the response generation within the conversational scenario, they still
simply adapt the single-turn RAG pipeline by only leveraging the reformulated query of the
current turn and its associated retrieved ranking list for response generation, which overlooks
some useful historical information. Unlike them, our studies focus on the transition from
Conversational IR to RAG, aiming to explore leveraging useful historical information to
boost RAG in conversations, which incorporates more effective information and filters out
the potential noise for answer generation.

5.2.3. Historical Information Processing and Management in Conversational RAG
Historical information is indispensable for conversational systems, as it provides the
necessary grounding to interpret the current turn’s intent. Traditional retrieval models
often process queries in isolation, an approach that suffers from significant inefficiencies
in long-term context retention (54; 131). To mitigate this, recent research has focused on
equipping LLMs with sophisticated memory mechanisms designed to manage and utilize historical context across extended interactions (111). Various memory-augmented architectures
110

have emerged (53; 5), which explicitly store, retrieve, and update task-specific information
over multiple turns. Specifically, techniques such as retrieval-based memory (205; 87) and
parametric memory adaptation (234; 146) allow models to access relevant past interactions
without violating context window constraints. Building on these advancements, this study
investigates the utility of historical information within conversational RAG systems and
proposes novel mechanisms to optimize its integration.

5.3. Motivation
5.3.1. Task Definition of Conversational RAG
In the task of conversational RAG, a conversational session could contain the current
n−1
query qn , and n − 1 associated historical turns Hn = {qi , Pi , ri }n−1
i=1 , where qi , {P i }i=1 and

ri denote the user queries, search results and generated response of each preceding turns,
respectively. The conversational search system requires to retrieve top-k relevant passages
Pn = {ptn }kt=1 from a large collection C, then apply a mechanism M to manipulate the input
for the generator model as GnIN = M(qn , Pn , Hn ). We expect the performance of the final
generated response could be better by leveraging the useful explicit context and implicit
dependency among the historical information Hn .

5.3.2. Validating Effectiveness of Historical Search Results
To address RQ1, we begin with preliminary tests to validate the effectiveness of historical
search results for conversational RAG. We conduct experiments under both “oracle” and
“practical” settings, comparing the quality of responses generated using only current turn
search results against those incorporating historical search results. Furthermore, we analyze a
“mixed” setting to evaluate the model’s context denoising capabilities and to demonstrate the
necessity of such mechanisms. These experiments are performed across three conversational
search benchmarks using Claude-Sonne-3.5, a state-of-the-art LLM with extensive longcontext capacity. Performance is assessed using the F1-score, a standard metric in the field.
The overall results are shown in Table 5.1.
Evaluation on Oracle Setting.

The oracle setting assumes the gold evidence would

be retrieved by an oracle retriever for each query turn, i.e., the response is generated by
an LLM with the supportive information provided in the gold evidence. From the result
presented in Table 5.1, we observe that incorporating gold evidence from historical turns into
the prompt, alongside the user query, enhances the quality of the responses generated by the
LLM. Furthermore, incorporating evidence from a broader window of historical turns yields
consistent and incremental performance improvements.
111

Evaluation on Practical Setting

For a practical setting, only the retrieved top-k ranking

list is available for the response generation rather than the gold ones. As present in Table 5.1,
incorporating historical retrieved evidence (+ historical top-3) achieves better performance
than the single-turn RAG that retrieves evidence from the current turn only (only current
top-k). Although using a larger k in the retrieved ranking list for the current turn may
slightly enhance response generation performance, it still falls short compared to incorporating
historical retrieved evidence and incurs higher latency.
Evaluation on Mixed Setting

For a mixed setting, we combine the previous two settings

by replacing the top-1 rank position with the gold evidence and keeping the remaining
retrieved evidence in the ranking list. The goal is to evaluate the ability of the generator
model to denoise context, which can be regarded as an assessment of long-context LLM
performance. Table 5.1 shows that although the gold evidence is ranked in the top position,
the generation performance keeps dropping as the k becomes larger. The results indicate that
the longer input context might be challenging for the generator to deal with, even though the
gold evidence is included. This observation is the same as the previous studies (205; 218)
that the existing LLMs supported with longer input context windows for language modeling
might not always deal with long context de-noise in the downstream tasks. Thus, filtering the
noise within the input context before generation is necessary, especially under conversational
scenarios with abundant available long context.
Overall, the potential impact of leveraging historical information for current turn response
generation in the above observations confirms our conjecture for RQ1. Then, the problem is
how to use the historical information (RQ2) and understand why they can contribute to the
conversational RAG (RQ3).

5.4. Leveraging Historical Information for Conversational Retrieval-Augmented Generation
While historical retrieved evidence often contains information vital to current turn’s response generation, incorporating data from all preceding turns becomes increasingly infeasible
as a conversation progresses. This limitation arises from both efficiency constraints and the
critical need for context denoising within Hn . Consequently, effective conversational context
modeling is essential to identify high-utility information while filtering irrelevant noise. To
address these challenges, we design three strategies that leverage the advanced capabilities
of LLMs to optimize response generation. These strategies focus on different functional
aspects: integrating search results (Section 5.4.1), modeling and leveraging the dependency
of historical turns (Section 5.4.2), and identifying pertinent evidence within the historical

5.4.1. Search Results Integration
Some useful information might be contained in top-ranked historical retrieved passages,
but may fail to be retrieved or not be top-ranked for the current turn. Thus, the search
results from historical turns could be supplementary to the retrieved set used for the response
generation of the current turn. Formally, given the retrieved passages from the current

5.4.2. Historical Turns Dependency
Prior studies (116; 131) indicate that the indiscriminate inclusion of all historical context
can introduce significant noise into the model. Furthermore, our observations in Table 5.1
confirm that adapting a single-turn RAG pipeline without specialized conversational context modeling is sub-optimal. To address this, we propose a strategy that first identifies
dependencies between historical turns before utilizing them for response generation. This
approach also mitigates system latency by selectively retaining only the queries relevant to the
captured turn dependencies. Concretely, we instruct the LLMs with I Judge to interactively
determine whether a given query pair shares similar information needs between the current
(n-th) turn with each associated historical turn and eventually obtain a binary judgment
list Jn as Eq. 5.4.3. Then, we explicitly select which historical turns would be used for the
current turn’s response generation based on the turn dependency judgment results from J
with two different strategies as shown in Eq. 5.4.4.
Jn ← I Judge (qn , qi ),

Specifically, the Dep-Hard strategy retains only the turn with similar information
needs (131), while Dep-Soft is designed to start retention from the first turn judged as
being with similar information needs. The Dep-Soft allows more flexible turn dependency by
maintaining transitions across consecutive turns. By applying the specific selection mechanism
MDep on the historical information Hn to produce the input context based on the judgment
list Jn as Eq. 5.4.5, we expect to reduce the noise contained in the input of generator GnIN

5.4.3. Historical Evidence Identification
Evidence identification is widely used in single-turn RAG systems (48), aiming to increase
the credibility of the generation results and arouse the self-check ability of LLMs to obtain
a more accurate answer (8). Inheriting a similar idea, we design the historical evidence
identification (HEI) strategy to not only provide the answer but also indicate the referenced
historical retrieved passages. Specifically, by instructing the LLMs with I HEI , the output of
current (n-th) turn should contain two parts of information, the response rn for the query qn
and the grounded passages Pnindex to rn as Eq. 5.4.6.
rn = M

(5.4.6)

This strategy further enables us to scrutinize the implicit conversational modeling capabilities of LLMs by forcing an explicit focus on historical context. Specifically, we evaluate
the model’s ability to discern utility across historical turns by comparing the index of selected passages Pnindex with the binary judgment list Jn from Eq. 5.4.3. This allows for a
rigorous assessment of information identification beyond mere sequence modeling of historical
query-response pairs.

5.4.4. Hybrid Strategy by Integration
The aforementioned strategies are designed independently. An intuitive approach is
to integrate them into a hybrid strategy, which includes all three strategies with all their
advantages to leverage historical information in a predefined order. Specifically, the used
LLM first judges the relevant historical turn based on dependency as MDep . Then, the
LLM is instructed to identify the historical evidence by MHEI from the turns judged as
relevant. Finally, the identified historical evidence together with the retrieved results from
the current turn are delivered to MSRI for search results integration. The selected historical
information in this hybrid approach is used as the input of generator GnIN for the current n-th
turn generation, where we expect each aspect could be integrated to boost the generation
quality. While the extended input length and multi-stage LLM processing could potentially
increase inference latency, we mitigate these costs by consolidating all three instructions into
a single LLM call. By processing the original input (qn , Pn , Hn ) in one pass, we significantly
reduce the computational overhead and total inference time. The whole procedure could be
115

formulated as

5.5. Experimental Setup
5.5.1. Datasets
We evaluate our methods on three widely-used conversational search benchmarks, including
TopiOCQA (2), QReCC (6), and OR-QuAC (149). Each of them contains the ground-truth
for both passage retrieval and response generation and thus is suitable for evaluation. The
TopiOCQA has the longest conversation, and the average turn is 12.9, while the other two
are relatively shorter. Besides, one of the main differences among them is the average number
of passages per conversation, which are 9.0, 1.6, and 1.0 with respect to TopiOCQA, QReCC,
and OR-QuAC. It reveals that in QReCC and OR-QuAC, most conversations only involve
one topic/passage. Thus, the TopiOCQA is much more practical than the others.

5.5.2. Evaluation Metrics
The evaluation metrics contain two parts with respect to the retrieval task and the
generation task. For conversational information retrieval, we deploy the NDCG@3, Recall@3,
and Precision@3 for top-ranked results (27). For evaluating response generation, we employ
F1, Accuracy (Acc.), and LLM-EM following the previous studies (62; 8; 142).

5.5.3. Baselines
We compare our methods with a variety of prompt-based approaches that can mainly be
classified into three categories. More precisely, the first group is LLM-based reasoning methods
with parametric knowledge only, including Zero-Shot (ZS), Chain-of-Thought (CoT) (195),
and Tree-of-Thought (ToT) (209). The ZS directly instructs the used LLM to generate a
response for the user query with the associated preceding question-response pairs, while the
CoT and ToT provide additional guidance to enable the model to generate the thought chain
before the final response. The second group leverages external knowledge by integrating
the single-turn retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) component on top of the previous
systems, i.e., only using the retrieved evidence from the current turn, including vanilla
RAG (88), Self-Reflection (SR) (213), and Reasoning and Action (ReAct) (210). The vanilla
RAG incorporates retrieved evidence for the current turn for response generation, while the
Self-Reflection and ReAct perform self-correction and reasoning to leverage the search results,
respectively. The third group leverages historical information, including query-response pairs
116

and retrieved evidence in previous turns, to conduct response generation for the current turn.
This is our target and main competitors in terms of our designed strategies. Among them,
the Conversational Chain-of-Action (CCoA) (142) relies on a dynamic reasoning-retrieval
mechanism that extracts the intent of the question and decomposes it into a reasoning chain
with an updated contextual knowledge set. The Base and Oracle serve as our baseline
and the ideal situation of our proposed methods corresponding to the practical and oracle
settings in Section 5.3.2, respectively. Note that the Base can be considered as a longcontext scenario for LLM, which directly takes all the information as model input without
specific identification/de-noising. This approach aims to test whether the existing LLM can
implicitly leverage the useful historical information in the input, with the assumption that
the sophisticated LLM has long-context modeling capacity.

5.5.4. Implementation Details
We deploy ANCE retriever for searching relevant passages (203) and Claude-Sonnet-3.5
LLM as the generator to obtain the final response. The retrieval evaluation metrics are
implemented from the pytrec_eval tool (183) based on Faiss (69) libraries. The lengths
of the query, concatenated input, and passage are truncated to 32, 512, and 384 tokens for
the retriever, respectively. For response generation, the evaluation metrics F1, Accuracy,
and LLM-EM are implemented following the code released by (149), (8), and (142). Besides,
we limit the generation length to 128 with a temperature of 0.1 to ensure the generation
stability. All the experiments are conducted on an NVIDIA V100 32G GPU. For each of our
proposed strategies to manipulate the input for generation, we still use Claude-Sonnet-3.5 as
the backbone model and investigate the other LLMs in ablation studies. For our strategy of
search results integration (SRI), we additionally implement monoT5 (139) as a small language
model approach for comparison. To make the baseline methods directly comparable, we
follow the evaluation protocol from (142) to implement the compared systems with the same
instruction by using GPT-4o as evaluator. All used prompts can be found in Appendix B.

5.6. Experimental Results
In this section, we address RQ2 by investigating best practices for identifying and
leveraging useful historical information to guide response generation. We evaluate our
proposed strategies through a rigorous comparative analysis against existing state-of-the-art
methods and conduct a detailed ablation study of each approach.

5.6.1. Main Comparison
Table 5.2 shows the comparison of the results between our proposed methods and previous
studies on three conversational datasets in terms of response generation. First, we observe
that leveraging the historical information within conversational RAG scenarios consistently
outperforms both the single-turn RAG-based and the reasoning-based approaches. Among
the conversational RAG systems, our Base method achieves absolute gains of 3.7%, 2.6%,
and 0.3% over the previous best system, ConvCoA, on each dataset. Our proposed strategies,
SRI, Dep, and HEI, significantly improve performance on top of it. In addition, combining
them can result in a further performance boost. The strong effectiveness is attributed to the
sophisticated strategies to leverage historical information, and demonstrate that our principle
to identify useful historical retrieved evidence can boost the RAG in conversations. The
impact of each strategy is provided in the following sections (Section 5.6.2, Section 5.6.3, and
Section 5.6.4). Overall, the HEI strategy achieves the strongest performance among the three
approaches. This suggests that modeling turn dependency as implicit historical context is
more effective than relying on the explicit historical search results utilized in the SRI strategy.
Furthermore, we observe that while leveraging reasoning frameworks (e.g., CoT and
ToT) and integrating external knowledge from the current turn (RAG, SR, and ReAct)
enhances response quality, the failure to account for multi-turn historical context remains a
significant bottleneck. These findings underscore the necessity of incorporating high-utility
historical search results and turn dependencies to optimize current turn generation. Finally,
the persistent performance gap between our current results and the Oracle baseline suggests
that more sophisticated strategies, such as training models specifically to discern the utility
of historical information, are warranted, a direction we leave for future exploration.

Table 5.3 illustrates the performance of our Search Results Integration (SRI) strategy
across three conversational search datasets. The primary objective of this analysis is to
evaluate the correlation between retrieval quality and final response generation. As shown,
our Base method, which utilizes historical search results, consistently outperforms Vanilla
RAG (relying solely on the current turn’s search results). This validates the utility of
historical retrieved information for grounding current turn responses. Furthermore, the
precise integration of historical evidence through SRI yields additional gains in retrieval
effectiveness, subsequently boosting generation quality. This improvement is most pronounced
in the QReCC dataset, likely due to its topic consistency. In contrast, the high frequency
of topic-switching in TopiOCQA makes search intent detection more challenging. Finally,
the fact that LLM-based SRI outperforms monoT5 suggests that higher model capacity is
essential for complex evidence integration.
The effectiveness of retrieval for RAG is still an open question in the community (162),
as superior retrieval results do not always guarantee higher-quality response generation.
Nevertheless, our findings indicate a positive correlation between retrieval and generation
efficacy. Specifically, we observe that enhancing retrieval performance consistently supports
the quality of conversational response generation, even if the marginal gains are modest
in certain scenarios. Consequently, we leave the development of a more robust alignment
strategy, specifically one that leverages performance signals from the generation stage to
refine the retrieval integration process, for future work.

5.6.3. Impact of Historical Turns Dependency
Effectiveness and Efficiency.

Table 5.4 shows the performance of leveraging the turn

dependency judgment results for effectiveness and efficiency. We can observe that both
hard and soft strategies can reduce the latency cost, i.e., lower average input token numbers
(Avg. |Token|) per turn for the generator model. The Dep-soft that maintains more implicit
turn dependency can obtain better effectiveness and even outperform the Base approach by
eliminating the noise, while the Dep-hard might result in a degradation due to the information
loss with strict restriction. The results imply that the judgment for turn dependency from LLM
might not exactly be accurate, but can still help in achieving an effectiveness and efficiency
trade-off. This trade-off is important in the RAG task because of the context-denoising
requirement, especially when the input context is usually much longer in conversational
scenarios, as the corresponding analysis provided in Section 5.7.4. Overall, although the
implicit transition among historical turns is still hard to capture, it is a crucial factor for
response generation performance improvement with appropriate adaptation.

5.7. Results Analysis
In this section, the goal is to analyze and understand how the retrieved and dependency
information from historical turns contribute to the response generation of the current turn,
as illustrated in RQ3.

5.7.1. Impact of Statistics of Ranking List
We first investigate why historical retrieved results can contribute to current turn response
generation from the aspect of the information in the ranking list, since the final performance
of conversational RAG might highly depend on the search results. The statistical results are
shown in Table 5.7, where the information of “Bottom Hit” denotes that the gold evidence
of the current turn is ranked beyond top-5 in the top-k ranking list, while “History Hit”
indicates that the gold evidence of the current turn has occurred in any previous turns’ top-3

These two statistics show the correlation between the rank position of gold evidence and

the historical top retrieved results could act as enhancement factors to supply the evidence
used for boosting response generation. We observe that after applying our SRI strategy to
integrate the historical and current search results, the “Bottom Hit” and “History Hit” are
reduced and improved, respectively. The “Enhancement” has also been improved, which
means the frequency of the gold evidence in the input has increased. These results imply the
utility of historical search results by supplying more useful information in the current turn’s
response generation to boost the final performance.

5.7.2. Impact of Historical Turns and Noisy Context
In this experiment, we study the impact of the historical turns and the associated noisy
context effect on various strategies. We use their per-turn F1 score for response generation
evaluation. As shown in Fig. 5.2, the performance of vanilla RAG keeps dropping as the
conversation diving in and consistently underperforms our proposed methods. This is because,
as the conversation becomes longer, the difficulty of context modeling increases with much
more contained noise, while the vanilla RAG lacks a specific design for denoising the used
historical information. Among our approaches, different strategies can keep an even trend
as the historical turns increase, and the three specifically designed strategies are generally
better than the base method. These observations could be attributed to the effectiveness of
leveraging historical search results and the information of turn dependency modeling. Besides,
various performances are observed on the different datasets due to the original features of
each dataset.

5.7.3. Impact of Different Backbone Models
We evaluate the response generalization effect of our proposed methods based on different
types of LLMs, including open-source ones with various sizes and commercial ones. Table 5.8
shows the performance of various LLMs based on whether leveraging historical information,
i.e., using top-20 retrieved passages of only current turn as vanilla RAG or our base method
with top-3 retrieved passages of each historical turn and current turn.We observe that our
method to leverage historical information outperforms the vanilla RAG paradigm across
various backbone models, indicating the effectiveness of historical information in boosting
response generation in conversations and the robustness of our strategies.

5.7.4. Trade-off Analysis of Efficiency and Effectiveness
We then supply the impact of the token numbers of the input context for efficiency
analysis. Fig. 5.3 shows the average input token numbers for the LLMs across various turns.
We can see that when applying our Base method, which leverages historical search results
with a basic strategy, the average input token numbers increase quickly as the conversation
dives in. Although it improves performance, as shown in Fig. 5.2, its efficiency decreases due
to the increased latency required to generate outputs conditioned on the longer input context
In contrast, our designed Dep-soft strategy can reduce the inference cost and keep good
effectiveness at the same time. This experimental analysis suggests that a better effectiveness
and efficiency trade-off strategy is important and desirable in conversational RAG tasks.

5.8. Conclusion and Future Work
In this Chapter, we provide an exploration of boosting retrieval-augmented response
generation in conversations by leveraging various historical information. We verify its
feasibility by preliminary experiments and further propose different strategies to incorporate
historical search results and turn dependency, and grounding with the evidence to improve
the quality of the generated response from different aspects. These strategies can be used
alone or in combination for enhancement, depending on the requirements of the practical
situation. Besides, these strategies can be generalized to other domain-specific conversational
systems with information-seeking goals. Experimental results on three public benchmarks
demonstrate the effectiveness of our methods by outperforming various types of existing
systems. The thorough analysis of our approaches and observations reveals the potential
principles and effectiveness of leveraging historical information in conversational RAG tasks.
125

As an initial exploration, our methods are prompting-based relying on the capacity of
LLMs in a training-free fashion. However, the prompts might not generalize to different
backbone models of various sizes. Besides, the output of LLMs is not controllable during
the decoding phrase and they might not truly understand how to leverage the historical
information and the search results from different aspects. Thus, future studies could explore
training-based methods by using suitable external supervision signals to leverage different
information integrated in the prompt input template, which is helpful to guide the generator
to accurately identify the useful information.

126

Chapter 6

Open Large Language Model Decoding to
Incorporate Document Quality in
Retrieval-Augmented Generation

6.1. Introduction
In Chapter 5, we explore how to enable large language models (LLMs) to leverage
historical information and the retrieved knowledge to boost conversational response generation
task. Although showing effectiveness, the improvement relies on the original capacity of
LLMs (176; 229; 1) via their parametric knowledge acquisition from the training documents.
However, LLMs still encounter foundational problems, such as understanding the limits of
their knowledge and capability (56; 191), where a lack of sufficient knowledge might lead to
hallucinations or generating outdated results (63; 90). In this Chapter, we aim to develop
a training-based approach to guide the LLMs to accurately identify the useful information
during the content generation procedure.
In the literature, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) (88) is a common practice to
address the aforementioned incomplete knowledge issue by incorporating external information
to obtain more accurate and reliable content generation. Despite the fact that the RAG
technique alleviates the knowledge boundary issue of LLMs, existing approaches to RAG
face fundamental challenges: the quality of generated content heavily relies on the usefulness
of the retrieved information and the capacity of LLMs’ internal information processing
mechanism (99; 171). It is generally assumed that the retrieved information is relevant
and useful for content generation, or LLMs have the capability to judge its relevance.
However, the existing literature (37) showed the vulnerability of automated usefulnesschecking systems when confronted with noisy information. Thus, the defective and imperfect
retrieved information would degrade the performance of LLMs. As a matter of fact, when

an LLM is asked to answer a question based on an irrelevant document, the quality of the
answer is negatively affected (178). Such a situation with irrelevant information may often
occur when RAG is asked to deal with a large variety of questions. An ideal RAG system
should be able to understand and tolerate the noisy input, i.e., process the diverse inputs
that include useful evidence and irrelevant information, without being affected by the noise
and resulting in significant degradation in performance (233; 168). For example, if the input
context is partially noisy or extremely irrelevant, the system can attend only to the useful
part or ignore the whole misinformation when generating an answer.
Existing studies attempt to address this issue from various perspectives, which can be
categorized into (i) workflow-based methods and (ii) fine-tuning-based methods. The first
category aims to design a workflow that navigates LLMs to identify useful pieces from
retrieved information and append them to the final input context for generation. The
intermediate steps in the workflow vary and may include self-correction through LLM-asa-judge (212; 50), isolating individual results for later aggregation (201), and step-by-step
filtering via reasoning (14), among others. This training-free approach is highly sensitive to the
used prompt template and follows the strong assumption that the model could have enough
capacity to distinguish the useful information by following the instruction to produce ideal
output (56). However, one cannot expect that LLMs always generate correct judgments, and
thus the manipulated final input might lose crucial information or include wrong information
before conducting answer generation (218). Besides, the judgment workflow with multiple
steps with LLM calling would significantly increase latency (161). On the other hand, the
fine-tuning methods aim to teach the model to incorporate external useful knowledge in an
effective way. For example, one can equip the LLMs with retrieval defect detection and utility
extraction via instruction fine-tuning (178; 175) or enable the LLMs to interact with the
retriever multiple turns until appending sufficient information for answer generation (8; 67).
Though effective, the existing approaches still inherit the original method of LLMs
to perform the online computation of key-value pairs in the attention networks of the
decoder (171) for generation, which means that the autoregressive decoding of LLMs is
mainly impacted by the attention score to produce generation probability. We notice that the
attention score is assigned by LLMs alone once the retrieved documents are appended into
the prompt template. The original relevance judged by the retriever of the input documents
is never used by the LLMs. Thus, the LLMs might treat the input documents as equally
relevant or slightly different according to their input position (79) based on the implicit
internal judgments. This gives rise to several critical questions: Should RAG ignore the
relevance signals of the retrieved documents in its generation? Are such relevance signals
useful for generation? How should the generation be impacted by document relevance?

128

Search for

Fig. 6.1. Comparison between the existing decoding LLMs that use their default probability
distribution and our proposed approach that modifies the distribution by leveraging external
explicit relevance signals.
We believe that document relevance should be explicitly considered in answer generation
in RAG, so that answer generation can be more tuned toward relevant information than
irrelevant one. To achieve this goal, in this paper, we propose OpenDecoder, a new approach
that directly leverages document relevance to change the information processing procedure of
LLMs decoding, namely, its attention mechanism. As shown in Fig. 6.1, compared to the
current decoding paradigm of LLMs, our proposed OpenDecoder does not only rely on the
attention score produced via the internal network and instruction-following training, but also
leverages explicit relevance signals as external indicator features. The model is expected to
become more robust to varying levels of noisy input context by reshaping the generation
probability distribution via the useful information among the retrieved knowledge, and thus
produce more accurate answers as output.
To implement OpenDecoder, the first step is to construct external indicators by extracting
quality features from the retrieved documents. We consider three types of signals: relevance
score from the retriever, LLM-judged semantic score, and query performance prediction
score. Then, we design a training framework to teach the LLMs to leverage these explicit
indicator features (either separately or in combination) for answer decoding. Specifically, we
incorporate the external features into the internal attention networks computation to directly
modulate the LLMs when producing generation probabilities for the decoding candidate
tokens. Additionally, to make the training and inference more robust to noisy information
within the input, we conduct robustness training by reconstructing the input top-k documents
via sampling additional documents with various relevant levels. During the online inference,
the corresponding indicator features from external information are processed by the trained
LLMs via the learned parameters in OpenDecoder. Experiments on five benchmark datasets
covering both general and multi-hop question answering (QA) demonstrate the effectiveness
and enhanced robustness of the proposed approach, which consistently outperforms the
129

vanilla RAG and other strong baselines across diverse noisy environments. Importantly, our
designed OpenDecoder is flexible to be integrated with the post-training of LLMs for any
purposes and incorporate any other type of external indicator features towards effectiveness,
robustness, or trustworthiness enhancement.

6.2. Related Work
6.2.1. Knowledge-Intensive Answer Generation
The knowledge-intensive tasks (52; 74; 35) require the systems to incorporate external
knowledge as a supplement, where Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) (88; 49) is a de
facto paradigm aiming to search useful knowledge for LLMs to generate a response. Earlier
RAG methods follow the “Retrieve-then-Read” framework (88; 60) by adopting a retriever to
search for relevant information from external resources based on the user’s query. To further
enhance RAG performance, subsequent studies focus on refining retrieval quality through
techniques such as query reformulation (108; 129), re-ranking (172; 218), and noise filtering
as intermediate steps (66), thereby improving the relevance of documents before they are
appended to LLMs’ input.
However, retrieval errors remain common due to limitations in search effectiveness and
corpus quality (144), which can ultimately degrade RAG performance. To address this
problem, robust RAG (104; 232) focuses on input optimization and knowledge integration.
For instance, Weller et al. (197) conduct query augmentation and introduce a novel confidence
method based on answer redundancy. RobustRAG (201) employs an isolate-then-aggregate
strategy to ensure the robustness of LLM responses against retrieval corruption attacks. By
generating self-synthesized rationales, InstructRAG (196) explicitly denoises the retrieved
content, thereby enhancing the robustness of RAG systems. AstuteRAG (186) turns to refine
and integrate knowledge derived from different sources to improve knowledge utilization
and enhance the robustness of the generated answer. RbFT (178) proposes a robust finetuning strategy against retrieval defects with two defined tasks, defect detection and utility
extraction, with associated instructions. In addition, recent studies on developing deep search
agents (67; 92; 167; 231) introduce a new paradigm for enhancing input quality by integrating
in-context reasoning with dynamic search tool invocation when needed. Although effective,
these existing methods rely only on the internal mechanism of LLMs to process information,
e.g., attention network (184). Unlike them, our method OpenDecoder is developed to enable
LLMs to distinguish useful information via both internal mechanisms and external explicit
indicators.
130

6.2.2. Decoding Optimization in LLMs
Prompting (102) the advanced LLMs is a simple and effective way to instruct them
to generate answers, where the answer decoding highly relies on the designed prompt and
internal attention mechanism. Existing literature optimizes the decoding procedure of LLMs
on various aspects. For efficiency, Performers (21) propose compressed attention, reducing
attention complexity from quadratic to linear. StreamingLLM (202) leverages attention sinks
to decrease Key-Value cache memory for long-context generation. For effectiveness, a series
of studies (13; 219; 174) investigate how to leverage inference scaling and deep reasoning for
RAG decoding. A recent study REFRAG (99) rethinks RAG-based decoding and proposes
an optimized architecture to compress only a small subset of retrieved documents that are
directly related to the query for effective and efficient decoding. For faithfulness, the existing
studies aim to detect and manage misinformation within retrieved documents (233), such as
explicitly identifying and resolving knowledge conflicts (226; 187; 31). These studies focus
on selecting relevant and reliable information for LLM input, which still operate in the way
that has been trained, by assuming the input information to be relevant. In contrast, in
our approach, we modify the attention mechanism according to the relevance of retrieved
information. Such an approach has not been proposed in the literature.

6.3. Our Methodology: OpenDecoder
The principle of our methodology OpenDecoder is to modify the decoding procedure of
LLMs with explicit relevance information as quality indicators, rather than solely based on
prompt design. The goal is to enable the model to be robust to noisy retrieved information
that can be irrelevant. In the following sections, we first formulate the problem and provide an
overview of our OpenDecoder as shown in Fig. 6.2. Then, we present the detailed design for
the components in OpenDecoder, including (1) constructing quality indicators via extracting
features from external information; (2) learning to leverage explicit indicators for decoding;
and (3) robustness training via replacing the input retrieved documents with various relevant
levels.

6.3.1. Task Formulation
A vanilla RAG system typically consists of an ad-hoc retriever Ret(·), a generator (i.e., the
LLM) G(·), and a corresponding corpus C with a large collection of documents. Given a user
query q, the retriever Ret(·) would identify its top-k relevant documents Ret(q) = {docqi }ki=1 .
Then, the LLM G(·) would generates an answer as response r based on the query and relevant

applying Robust Training, and finally obtaining the reshaped token probability distribution

(6.3.1)

The quality of the generated answer a highly depends on the useful information returned
by the retriever Ret(·) and the understanding capacity of LLMs for the input context with
the corresponding prompt. The inevitable noise in the retrieved context would significantly
degrade the answer quality of LLMs on top of it. These issues are unavoidable with the current
prompting-based approach, where the content decoding only inherits the internal information
processing mechanism of LLMs by following the prompt instruction (56; 191). Our work
focuses on guiding the decoding processing with explicit signals of external indicators of
usefulness beyond the scores produced by the internal attention network.

6.3.2. Constructing Indicators via Extracting Features from External Information
Our goal is to incorporate external explicit indicators for LLMs to utilize internal knowledge
stored in their parameters. Thus, the first step is to construct the indicators by extracting
quality features from the retrieved information. The most intuitive feature is the relevant
score computed by the retriever model in terms of the given query and candidate documents.
In general, the retrieved top-k relevant documents {docqi }ki=1 for the query q are associated

have additional impacts or have been integrated in model internal processing implicitly.
Besides, the QPP scores provide the indicators about the difficulty of the query, which might
imply the possible noisy level of the retrieved information for the generator.
Eventually, these scores calculated based on different aspects are used individually or as
a combination S agg by an aggregation function to guide the LLMs to process the external
information during generation, i.e., to decide to what extent it should focus on different parts
of the input context in decoding.

6.3.3. Learning to Leverage Explicit Indicators for Decoding
The fundamental problem in the current paradigm of RAG is that adding external retrieved
information in the input prompt could only affect the online computation of key-value pairs
in the attention networks of LLMs, which is not tailored to the input with noise. Since the
retrieved context is usually not perfect, the inherent defects are only implicitly processed
via the attention score computation, which is influenced by the mechanisms (e.g., predefined
system prompt) in the pre-training procedure. Thus, a better way is to inform the decoding
with additional explicit indicators directly, so that the LLMs know how much they should
rely on external or internal knowledge to generate an answer.
To this end, we aim to teach the model to leverage the explicit indicator features from
external information generated in Section 6.3.2, and integrate them into the original attention
networks computation. Following the procedure of the standard RAG, the user query q and
its corresponding retrieved top-k documents R(q) = {docqi }ki=1 would fill the prompt template
together with the instruction as [Instruction, docq1 , docq2 , · · · , docqk , query] to instruct the LLM
to produce an answer. To teach the LLMs to leverage explicit indicator features, we first
construct a score distribution by concatenating any types of score {si }ki=1 as features of the

6.3.4. Robustness Training
It may often be the case that some retrieved documents are not relevant. To make the
training and inference more robust to noisy information, we conduct robustness training by
replacing the second half of the top-k retrieved documents {doci }ki=1 with partial relevant
ones {docpart-rel } and irrelevant ones {docirrel } as Eq. 6.3.6. They are sampled from the top-k
set excluding the top-5 documents and the whole collection excluding the top-k documents,
respectively. The goal of constructing a noisy document list {doc}noisy is to provide a
necessary environment for the model to learn to distinguish the useful and noisy information.
A further alternative is to shuffle the position of the noisy document list as {doc}shuffle
noisy ,
aiming to emphasize the impact of external signals and reduce the common issue of position
bias (50; 212) of retrieved documents in RAG.
{doc}noisy = {doci }51 ∪ {docpart-rel } ∪ {docirrel },

where

{docpart-rel } ∼ {doci }ki=6 , {docirrel } ∼ (C − {doci }ki=1 )

(6.3.6)

Then, the reconstructed noisy retrieved documents {doc}noisy or {doc}shuffle
noisy with various
levels of noise and random relative position are used for robustness training by replacing the
original input documents list {doc}k1 in Eq. 7.4.3.

135

6.4. Experimental Setup
6.4.1. Datasets and Evaluation Metrics
We evaluate OpenDecoder on five benchmark datasets, including two categories: (1)
General Question Answering: NQ (82), TriviaQA (72), and PopQA (112), and (2)
Multi-Hop Question Answering: HotpotQA (208) and 2WikiMultiHopQA (57). These
datasets encompass a diverse range of retrieval with noise in RAG, enabling a comprehensive
evaluation in different settings. Statistical details about the used datasets are provided in
Appendix A.

6.4.2. Evaluation Settings in Noisy Environments
We evaluate our OpenDecoder and all compared baselines among three settings with
different noisy retrieval results. The first one is Normal Evaluation, where the input search
results for RAG are the original top-10 documents from the retriever. The second one is
Noisy Evaluation, where the search results for RAG are constructed in the same way as
the robust training in Section 6.3.4, i.e., replacing the second half of the top-10 retrieved
documents with partial relevant ones and irrelevant ones, which aims to evaluate whether the
RAG system can distinguish the noise and solely rely on the useful input information. The
third one is Extreme Noisy Evaluation, where the search results for RAG are obtained
by randomly sampling from the irrelevant document set, which simulates the extreme cases
when the retrieval fails among difficult queries or domains.

6.4.3. Baseline
To evaluate the effectiveness of OpenDecoder across various noisy settings, we compare
it against the following baselines: (1) Vanilla retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) (88)
approach with standard “retrieve-generation” pipeline; (2) Vanilla supervised fine-tuning
(SFT) (23); (3) RobustRAG (201): An isolate-then-aggregate strategy to filter out the noise
in retrieved context; (4) AstuteRAG (186): A retrieval-refined method to improve knowledge
utilization and enhance robustness; (5) InstructRAG (196): Instructing LLMs to denoise
retrieved content by generating self-synthesized explanatory rationales; (6) Robustness finetuning (RbFT) (178): A more recent approach to conduct robustness training with two
instruction fine-tuning tasks, defect detection and utility extraction.

136

6.4.4. Implementation Details
We implement OpenDecoder based on Qwen-2.5 series backbone models (206) with the
official open-source code repository. The compared baselines are also implemented with
the same Qwen-2.5-3B-Instruct model as our main experiments. For retrieval, we use the
2018 Wikipedia dump (76) as the knowledge source and E5 (188) as the retriever, with the
number of retrieved documents set to 10, following (201; 178). For the robustness training,
the number of relevant, partially relevant, and irrelevant documents is set to the same as the
noisy evaluation, as 5, 3, and 2, respectively. The partially relevant and irrelevant documents
are randomly sampled five times from corresponding document sets and fixed for all compared
methods for fair comparison. For training, we merge the training sets of NQ and HotpotQA
to form a unified training dataset for OpenDecoder and other fine-tuning-based baselines
following (67). The training epoch is set to 1 to ensure the model learns to use the explicit
guidance and generalizes to out-of-domain evaluation datasets without overfitting. Evaluation
is conducted on the test sets of five datasets to assess both in-domain and out-of-domain
performance. The F1 score and Exact Match (EM) are used as the evaluation metrics,
following (201; 178).

6.5. Experimental Results
6.5.1. Main Results
The overall performance of OpenDecoder is presented in Table 6.1. It is tested on five
datasets, with three evaluation settings of different noisy environments in terms of the input
retrieved documents. We can make the following observations:
(1) Our OpenDecoder consistently outperforms most compared baseline methods on three
evaluation settings and significantly surpasses the Vanilla SFT approach without external
indicators. Beyond the noisy and extremely noisy evaluation, the retrieved top-k documents
in the normal evaluation might still contain noise in the input for answer generation (We will
investigate the impact of noise Section 6.5.5). Thus, these results demonstrate the superior
effectiveness of our OpenDecoder in tolerating noise, which can be attributed to our designed
mechanism of modulating the decoding using external relevance signals as indicators and
enabling the LLMs to grasp such capacity via specific training.
(2) Compared to other approaches targeting robustness improvement (RobustRAG and
RbFT), our OpenDecoder exhibits more robust answer generation in noisy and extremely
noisy settings. This is mainly because the compared methods still follow the current approach
of internal information processing mechanism of the LLMs, which highly rely on the original

are more useful and necessary; while for the more difficult datasets, the retrieval defects are
more common, and thus the urgent goal is to improve the success rate of retrieving relevant
documents before aiming to enhance the robustness of the answer generation.

6.5.2. Ablation Study
The ablation studies are shown in Table 6.2. We can observe that by providing explicit
indicators, the LLMs can better process the input information compared to the Vanilla SFT,
which is the key idea of our method, and achieve the highest improvement. The feature
aggregation is effective in some datasets, while robust training can contribute more to stable
performance. A possible explanation is that the most effective features may vary depending
on the distribution of the dataset, necessitating a more adaptive feature selection mechanism
to enhance generalizability. Meanwhile, introducing noisy training inputs remains essential to
improve the model’s robustness and noise tolerance during inference. Nevertheless, combining
all these mechanisms for implementing OpenDecoder can obtain better results across three
different evaluation settings with various levels of noisy context on five datasets, which
indicates the effectiveness of each component.

139

Fig. 6.3. Performance of aggregating various scores as guidance features across different
evaluation settings and datasets.

Fig. 6.4. Performance of normalizing scores features with various approaches across different
evaluation settings and datasets.

6.5.3. Feature Aggregation and Normalization
In this section, we further investigate the impact of aggregating and normalizing various
scores for answer decoding as follows.
Aggregation. The results of score aggregation are depicted in Fig. 6.3. We can see that
aggregating any types of relevant scores can achieve better results compared to the Vanilla
SFT without explicit indicators. Leveraging the retrieval score S Ret alone could be sufficient
for the general QA datasets (NQ, TrivialQA, and popQA), where aggregating more features
might not always bring additional gain. This might be because when one indicator feature
is satisfied, adding the others might raise the risk of interference, as these features are
measured from different aspects. For the multi-hop QA datasets (HotpotQA and 2wiki),
aggregating more feature scores helps to achieve better performance, which implies that
complex questions desire more external indications to generate correct answers. In addition,
the improvement with aggregating LLM-based ranker score S Rank compared to vanilla SFT
demonstrates that the internal information processing of LLMs cannot implicitly ignore the
noise, which emphasizes the importance of impacting the decoding of LLMs with explicit
relevant indicators as our OpenDecoder.

denoted in Eq. 6.3.2. The other two normalization approaches, Min-Max and Exponentials −min({sj }kj=1 )

the former one considers the relative gap among the original scores and the latter one
further consider the impact of the rank position with exponential decay for each document
candidate. Our results indicate that the Max normalization approach outperforms MinMax normalization on general QA datasets; however, this trend reverses for multi-hop QA
tasks. Interestingly, the more complex Exponential normalization with rank decay leads to a
substantial performance degradation. These findings suggest that the choice of normalization
strategy is a critical hyperparameter that significantly dictates effectiveness. Specifically,
appropriate normalization can yield significant gains, while suboptimal choices can induce
performance drops, even within the fixed architecture of our OpenDecoder. Thus, a more
sophisticated approach could be further explored in future studies.

6.5.4. Document Order in Robust Training
In this section, we examine the effect of varying document position orders on robust
training. As mentioned in Section 6.3.3, the original input context order before applying
robust training is Input = [Instruction, docq1 , docq2 , · · · , docqk , q]. On top of it, we investigate
three types of reorder methods, including reversing the document position from docqk to docq1 ,
shuffling them, and further injecting noise with various relevant levels as Section 6.3.4. The
results are presented in Table 6.3. We observe that reversing the document order can obtain
better performance than the original one. This might be because the new reversed order
InputRev. = [Instruction, docqk , docqk−1 , · · · , docq1 , q] enables the higher top-k documents to be
much closer to the question and thus might raise their attention score by alleviating the
long-distance distraction. This phenomenon suggests that specifying document positions in
the prompt template as plain text may not be fully interpreted by LLMs. Consequently,

141

Fig. 6.5. The performance between the Vanilla SFT approach and our OpenDecoder using
various top-k retrieved documents in the normal evaluation setting.
shuffling input documents during training can mitigate position bias, as the top-1 document
is not always more informative than the top-2 for answer generation. Moreover, injecting
noise further enhances model robustness by encouraging it to assess the true relevance of
input documents based on external indicators, rather than relying on positional cues.

6.5.5. Noise Tolerance of Input Top-K
As the evidence for the correct answer might relate to only a small portion of the relevant
documents, the normal evaluation using the original top-k retrieved results would still
inevitably contain irrelevant information. We evaluate the noise tolerance ability of Vanilla
SFT and our proposed OpenDecoder in terms of the impact of various input top-k values. The
results are shown in Fig. 6.5. As the number of input documents increases, the probability of
identifying relevant documents with answer information and the degree of injecting potential
noise both increase. In most of the datasets, the larger top-k cannot guarantee higher
performance except on TrivialQA, which indicates that the accurate search results are
crucial for answer generation. Overall, our OpenDecoder exhibits better performance than
Vanilla SFT in different numbers of input documents, which demonstrates the effectiveness
of leveraging relevance score to impact decoding across various input top-k.

6.5.6. Investigation of Scaling Model Size
We further investigate the impact of scaling up model size for vanilla SFT and our
OpenDecoder. The results in the noisy evaluation setting are depicted in Fig. 6.6. Overall,
both the SFT and our proposed approaches benefit from larger model sizes, suggesting
that larger models are more capable of tolerating contextual noise, which aligns with prior
studies (75). Moreover, the effectiveness of leveraging explicit indicators to influence answer
generation becomes more pronounced with larger models, whereas smaller models (e.g.,
1.5B) do not consistently achieve better performance across all datasets. These observations
indicate that effectively integrating external signals with internal LLM reasoning processes is

142

Fig. 6.6. Comparison between SFT and our OpenDecoder of scaling model size across five
datasets in the noisy evaluation setting.

Fig. 6.7. Comparison between SFT and our OpenDecoder of scaling model size across five
datasets in the normal evaluation setting.
a non-trivial task that demands higher model capacity. A similar trend is observed when
aggregating multiple guidance score features, implying that this aggregation process also
requires implicit learning during training. Therefore, designing more sophisticated learning
objectives to better incorporate this aggregation mechanism and employing larger backbone
models for training OpenDecoder could further enhance performance, which we leave for
future work.
The results in the normal evaluation and extreme noisy setting are depicted in Fig. 6.7
and Fig. 6.8, respectively. Overall, similar trends are observed in the noisy evaluation
setting, where larger models are more capable of tolerating contextual noise. Besides, the
improvement in scaling model size is more pronounced in complex QA datasets than in
general ones, indicating that a larger model may be equipped with a more powerful reasoning
ability.

6.5.7. Discussion on Time and Space Efficiency
The computation cost of our method is the same for the offline training and online
inference. The computation complexity of the Vanilla SFT method and our OpenDecoder is
O(|d|2 h + |d|h2 ) in the RAG setting, where d is the average number of tokens in a document
doc, and h is the hidden dimension size of the decoder-only LLMs. This is because the
explicit guidance, i.e., the relevance scores, are produced simultaneously with the retrieved
143

Fig. 6.8. Comparison between SFT and our OpenDecoder of scaling model size across five
datasets in the extreme noisy evaluation setting.
documents, and the normalization of the scores should be negligible. In terms of the storage
overhead, the normalized score Snorm ∈ Rh×h is stored as a token-level metric, whose shape is
the same as the Query, Key, and Value metric in the attention computational network inside
the LLMs. Thus, the additional storage overhead compared to Vanilla SFT is O(nh), where
n is the number of Transformer layers with the impact of explicit guidance.

6.6. Conclusion
In this Chapter, we focus on developing a training-based approach to guide the LLMs to
incorporate document quality in the retrieval-augmented generation task. We propose a new
paradigm to modulate the LLMs’ internal information processing mechanisms with explicit
indicators to improve robustness in answer decoding when the input context contains various
noise. To achieve the goal, we proposed OpenDecoder framework, which constructs various
explicit quality indicators via extracting features from the retrieved document and applies
them to modify the attention score computation among the networks of LLMs. Additionally, a
robustness enhancement mechanism is integrated into the training procedure to enable LLMs
to handle various noisy environments. Our experiments demonstrate that incorporating
explicit indicators from retrieved information in RAG tasks enhances the LLMs’ ability
to tolerate noise in the input context and leads to better performance compared to prior
approaches. Importantly, this paradigm is flexible to be integrated with the post-training of
LLMs for any purposes and incorporated with any type of external indicators.
Although our OpenDecoder is designed based on single-turn queries as initial exploration,
it is flexible to be extended to multi-turn scenarios by incorporating the retrieved documents
for each turn during Human-AI interactions. Following the same principle, the additional
indicators could be designed for the historical turns, which are determined to be incorporated
into the input of the current turn.

144

Chapter 7

Optimizing Conversational Information
Retrieval and Response Generation via
Unified Frameworks

7.1. Introduction
So far, we have developed different effective methodologies for either the information
retrieval task or the retrieval-augmented generation task for conversational scenarios in
preceding Chapters. The next crucial question is to investigate the possibility of jointly
optimizing both tasks for the whole conversational search system via unified frameworks.
Existing conversational search systems are usually composed of two different models: a
retriever and a generator (46; 128), which are trained and inferred separately. The retriever
aims to identify the relevant passages by understanding conversational queries, while the
generator crafts the final response for the information-seeking goal. The deployment of
separate models in the whole pipeline induces the problems in two folds: i) The separation
restricts the system from leveraging the model’s intrinsic knowledge simultaneously, which
raises the risk of lacking correlation with the performance of both tasks, leading to inconsistent
results, i.e., the effectiveness of retrieval might not always benefit response generation (162);
and ii) Deploying and maintaining two distinct models adds extra hardware requirements and
increases maintenance expenses (224). An intuitive solution is to design a unified framework
to jointly optimize the conversational information task and the response generation task.
One alternative is to develop a unified model that acts as a retriever and generator in
conversational scenarios. Another idea is to optimize both components in an end-to-end
manner, so the trained models are expected to mutually improve retrieval and generation
performance through seamless integration.

Table 7.1. The functionality comparison between ours and existing systems, including
whether support conversational scenarios, first-stage retrieval, and response generation.
System
