Existing studies have demonstrated the feasibility of developing LLM-based unified models
in conversational question answering, involving open-domain retrieval1 and response generation. However, these systems can only address two aspects of understanding conversational
context, managing retrieval independently, or generating responses, as illustrated in Table 7.1.
Among them, RepLLaMA (109) and E5 (189) successfully implement generative LLMs for
retrieval tasks, and ChatRetriever (113) further adapt it to conversational scenarios. However,
the fine-tuning for retrieval objectives leads to the collapse of generation ability in these
systems. The RankRAG (218) and ChatQA (105) enable the system to exploit a more
accurate input context for the generator to produce better responses to user queries. However,
they should rely on an additional retriever to address retrieval needs, and the optimization is
not performed in an end-to-end manner. To develop a unified model capable of handling both
retrieval and generation tasks, GRIT (136) attempts to train an LLM with distinguished
instructions, but it is not designed for conversational applications.
To address the limitations of previous studies, we propose UniConv, a unified LLM-based
model to investigate the feasibility of handling both retrieval and response generation in
conversation. To achieve this, we inherit the training data selected by ChatRetriever (113) to
adapt LLM to serve as a powerful conversational dense retriever. To improve the response
generation ability while fine-tuning dense retrieval, we design a context identification instruction mechanism as part of the learning objective. This mechanism is designed to seamlessly
integrate retrieved information into the response generation process. Additionally, we identify
a discrepancy in previous training data: the same data format is applied to different learning
1In this chapter, the retrieval denotes retrieving information from a large external collection as an

open-domain setting, rather than only identifying specific pieces from the initial search results with limited
top-k candidates similar to ranking.

146

objectives, which does not align well with the distinct output requirements of retrieval and
generation tasks. To mitigate this issue, we include additional well-formatted conversational
search data for model fine-tuning.
In addition to developing unified models, recent developments in deep search agentic
models (67; 231; 167) achieve remarkable performance on complex information-seeking tasks
by interleaving multi-round reasoning and external search. The flexible architecture allows
it to dynamically explore external knowledge sources and iteratively refine its reasoning
trajectory for open-domain question answering (228; 235). Although these advanced deep
search agentic models are designed for single-turn information accessing tasks, it is capable of
facilitating the multi-turn conversational scenarios from static “rewrite, retrieve, and generate”
workflows into an end-to-end dynamic fashion. Under user-system multiple interactions,
the user’s queries are context-dependent and involve topic-switch (116; 131; 214). Thus,
it is crucial to adapt the agentic models into conversational scenarios to learn to leverage
useful historical context to reply to the current turn via reasoning ability. One important
aspect is to generate a stand-alone search query condition on comprehending the historical
context (61; 127), which is different from the existing single-turn deep search agent that
decomposes a self-contained query. Another crucial factor lies in optimizing the model to
effectively leverage information obtained from search results through de-contextualization,
enabling accurate answer generation that integrates retrieved knowledge with the model’s
inherent parametric knowledge (186; 171), while the existing studies on deep search lack
search results optimization (67).
To address the aforementioned issues, we propose UniCSA, a unified framework via
end-to-end training to optimize the conversational search agent models from multiple aspects
with contextualized reasoning, including history-conditional search query generation, and the
utilization of search results for accurate answer generation. To achieve it, we decompose the
overall reward into three complementary components under a reinforcement learning (RL)
training framework (164) to interleave search and reasoning across turns, enabling exploratory
and adaptive behaviors learned through tailored design. Specifically, to help the agentic
model optimize search queries through de-contextualization reasoning in conversations and
learn to extract useful information from retrieved results for answer generation, we introduce
a search optimization reward. This reward measures the information overlap between the
retrieved content and the ground-truth answers, providing additional supervision beyond the
ground-truth answer signal alone.
We conduct extensive evaluations on several widely used conversational search datasets,
where UniConv and UniCSA demonstrate strong generalization capabilities for representing
complex conversational sessions in dense retrieval, along with robust generation abilities for

147

crafting responses. Moreover, our approaches achieve better seamless consistency between
information retrieval and retrieval-augmented response generation in terms of effectiveness
and reliability compared to non-unified optimization frameworks.

7.2. Related Work
7.2.1. Interactive Systems in Generative AI Era
Conversational mode has become a common practice when users interact with AI systems (130). Such systems are powered by LLMs, which can follow user instructions (143).
To help users seek information, LLM-based interactive systems are usually equipped with a
search engine as an external tool for assisting response generation (106). Then, the conversational systems can synthesize an answer from the top-retrieved information into a single
response (122; 156; 19; 91; 124). Different from single-turn retrieval-augmented generation
(RAG) (88; 8; 118; 223), which only needs to consider the given stand-alone query with its
associated retrieved results for response generation, a conversational response generator (213)
requires modeling conversational turn dependency and understanding the context-dependent
query and search results. The main challenge lies in modeling the interaction between LLMs,
users, and the search engine (192).

7.2.2. LLM-based Dense Retrieval
To explore the potential of LLMs in dense retrieval tasks, some existing studies (189; 109)
attempt to follow the observed scaling law (75) in search model (138) by replacing the
backbone model from the small ones (e.g., BERT-base (32) and T5-base (155)) into the
generative LLMs (e.g., Mistral (64) and LLaMa (177)). They keep the training paradigm
similar to DPR (76) using relevance judgments as supervision signals while changing the
representation of queries and passages different from the ones in encoder-based models.

7.2.3. Unified Models for Retrieval and Generation
The motivation to develop unified LLMs for retrieval and generation is to attempt to
mutually leverage the intrinsic knowledge from both sides to improve the model’s general
multi-task ability and reduce cost. To this end, Mueenighoff et al. (136) propose GRIT, to
train LLMs to handle both generative and retrieval tasks by distinguishing between them
through instructions, and Li et al. (93) design a unified framework based on generative
retrieval and open-domain question answering. Then, Yu et al. (218) propose RankRAG,
which unifies the re-ranking and generation through simultaneously instructing the LLMs on
context ranking and answer generation. However, they cannot address multi-turn scenarios
148

due to a lack of conversational adaptation. In a conversational setting, Mao et al. (113)
and Liu et al. (105) fine-tune LLMs specifically for conversational retrieval and response
generation tasks, respectively, but these adaptations do not preserve the model’s ability to
perform both functions concurrently. Recently, a parallel study, OneGen (224), proposes
unifying the traditionally separate training approaches for generation and retrieval by incorporating retrieval tokens generated in an autoregressive manner. However, it cannot follow
conversational context and independently handle retrieval tasks.

7.2.4. Agentic Search with End-to-End Unified Optimization
Recent studies (228) focus on developing agentic LLMs for information accessing tasks via
RL training with end-to-end optimization. The principle is to decompose the complex questions via integrating in-context reasoning with dynamic search tool invocation when needed.
Representative studies such as Search-r1 (67), R1-Searcher (167), and DeepResearch (231) optimize RL policies for generating multiple queries through multi-turn search interactions and
integrating retrieved evidence, thereby achieving strong performance on knowledge-intensive
QA tasks. However, they are limited to handling self-contained questions as one-shot querying
and do not require understanding historical context. Building on these advancements, we
adapt this framework for agentic conversational search. Specifically, we define an intermediate
reward tailored for multi-turn dialogue that explicitly optimizes the agent’s ability to leverage
search results. This mechanism encourages the model to generate more effective rewritten
queries, thereby improving the capture of user intent at each conversational turn.

7.3. An LLM-based Unified Model for Conversational
IR and Response Generation
7.3.1. Task Formulation
The task is to establish a unified model that can handle both conversational retrieval and
response generation. Formally, given a conversational session that contains n − 1 historical
turns Hn = {qi ,ri }n−1
i=1 and current query qn , the unified model M is expected to act as
a retriever to identify the relevant passages Pn from a large collection C and also act
as a generator to produce a response rn on top of external knowledge Pn to satisfy the
information needs in qn . Thus, the unified model M is required to handle the multi-turn
session query to retrieve the relevant passages as Pn = M(qn ,Hn ), and generate the response
as rn = M(qn ,Hn ,Pn ). In our setting, the unified model M is a generative LLM with a
decoder-only architecture.

149

Fig. 7.1. The performance of different systems to fine-tune language models for conversational
retrieval and response generation with various settings.

7.3.2. Feasibility of Unifying Conversational Information Retrieval
and Response Generation
We first examine the feasibility of unifying conversational information retrieval and
response generation to verify whether jointly fine-tuning can maintain the model’s generation
ability while grasping retrieval capacity. The results for both tasks on two datasets are shown
in Fig. 7.1, which includes four different systems: i) fine-tuning SLM for retrieval and using
the original LLM as the generator; ii) fine-tuning LLM for retrieval only and iii) for response
generation only; iv) fine-tuning LLM for both tasks.
By comparing systems ii), iii), and iv), we observe that only fine-tuning a single part on
the backbone LLM for retrieval or response generation hurts another ability. However, jointly
fine-tuning the model with the objective functions for both tasks can obtain a unified model.
Besides, the LLM-based retriever performs better than the SLM-based one, indicating the
potential for conversational information retrieval performance with an LLM.
Then we investigate the RAG setting by incorporating the corresponding retrieved passages
for the response generation, except applying the search results from system ii) to system iii),
since these two systems cannot handle both tasks. We can see the improvement from RAG is
higher in system iv) with a unified model compared with system iii) with a separated one.
These results confirm the feasibility of developing a unified model for conversational search,
aligning with our motivation for developing the UniConv Model.

7.3.3. Generative Language Models for Conversational Search
Following the verified feasibility, we design our proposed UniConv, whose overview is
illustrated in Fig. 7.2, which consists of three parts, including various learning objectives
toward conversational information retrieval, conversational response generation, and context
identification instruction. We describe each component as follows.
Conversational Information Retrieval. The common practice for dense retrieval finetuning is the paradigm of DPR (76), which leverages a bi-directional encoder-only model to
encode the queries and passages separately on top of a bi-encoder architecture. Then, the
first sequence token “[CLS]” is employed as the text representation for similarity calculation.
When the backbone model, e.g., an LLM(·), turns into generative ones with uni-directional
decoder-only architecture, e.g., LLaMA, the adaptation is to form the representation Vseq
using an appended end-of-sequence token “</s>” to both the queries and passages (109) as
Vseq = LLM(·)[−1].
To adapt the conversational scenario, the input query for each turn n is reformulated as
qn′ = qn ◦ Hn by concatenating the context of the previous turn. Then it is vectorized with
candidate passages pn by the model M and calculate their similarity S (qn′ , pn ) =< Vqn′ , Vp >
via dot product. With the established representation, contrastive learning with InfoNCE loss
is used for end-to-end conversational dense retrievers optimization as

7.3.4. Context Identification Instruction
During the inference phase with the retrieval-augmented setting, the model input is
usually the query together with the retrieved evidence serving as the main part of the
instruction, where the model is expected to generate the response grounding on the useful
piece of the retrieved evidence. Since the relevant passage and ground-truth response used
as supervision signals are separately conducted during the training phase within the unified
model, potential inconsistency risk might occur (218). To this end, we design a context
identification instruction to help the model implicitly identify the useful passage during
the fine-tuning, which is consistent with the input instruction format of inference. This is
achieved by combining the query with the positive passage in the same sequence and using
different responses as contrastive samples as

7.3.5. Data Discrepancy Mitigation
To equip the LLMs with conversational dense retrieval capability, (113) leverage the ad-hoc
search data with relevant query-passage pairs and instructional conversation with multiturn query-response pairs to enable the model to obtain retrieval and conversational context
understanding ability. In practice, their implementation utilizes each turn’s response rn+ in the
conversation dataset as the relevant passage p+
n and each ad-hoc query’s corresponding relevant
+
passage p+
n as the ground-truth rn for retrieval and generation fine-tuning, respectively.

However, a unified model should have different outputs for conversational retrieval (e.g., ranklist) and generation (e.g., synthesized response), whose requirement is not exactly matched
with the fine-tuned data form in existing studies and thus might lead to sub-optimal results.
A more practical way is to ensure each data sample includes both the relevant passage p+
n and
the corresponding ground-truth response rn+ as supervision signals for the given query turn
qn . Then, the model can learn the consistency from the various targets between retrieval and
152

generation. Thus, we include the conversational search data (2) to meet this requirement to
mitigate the data discrepancy issue. Another alternative is to construct synthetic data (105)
with well-formed signals, which is not the focus of our paper.

7.3.6. Training and Inference
For the training phase, we integrate the conversational dense retrieval, retrieval-augmented
response generation, and the context identification instruction tuning to form the training
objective LUniConv of our unified model as Eq. 7.3.1, where α is a hyper-parameter to control
the fine-tuning effect. For the inference phase, we use the same fine-tuned model to perform
retrieval to produce a top-k rank list and generation to produce a response within zero-shot
and RAG settings under conversational scenarios.
LUniConv = LR + LG + αLCII

(7.3.1)

7.4. An End-to-End Agentic Optimization Framework
for Conversational IR and Response Generation
7.4.1. Task Formulation
A conversational agent should be capable of performing various actions through reasoning
in conversation to arrive at the final answer. Formally, given a dataset of user–system
conversations, each composed of multiple turns, and a collection of passages C. At each
turn n, the agent system receives the conversation history Hn = {qi ,ri }n−1
i=1 and the current
user query qn . The conversational agent requires generating a response rn to query qn ,
leveraging context from Hn and grounding in external information C. In some cases, the agent
should also be capable of handling unanswerable or ambiguous queries through rejection and
clarification, resulting in requiring mixed-initiative action capabilities. We aim to develop
such an agentic model with contextualized reasoning via end-to-end optimization in this
study.

7.4.2. Rewards Modeling
Reinforcement learning (RL) plays a crucial role in training agentic models by enabling
self-exploration of diverse reasoning trajectories and rewarding those that are effective. This
aligns model behavior with the objectives of information seeking for users’ context-dependent
intents and efficient decision-making for actions in conversations. However, since these
trajectories are self-generated and do not always yield correct final answers due to noisy
conversational context, relying solely on outcome-based rewards yields sparse supervision

GRPO Optimization is guided by full trajectories sampled from the policy LLM, which
consists of reasoning, search, and answer steps, with rewards assigned to each signal. The
policy model interleaves the same action set while interacting with a search environment
within the Interaction Loop for both training and inference.
signals, making the training process harder to optimize (17). To this end, we decompose the
overall reward into three complementary components: outcome reward, search optimization
reward, and mixed-initiative action reward. These rewards guide the model to reason more
strategically with the conversational context towards the final answer, effectively capture
user intent for search optimization, and respond precisely to various types of queries with
mixed-initiative needs in conversations. We describe the overview of our framework in Fig. 7.3.
Outcome Generation Reward. The outcome generation reward directly reflects the final
task success. At the end of each rollout, the predicted answer response rn is extracted to
evaluate by comparing with the ground-truth answer response rn∗ using a task-specific metric
Sans as Sans (rn , rn∗ ), e.g., Exact Match or F1 score. The formulation of the outcome generation
reward is
Routcome = Sans (rn , rn∗ )

154

(7.4.1)

Information Gain Reward. Solely relying on the supervision signal from the ground-truth
answer is not enough to enable the conversational agent to interpret user intent and establish
high-quality intermediate results for external search. Different from previous studies (67; 167),
which only generate search queries for calling the search interface, the agentic model requires
optimizing the search query via de-contextualization in conversations and learning to utilize
the gained information via external retrieval, i.e., the useful parts of the search results in
terms of approaching the final answer. To this end, the information gain reward is designed
to measures the information overlap between the retrieved information and the ground-truth
rn∗ via applying a specific metric SInfo on them as SInfo ({Pn }k1 , rn∗ ). The {Pn }k1 is the top-k
relevant passages obtained by an ad-hoc retriever Ret via the generated rewritten-query qn′
for the n-th turn as {Pn }k1 = Ret(qn′ ).
Such an optimization is expected to improve the quality of the agent-rewritten search
query by aligning its search results with the associated final answer without requiring the
relevance judgments between query and gold passages as supervision signals. The information
gain reward RIG is formulated as the cumulative coverage of the search results in each retrieval
calling iteration. According to the length of the ground-truth answer for each query (11),
we use either F1-score or Accuracy as an evaluation metric to validate the information gain
reward from long or short types of answers, which is defined as

to maximize the aggregated reward R(τ ) given the history H, the user query q at the last
turn n, and the search engine Ret, while minimizing the distance between the optimized and
original policies as Eq. 7.4.3.

We adopt the efficient Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm (164),

results.

7.5. Experiments
7.5.1. Experimental Setup
Evaluation Datasets and Metric. We conduct the main evaluation on four widely-used
conversational search datasets, including TopiOCQA (2), QReCC (6), OR-QuAC (149), and
INSCIT (200). Each of them contains the gold standard for passage retrieval and response
generation. In addition, we utilize FaithDial (38), a benchmark for information-seeking
dialogue, alongside the TopiOCQA dataset to assess the reliability of generated content.
Specifically, we evaluate the UniConv model’s ability to ground its responses in the provided
evidence or rationales. We use NDCG@3 and F1 to evaluate the information retrieval and
response generation performance to conduct a fair comparison with baselines.
Training data. For our UniConv model, we use the ad-hoc search dataset MSMARCO (9),
the The Question About the World subset of the instructional conversation dataset UltraChat (34), and the whole conversational search dataset TopiOCQA for unified model
fine-tuning. For our UniCSA model, the TopiOCQA and INSCIT datasets are used as the
training set, and evaluated on all datasets with in-domain and out-of-domain settings.
Baselines. We compare our methods with various conversational information retrieval
and response generation baselines. For the retrieval phase, we compete with the most
effective conversational dense retrieval (CDR) systems based on small language models
(SLMs), including ConvDR (216), Conv-ANCE (119), and QRACDR (132) and most recently
LLM-based approaches, including RepLLaMA (109), E5 (189), (Conv-)GRIT (136), and
ChatRetriever (113). The GRIT is the only system that can handle both retrieval and
generation tasks, and its variant Conv-GRIT is fine-tuned for conversation in the same setting
as ours. Besides, the compared baselines also contain the methods based on conversational
query reformulation (CQR) on top of LLMs and then deliver the query into an ad-hoc

156

retriever, including LLM4CS (114), RETPO (214), and the agentic model Search-R1 as a
main competitor for our UniCSA model.
In the response generation phase, we conduct the comparison under zero-shot and RAG
settings. The UniConv is applicable for both settings, while the UniCSA is only evaluated on
the RAG setting. For the zero-shot manner, we include GRIT and its variants Conv-GRIT
and three powerful LLMs: Mistral, Claude (7), and ChatGPT (140). For the RAG setting, to
make the results comparable, we employ Mistral-2-7B-chat as the generator with two typical
conversational dense retrievers, Conv-ANCE and Chatretriever, and keep the Conv-GRIT on
the same workflow as our UniConv, i.e., using the same model for both tasks. The Search-R1
is still used as a main competitor for our UniCSA model in an agentic fashion.
Implementation Details. We initialize UniConv with Mistral-2-7B-chat, which can also
be applied on top of any generative models. We train it on eight 40G A100 GPUs using
LoRA (58) with a maximum input sequence length of 1024 for query and 384 for passages and
responses. The training process involves one epoch with a learning rate of 1e-4, a gradient
accumulation of 4 steps, a batch size of 32, and in-batch negatives per sample. The α for loss
balance is set to 0.5. During the inference stage, we deploy Faiss (69) for the dense retrieval,
set the maximum length as 128, and use top-10 retrieved passages for the response generation.
For the UniCSA model, we initialize it with the same backbone LLM as the generator and
use the same retriever model as Search-R1 to ensure they are comparable.

7.5.2. Results of Conversational Information Retrieval
Table 7.2 shows the conversational information retrieval results on four datasets and the
comparison with existing systems, where we have the following observations:
(1) Our proposed UniConv model outperforms the baseline methods on most of the
datasets, including the previous unified model (Conv-GRIT), the state-of-the-art conversational dense retriever (QRACDR and ChatRetriever), and conversational query reformulation
methods (LLM4CS and RETPO), which demonstrates that the superior dense retrieval ability
of our developed system by arousing the LLM capacity with specific fine-tuning.
(2) Our proposed UniCSA model also performs well than most CQR approaches. This is
attributed to the search optimization rewards for generating rewritten queries with supervision
signals via contextualized reasoning. Note that our UniCSA does not use any relevance
judgments between query and passages as supervision signals for optimization. Nevertheless,
it still outperforms some CDR systems (ConvDR and Conv-ANCE). These gains indicate that
our conversational agent implicitly identifies the contextual relevance better via contextualized
reasoning and emphasize the necessity of optimization toward adaptive user behaviors and
search quality.

(3) The state-of-the-art CDR systems, either SLM-based (QRACDR) or LLM-based
(ChatRetriever and UniConv), consistently perform better than the LLM-based CQR systems,
which indicates the end-to-end optimization can achieve better retrieval performance compared
with the rewrite-then-retrieval paradigm (40).
(4) The LLM-based retrievers (RepLLaMA, E5) do not always behave much more powerfully than SLM-based ones for conversational retrieval, although they are considered to have
strong foundational multi-turn context understanding capacity. This might be attributed to
the possible reason that they are fine-tuned solely on templated instructions, which fail to
handle complex and diverse conversational information-seeking scenarios via fully leveraging
the generalization capabilities of LLMs. Thus, it is still necessary and important to conduct
conversational dense retrieval fine-tuning when employing LLM as a backbone model.

158

7.5.3. Results of Conversational Response Generation
Table 7.3 shows the results of conversational response generation on four datasets with
two different settings and the comparison among existing systems.
Performance of UniConv. In the zero-shot scenario, our UniConv does not perform as
well as the current state-of-the-art LLM. This suggests that joint fine-tuning to enhance
retrieval capabilities may negatively impact direct response generation performance based on
parametric knowledge, due to modifications to the model parameters. In the RAG setting,
where responses are generated based on retrieved passages, we observe that our UniConv
outperforms the compared systems with separate retrievers and generators. This indicates
that the unified model may better leverage intrinsic consistency and shared knowledge,
mutually enhancing the performance of both retrieval and generation.
Performance of UniCSA. Our UniCSA approach consistently achieves the best performance
across nearly all datasets. Such observations demonstrate the importance of optimizing the
model in an end-to-end framework to learn to interact with the search engine and utilize the
useful parts of search results in conversations via our designed reward signals. Among the
agentic systems, the Search-R1 does not perform well on the datasets with long-form answers
due to the lack of intermediate rewards compared to our UniCSA.

7.5.4. Ablation Studies
We conduct ablation studies for conversational information retrieval and response generation to study the effects of our proposed two mechanisms in UniConv, a context identification
instruction (CII) mechanism to improve consistency when leveraging the retrieved information
for response generation within the same model, and a data discrepancy mitigation (DDM)
mechanism to induce well-formatted training data with desirable supervision signals. Then,
we investigate the effectiveness of various reward signals and optimization algorithms during
RL training for our UniCSA. The results are reported in Table 7.4 and Table 7.5, respectively.
Conversational Information Retrieval. In terms of UniConv, Table 7.4 shows an
interesting phenomenon that incorporating the CII mechanism would hurt its retrieval
performance, while it is helpful for the response generation as shown in Table 7.5. This might
be because the changed input query form as qn′ ◦ p+
n inevitably influences the contextualized
embedding obtained via the learning objective of retrieval LR , leading to the possible
confusion within the model due to the training is conducted simultaneously. A potential
solution is to perform fine-tuning for conversational retrieval LR and context identification
instruction (CII) LCII separately into a two-stage process, which can be explored in future
studies. Furthermore, removing the data discrepancy mitigation (DDM) mechanism leads to
performance degradation, indicating that utilizing well-structured conversational search data

7.5.5. Impact of Generated Response Reliability in Unified Models
In this section, we investigate whether the unified model can produce a more accurate and
reliable response than the system with the separated models. We use the variants of UniConv
without adding the LCII term in Eq. 7.3.1 as the generator and employ ChatRetriever as the
retriever for the RAG setting within the separated system while deploying the full UniConv
as the unified system. We evaluate both systems on the TopiOCQA and FaithDial datasets,
measuring similarity using F1 and BERT scores to assess the accuracy of the generated
response r′ compared to the ground-truth response r. Faithfulness is evaluated by comparing
the generated responses against the evidence or rationale E provided by the datasets. Since
FaithDial does not include a retrieval collection, we utilize the same database as TopiOCQA
for this purpose.
The results presented in Table 7.6, show that the unified system consistently enhances
the accuracy of generated responses across both datasets in two settings. For faithfulness,
the RAG setting further improves the unified system’s performance, whereas a performance
drop is observed for the separated system in TopiOCQA. These observations suggest that
developing the system as a unified model can improve reliability to a certain extent.

7.5.6. Impact of Conversational Context in Different Models
We examine the impact of conversational context (multi-turn conversations) on information
retrieval and response generation tasks for systems with unified and separated models,
following the setting of the last section. The evaluation is based on per-turn performance,
with the implementation for both systems consistent with the setup described in Section 7.5.5.
As shown in Fig. 7.4, the unified model consistently outperforms the separated model on both
tasks as the conversation progresses, except for the retrieval task on QReCC. This observation
162

Fig. 7.4. The performance of unified (Uni.) and separated (Sep.) models on retrieval
(NDCG@3) and response generation (F1) with different conversation turns.

Fig. 7.5. Correlation between search quality and answer generation performance on two
main competitors and our variants of w/. or w/o information gain reward (RIG ).
highlights the unified model’s (UniConv) more robust ability to understand conversations and
maintain better consistency between retrieved results and its augmented generation, even in
longer conversations.

163

7.5.7. Correlation between Search Quality and Answer Generation
Performance via Reinforcement Learning
The search results evaluated by traditional IR metrics (e.g., NDCG@3) might not always
reflect the effectiveness for answer generation. To this end, we investigate the correlation
between search quality and answer generation performance via RL training within our UniCSA
model, where the search quality is measured by the information gain during the retrieval
procedure in terms of approaching the final answer as described in Section 7.4.2.
We depict the results in Fig. 7.5. We observe that even with higher information gain via
search results, the Search-R1 still cannot perform better in answer generation than AgenticLM
and our approach with optimized capacity to utilize the retrieved knowledge from external
resources. In other words, without specific optimization for leveraging search results, the
agent models would still tend to answer the question using parametric knowledge. In contrast,
our method can better integrate externally retrieved information with original knowledge via
our designed information gain reward.

7.6. Conclusion and Future Work
In this Chapter, we aim to jointly optimize the conversational information retrieval task
and the retrieval-augmented response generation task via unified frameworks. We first present
UniConv, an LLM-based unified model capable of handling both dense retrieval and response
generation in complex conversational scenarios. We propose two mechanisms to seamlessly
integrate retrieved information into response generation and address data discrepancy issues
during joint fine-tuning. Then, following the development of agentic systems, we propose
UniCSA, a unified framework via end-to-end training to optimize the conversational search
agent models. The UniCSA interleave search and reasoning across turns in conversations and
is equipped with contextualized reasoning capability, including history-conditional search
query generation, and the utilization of search results for accurate answer generation via
reinforcement learning. Experimental results on different conversational search datasets
demonstrate the superior performance of UniConv and UniCSA, which demonstrate the
effectiveness of unified framework for conversational search systems.
For future studies, developing a unified system for a broader range of complex conversational search scenarios is valuable, including product search, item recommendation, proactive
retrieval, etc. Besides, it is important to continue improving the consistency between retrieval
and generation and conduct tailored training based on large-scale synthetic data. In addition,
the additional computational cost is added for both training and inference, when trained on

164

large models or with reinforcement learning optimization. Thus, exploring the possibility of
developing joint optimization in a more efficient way is desirable.

165

Chapter 8

General Conclusion and Future Studies

Conversational search engines enable multi-turn interactions between users and systems
to satisfy their information needs, which provides more intelligent and interactive user
experiences compared to traditional one-shot querying in ad-hoc search systems. Although
effective, different challenges remain to be further addressed to improve the performance of
conversational search systems.
In this thesis, we focus on two core tasks of conversational search: (1) retrieving relevant
information for context-dependent user queries in conversations, and (2) utilizing retrieved
information to facilitate user response generation. The goal for the first task is to improve
the retrieval accuracy, where we investigate this problem from the aspect of user search intent
understanding. To comprehend the user queries in conversational scenarios, we design two
approaches, (i) one is to conduct conversational query reformulation to convert the contextdependent queries into stand-alone ones; and (ii) another one is to develop conversational
dense retrievers with the capacity to be aware of the relevance among historical turns and
the current query. The target for the second task is to improve the quality of the generated
response for the users while utilizing the retrieved information from the first task. To achieve
this goal, we explore three approaches: (i) trying to leverage the historical information, e.g.,
historical retrieval results, historical turn dependency, and historical grounding evidence, to
serve as supplementary to enhance the quality of the current turn response generation; (ii)
optimizing the decoding procedure of generator models by incorporating the quality of the
retrieved information via sophisticated computational mechanism and training algorithm;
and (iii) developing unified model and framework to connect the optimization of both tasks
and improve them via jointly end-to-end training.
We conduct a series of experiments on multiple conversational search and question
answering benchmarks to evaluate all our proposed approaches and compare to existing
baselines. The experimental results demonstrate that our systems can comprehend user

search intents in conversational scenarios much better, with significant improvements in the
performance of conversational information retrieval. With the retrieved information, our
systems also demonstrate their capacity in utilizing the useful search results and the tolerance
of noisy information for final response generation.
In terms of future studies, we can further improve the conversational search systems from
the following aspects:
• More information could be leveraged to better understand user intent during usersystem interactions, including different formats of user implicit feedback, such as
clicks, touches, and body gestures. In the future, the interactive interface might not
just based on natural language, but would involve multimodal input and output.
Besides, the multi-party collaborative interaction would increase the complexity of
the interactive scenarios, e.g., the interactions between a set of humans and a set of
domain-specific AI systems. Thus, the systems should have the capacity to process
more complex information.
• Our study enhances the effectiveness and robustness of the information system by
identifying useful retrieved information for the improvement of noise tolerance. Further
efforts can focus on developing a new paradigm of information retrieval, which can
identify the reliability and trustworthiness of the retrieved information and incorporate
it into conversational search systems. This is crucial, especially when the web might
be full of AI-generated content in the future with the development of generative AI.
• Another important aspect is model training algorithms. The machine learning algorithms should be applied to both retrieval and generation tasks with consistency. For
example, more effective supervision signals or intermediate rewards in RL training
could be designed to ensure the usefulness of the retrieval is helpful for the final generation. Besides, enabling the systems to learn from the daily increased interactions data
would be a desirable way to continue improving the performance of conversational
systems, which require new learning paradigms to achieve it.

193

Appendix A

Datasets

We illustrate the statistical information and description of the used datasets in different
Chapters. In Chapter 3 and Chapter 4, we use TopiOCQA (2), QReCC (6), TREC CAsT19 (27), CAsT-20 (28), and CAsT-21 (29) datasets for conversational information retrieval
tasks. In Chapter 5, we use TopiOCQA, QReCC, and OR-QuAC (149) datasets for the
first investigation on the conversational retrieval-augmented response generation task. In
Chapter 6, we use single-turn open-domain QA datasets NaturalQuestion (82), TrivialQA (72),
PopQA (112), HotpotQA (208), 2WikiMultihopQA (57) to evaluate our OpenDecoder framework for language model decoding optimization. In Chapter 7, we use TopiOCQA, QReCC,
OR-QUAC, and INSCIT (200) to evaluate both the conversational information retrieval
task and conversational RAG task in terms of our approaches. The statistics and associated
description of each dataset are depicted as follows.
• TopiOCQA is a relatively new published dataset featured with topic switching. As
the conversation goes on, the topics may switch to related topics, a phenomenon
commonly observed in information-seeking search sessions. Therefore, TopiOCQA is
more challenging for identifying useful historical information and requires the ability
of accurate context modeling more, since the previous turns may not be directly
related to the current turn.
• QReCC is conducted specifically for conversational open-domain question answering.
The questions were sourced from the QuAC (20), NQ (82), and CAsT-19 datasets (27).
The annotators were required to give answers using a web search engine.
• TREC CAsT datasets are curated by the human experts of TREC Conversational
Assistance Track (CAsT). Each CAsT dataset has dozens of information-seeking
conversations comprising hundreds of turns. CAsT-19 and CAsT-20 share the same
retrieval corpora while CAsT-21 has a different one. In contrast, CAsT-20 and CAsT21 have a more complex session structure than CAsT-19 as their questions may refer

Table A.2. Statistics of the five used datasets in Chapter 5 for question answering.

to previous responses. All three datasets provide human rewrites and passage-level
(or document-level) relevance judgments labeled by TREC experts.
• OR-QuAC is a much earlier dataset transformed from the conversational machine
reading comprehensive dataset QuAC (20). The questions in a conversation are
sourced from the same section in Wikipedia, and the answers are extractive, i.e., exact
text spans in passages.
• INSCIT is a mixed-initiative information-seeking dialogue dataset with humangenerated interactions, including different system reactions.
• NaturalQuestion (NQ) is a factoid dataset whose questions consist of real
anonymized, aggregated queries issued to the Google search engine.
• TrivialQA is a reading comprehension dataset whose question-answer pairs authored
by trivia enthusiasts and independently gathered evidence documents that provide
high quality distant supervision for answering the questions.
• PopQA assesses factual question answering, challenging the model’s ability to recall
accurate knowledge and resolve ambiguity in entity representation.
• HotpotQA focuses on evaluating multi-hop reasoning skills, requiring models to
combine information from different contexts to address a single query.
• 2WikiMultihopQA (2wiki) is a dataset designed to test the model’s ability to
perform multi-hop reasoning by integrating information across multiple Wikipedia
passages.

196

We also provide an example to illustrate what information is included in a data sample,
which contains a query, its relevant passage, and the corresponding ground-truth response.

Turn 1: Initial Utterance
User Query: “What do dark chocolate health benefits include?”
Gold Passage: “Clinical studies indicate that dark chocolate rich in cocoa solids contains
high concentrations of flavonoids. These compounds act as potent antioxidants, which
significantly improve endothelial function, reduce blood pressure, and mitigate the risk
factors associated with cardiovascular diseases.”
System Response: “Dark chocolate is rich in flavonoids, which act as antioxidants.
These compounds have been shown to lower blood pressure, improve endothelial
function, and significantly reduce cardiovascular risks.”

Turn 2: Follow-up Utterance (Context-Dependent & Elliptical)
User Query: “Does it also improve cognitive functions?”
Gold Passage:: “Recent neuroimaging research demonstrates that the acute ingestion
of cocoa flavanols enhances cerebral blood flow to gray matter. Regular intake has been
correlated with improved performance on cognitive tasks involving memory, executive
function, and attention processing in young adults.”
System Response: “Yes, research suggests that the flavanols in dark chocolate enhance
cerebral blood flow. Regular consumption is linked to better performance in cognitive
areas such as memory, attention processing, and executive function.”

Turn 3: Ambiguous Utterance
User Query: “What about the sit and reach test?”
Gold Passage:: “The sit and reach test is a common field assessment used by researchers
and practitioners to measure the flexibility of the lower back and hamstring muscles.
It is a core component of health-related physical fitness batteries but does not measure
upper body flexibility.”
System Response: “The sit and reach test measures lower back and hamstring flexibility as a part of physical fitness. To better assist you, are you looking to contrast
the cognitive benefits of chocolate with physical exercise, or are you seeking the
instructions and scoring data for this specific fitness test?”

Turn 4: Resolved Ambiguity
User Query: “Just tell me how to do the test and what the standard scores are.”
Gold Passage: “To perform the sit and reach test, remove shoes and sit facing the box
with knees fully extended and feet flat against the device. Reach forward slowly as far
197

as possible with hands parallel. For adults, reaching past the toes (beyond 0 cm or 15
inches depending on the scale baseline) generally indicates average to above-average
flexibility.”
System Response: “To perform the test: sit with legs fully extended and feet flat
against the testing box. Slowly reach forward with parallel hands as far as possible.
Reaching past your toes indicates average to above-average flexibility. I can pull up a
complete demographic scoring chart if you would like.”

Appendix B

Prompts

B.1. Prompts for LLM-Enhanced Query Reformulation
We provide the used prompts in our CHIQ approach in Chapter 3 for LLM-enhanced
query reformulation, including producing enhanced historical context via the parametric
knowledge of LLMs and generating pseudo rewritten queries as supervision signals for rewriter
model fine-tuning.
Question Disambiguation (QD)
[System Prompt]: You are given a set of question-answer pairs and a new question that is
ambiguous. Your goal is to rewrite the question so it becomes clear. Write the new question
without any introduction.
[User Prompt]:
Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 }
Current Question: {qi }.

Response Expansion (RE)
[System Prompt]: Given a series of question-and-answer pairs, along with a new
question, your task is to give a one-sentence response to the new question.
[User Prompt]:
Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 }
Current Question: {qi }.

Pseudo Response (PR)
[System Prompt]: You are given a question-and-answer pair, where the answer is not clear.
Your goal is to write a long version of the answer based on its given context. The generated
answer should be one sentence only and less than 20 words.
[User Prompt]:
Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 }
Current Question: {qi }.

200

Topic Switch (TS)
[System Prompt]: Given a series of question-and-answer pairs, along with a new question, your
task is to determine whether the new question continues the discussion on an existing topic or
introduces a new topic. Please respond with either “new_topic” or “old_topic” as appropriate.
[User Prompt]:
Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 }
Current Question: {qi }.

History Summary (HS)
[System Prompt]: You are given a context in the form of question-answer pairs. Your goal is
to write a paragraph that summarizes the information in the context. The summary should
be short with one sentence for each question answer pair.
[User Prompt]:
Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 }
Current Question: {qi }.

201

Generating Pseudo Rewritten Queries as Supervision Signals
[System Prompt]: You are given a relevant passage, a series of question-and-answer pairs as
context along with a new question, your task is to generate a set of search queries based on
the relevancy between the new question and the relevant passage and also rely on the given
context. The output format should be in a list with indexes e.g., 1. 2. 3.
[User Prompt]:
Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 }
Current Question: {qi }.

B.2. Prompts for Leveraging Historical Information to
Boost Conversational RAG
In this section, we list the prompts that we have carefully designed for different proposed
strategies in leveraging historical information to boost conversational RAG, including search
results integration, historical turns dependency judgment, historical evidence identification,
and their hybrid usage in Chapter 5.
Search Results Integration
[System Prompt]: You are an intelligent ranker. For an information-seeking dialog, please
rank the retrieved passages from each utterance according to the utility to help you answer
the current question.
[User Prompt]: For an information-seeking dialogue, I will provide you with {top-k * (turn 1)} historical retrieved passages with their associated queries and {top-k} passages for current
query, each indicated by number identifier []. Rank the passages based on their helpfulness to
answer the current query: {qi }

202

Historical Turns Dependency Judgment
[System Prompt]: You are an expert evaluator. For an information-seeking dialog, given
the Current Question, please select all the Historical Questions that contain similar information needs. The output should be a list that only contains the index of selected questions.
[User Prompt]: Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 } Historical Question 2: {q2 }.
Response 2: {r2 }
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 } Current Question: {qi }.
Now, you should give me the selected Historical Questions. The output format should be a
list that only contains the index of selected questions, e.g., [1, 3, 5]. Never ask for clarification
or say you don’t understand the selection. Go ahead!

Historical Evidence Identification
[System Prompt]: This is a chat between a user and an artificial intelligent assistant. The
assistant gives helpful, detailed, and polite answers to the user’s questions based on the
retrieved evidence from historical turns and current turn. The assistant should also indicate
when the answer cannot be found in the context and then answer based on its own knowledge.
[User Prompt]: Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Response 1: {r1 }. Evidence id: [pid_1, pid_2,
..., pid_k]. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Response 2: {r2 }. Evidence id: [pid_1, pid_2, ..., pid_k].
Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Response n-1: {rn−1 } Evidence id: [pid_1, pid_2, ...,
pid_k]. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k].
Current Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Current Question: {qi }. Please give a complete
answer to the question. Cite the evidences that supports your answer within brackets []. The
output format should be:
Answer:
Citation: []
Current Question: {qi }.
Never ask for clarification, say you don’t understand or explain the reason. Go ahead!

203

Conversational Retrieval-Augmented Response Generation
[System Prompt]: You are an intelligent generator. For an information-seeking dialog, please
help generate the answer that can fully address the user’s information needs based on the
historical conversation and retrieved evidence.
[User Prompt]: Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Response
1: {r1 }.
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Response 2: {r2 }.
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Response n-1:
{rn−1 }.
Current Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Current Question: {qi }.
Now, you should give me the answer of the **Current Question** under the conversation
and **Evidences**. The output format should always be:
Answer:
Note that you should always try to answer it concisely. Never ask for clarification, say you
don’t understand or explain the reason. Go ahead!

Conversational Retrieval-Augmented Response Generation with Hybrid Strategies
[System Prompt]: You are an intelligent generator. For an information-seeking dialog, please
help generate the answer that can fully address the user’s information needs based on the
historical conversation, the relevant historical retrieved evidence, and the turn dependency.
[User Prompt]: Historical Question 1: {q1 }. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Response
1: {r1 }.
Historical Question 2: {q2 }. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Response 2: {r2 }.
...
Historical Question n-1: {qn−1 }. Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. Response n-1: {rn−1 }.
# Note: Only contain the turn determined by DEI strategy.
Current Evidences: [psg_1, psg_2, ..., psg_k]. # Note: The evidence is produced by SRI
strategy. Current Question: {qi }.
Now, you should give me the answer of the **Current Question** under the conversation
and **Evidences**. Please also cite the evidence that supports your answer within brackets
[]. The output format should always be:
Answer: “”. Citation: []
Note that you should always try to answer it concisely. Never ask for clarification, say you
don’t understand or explain the reason. Go ahead!

Appendix C

Concrete Case for Analysis

C.1. Case of Qualitative Analysis for History-Aware Conversational Dense Retrieval
We provide the corresponding case in Table C.1 for the qualitative analysis in terms of
HAConvDR approach with the Tsne visualization in Sec. 4.5.6, which illustrate how historical
ground-truth passage benefit the current query retrieval.

Conversation (id: 4-13)
q1 : who sang all i want for christmas in 1995? (irrelevant) — p1 : All I Want for Christmas Is You
is a Christmas song... (536, -, -)
q2 : who is she? (relevant) — p2 : Mariah Carey (born March 27, 1969 or 1970) is an American
singer-songwriter... (5, 20, 17)
q3 : what was her early days like? (irrelevant) — p3 : Mariah Carey was born in Huntington, New
York... (614, -, -)
q4 : what are some famous songs she performed during 2010? (relevant) — p4 : It missed out on
the top one-hundred in the UK... (-, -, -)
q5 : who composed the former mentioned one? (irrelevant) — p5 : Cox plated the keyboard and
percussion. The background vocals... (-, -, -)
q6 : how did it perform in the charts? (relevant) — p6 : In the US, Oh Santa! became a recordbreaking entry... (-, -, -)
q7 : how was it received critically? (relevant) — p7 : Mike Diver of the BBC wrote that Oh Santa!
is a “boisterous”... (-, -, -)
q8 : what was her other song about? (irrelevant) — p8 : Auld Lang Syne (The New Year’s Anthem)
is a re-write... (-, -, -)
q9 : how was it received critically? (relevant) — p9 : Auld Lang Syne garnered a negative response
from critics... (937, -, 322)
q10 : what are some philanthropic activities? (relevant) — p10 : Carey is a philanthropist involved
with several... (197, 502, 31)
q11 : what does the foundation do? (relevant) — p11 : The Make-A-Wish Foundation is a 501(c)(3)
nonprofit... (-, -, -)
q12 : what is her style of music? (relevant) — p12 : Love is the subject of the majority of Carey’s
lyrics... (6, 68, 29)
q13 : what are some awards she has received?
Gold Passage (Target) (Rank: 107, 68, 2)
Throughout her career, Carey has earned numerous awards and honors, including the World
Music Awards’, Best Selling Female Artist of the Millennium, the Grammy Award for Best New
Artist in 1991, and “Billboard”s Special Achievement Award for the Artist of the Decade during
the 1990s. In a career spanning over 20 years, Carey has sold over 200 million records worldwide,
making her one of the best-selling music artists of all time. Carey is ranked as the best-selling
female artist of the Nielsen SoundScan era, with over 52 million copies sold. Carey was ranked
first in MTV and “Blender” magazine’s 2003 countdown of the 22 Greatest Voices in Music, and
was placed second in “Cove” magazine’s list of “The 100 Outstanding Pop Vocalists.” Aside from
her voice, she has become known for her songwriting.

Table C.1. Qualitative example of historical ground-truth passage benefiting current query
retrieval. Brackets after queries indicate relevance; brackets after passages indicate rank
positions by ANCE, Conv-ANCE, and our HAConvDR.
